**Summary.** This paper tackles the challenge of reconstructing true quantum states from digitized measurement data by defining and calculating the 'Robinet state'. The authors introduce a novel mathematical technique based on unitary dilation that yields an exactly completely positive evolution map, allowing for highly accurate simulation of time-averaged quantum trajectories.

**Why it may be interesting.** This is highly relevant for theoretical physicists working on open quantum systems or quantum optics, as it provides a rigorous, computationally tractable method to handle the non-Markovian effects inherent in real-world continuous quantum measurements.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The core problem is accurately reconstructing quantum states from continuous measurement records that are inherently digitized, leading to information loss due to finite time binning ($\Delta t$).

**Main result.** The authors developed a novel numerical discretization scheme for the 'Robinet state' reconstruction map that is provably completely positive and accurate to arbitrarily high orders in $\Delta t$ without needing external solvers.

**Method.** The method involves constructing an explicit, completely positive evolution map by employing a unitary dilation of the stochastic master equation into an augmented system plus transmission line setup.

**Model / system.** The model describes a finite-dimensional quantum system undergoing open evolution governed by a Hamiltonian $H$ and measured via an operator $L$. The dynamics are analyzed using the Stochastic Master Equation (SME) framework.

**Key observables.** The Robinet state ($ar{ho}_n$), which is the time-averaged density matrix conditioned on the discrete measurement record $\{I_k\}$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The time bin size $\Delta t$, and the measurement efficiency $\eta$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The derivation relies on extending the stochastic master equation via a unitary dilation, which is systematic but involves truncating infinite series for practical computation.

**Figures summary.** No explicit figure summaries are available; the notes indicate extensive mathematical derivations and tabulated coefficients related to time-step expansions.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from defining the information loss problem (Robinet state) to introducing the unitary dilation formalism. It then details deriving the completely positive map $K_{In}$ through this dilation, verifying its accuracy on challenging examples, and finally providing physical insights by comparing results with prior work.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Truly continuous quantum trajectories, obtained from homodyne or heterodyne readouts, can only ever be reconstructed approximately. The continuous measurement signal, needed for exact reconstruction, is averaged over bins of finite time $Δt$ during any analog to digital conversion step. The best reconstruction possible, knowing only this discrete record, was introduced recently and dubbed the Robinet state. In this article, we show how the Robinet state can be computed with a numerical discretization scheme that is completely positive, accurate to arbitrarily high order in $Δt$, and that does not rely on any other external solver. Our derivation relies on a dilation of the stochastic master equation into a system + transmission line setup, constructed in such a way that measuring what we call the "zero mode" of the line yields the Robinet state. We test the method on a challenging example with random Hamiltonian and jump operator, and verify its accuracy up to order $10$. Apart from its numerical interest, our approach provides a wealth of physical insights, extending in particular recent results on purity obtained by Wonglakhon, Chantasri, and Wiseman, that would be difficult to obtain in any other way.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper analyzes how coherent spatial disorder and dephasing affect the computational hardness of quantum random sampling on a lattice architecture. Using advanced tensor-network simulations, the authors demonstrate that increasing disorder causes predictable crossovers in statistical properties, leading to a transition from classically hard (anticoncentrated) to easily simulable regimes. This provides crucial theoretical benchmarks for assessing near-term quantum hardware performance.

**Why it may be interesting.** The work provides quantitative error-budget bounds for near-term quantum devices by mapping physical noise (disorder/dephasing) onto measurable computational complexity transitions using established tensor network diagnostics.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates how coherent spatial disorder affects the computational complexity and practical simulability of Quantum Random Sampling (QRS) using IQP circuits.

**Main result.** Increasing disorder drives two crossovers: one where the output distribution loses anticoncentration, and another where the tensor-network simulation cost drops from exponential to polynomial in the large system limit.

**Method.** The analysis employs exact tensor-network simulations (PEPS/PEPO) combined with finite-size scaling techniques applied to statistical measures like collision probability and quantum fidelity metrics.

**Model / system.** The model is a square-lattice Instantaneous Quantum Polynomial-Time (IQP) architecture subject to two-qubit gate-angle disorder ($\sigma$) and single-qubit dephasing noise ($\epsilon$).

**Key observables.** Rescaled collision probability ($	ilde{Z}$), Total Variation Distance ($\delta_{TVD}$), Kullback–Leibler divergence ($\delta_{KL}$), and maximum entanglement ($S_{\max}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Disorder strength ($\sigma$), dephasing rate ($\epsilon$), system size ($n$), and critical crossover points ($\sigma_Z, \sigma_S$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on tensor-network representations (PEPS/PEPO) for simulating the quantum state evolution under disorder and noise.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the IQP circuit structure and PEPS representation; subsequent figures show how $	ilde{Z}$, $S_{\max}$, and various statistical distances collapse or change behavior as a function of increasing disorder ($\sigma$) or dephasing ($\epsilon$).

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the model (IQP on a lattice) and introduces diagnostic tools like $	ilde{Z}$ and entanglement measures. It then analyzes the effect of coherent disorder ($\sigma$), identifying crossovers in statistical properties and simulation cost. Finally, it incorporates dephasing noise ($\epsilon$) and performs scaling analyses comparing both sources of decoherence.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

While decoherence is known to erode classical hardness in quantum random sampling, the impact of coherent spatial disorder remains an open question. We study a square-lattice instantaneous quantum polynomial-time (IQP) architecture subject to two-qubit gate-angle disorder and single-qubit dephasing using exact tensor-network simulations up to 576 qubits. For finite systems without dephasing, increasing disorder drives two consecutive crossovers toward classical simulability: the output distribution first loses anticoncentration, and then the tensor-network simulation cost drops from exponential to polynomial as entanglement is suppressed. The finite-size scaling collapses are consistent with continuous transitions in the large-system limit. Dephasing further reduces the complexity. We characterize the computationally hard regime through scaling laws that provide quantitative error-budget bounds for realistic near-term devices.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper introduces a 'route-truncate-repair' procedure to compress the memory requirements of quantum adaptive agents. By mapping these agents onto Matrix Product States (MPS) and systematically truncating the bond dimension, it achieves significant reduction in physical memory size. This compression is rigorously certified by a fidelity-divergence rate, establishing a practical link between theoretical entropic advantages and engineering feasibility.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a rigorous, mathematically grounded method for compressing complex quantum dynamics derived from memory effects, which is highly relevant for simulating large-scale open quantum systems or modeling information processing in quantum devices.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The core problem is converting the theoretical advantage of quantum memory (entropic efficiency) into a practical, physically realizable reduction in the physical dimension required for an adaptive quantum agent.

**Main result.** A novel 'route-truncate-repair' procedure successfully maps complex quantum agents to compressed Matrix Product States while providing quantifiable fidelity guarantees that preserve underlying behavior.

**Method.** The method involves routing the agent dynamics through a reference input process to obtain an MPS representation, followed by local truncation of the memory bond and subsequent repair of the Kraus operators to ensure physical validity.

**Model / system.** The system models quantum adaptive agents performing sequential input-output processes. The dynamics are analyzed using tensor network representations (MPS) derived from the agent's internal memory state conditioned on external stimuli.

**Key observables.** Fidelity-divergence certificate ($R(Q)_F$), canonical bond state eigenvalues, and the reduced memory dimension ($	ilde{d}_q$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The reference input process $R$, which dictates the behavior prioritized during compression; the target fidelity threshold $\delta_\star$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The processes are assumed stationary (time-invariant), and the procedure relies on local repair steps to maintain trace preservation after dimension reduction.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the transition from complex history structures to a routed MPS, benchmark compression results comparing memory costs ($C_\mu$ vs $C_q$), and plots showing fidelity divergence rates versus retained dimensions for specific benchmarks like cyclic walks.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops the theory by first establishing the MPS representation (Theorem 1), then detailing the necessary repair procedure to ensure physical validity (Theorem 2), and finally applying this framework to benchmark processes to quantify dimension reduction while bounding fidelity loss.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Adaptive agents realise complex reactive behaviours by using a memory of past input stimuli and output actions to guide structured future responses. Quantum adaptive agents can operate while storing less information in memory than optimal classical counterparts; yet, this does not necessarily translate into a reduced dimension of the memory that must be physically realised. We introduce a route-truncate-repair procedure that converts entropic quantum memory advantages into reductions in memory dimension. Routing a reference input process through an agent yields a temporal matrix product state representation whose canonical bond is identified with the agent's memory. Truncating this bond and locally repairing the resulting dynamics produces a smaller, physically-valid agent that remains capable of responding to arbitrary input sequences. A fidelity-divergence certificate quantifies the resulting trade-off between accuracy and memory dimension. Benchmark adaptive processes exhibit substantial dimension reduction whilst preserving the underlying behaviour with high fidelity. These results establish a route from entropic memory advantages to practical, dimension-reduced adaptive quantum agents.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper analyzes the long-term behavior of a random process modeled after an open dynamical system. It rigorously determines the existence and structure of quasi-stationary distributions (QSDs) and quasi-ergodic distributions (QEDs). The key finding is that stable, normalizable QEDs only exist when the underlying Markov chain is in a specific R-recurrent regime.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides rigorous mathematical tools (R-recurrence/transience analysis) to study non-equilibrium steady states (QSDs/QEDs) in open systems, which is highly relevant for modeling dissipation or particle loss in complex physical models beyond standard equilibrium statistical mechanics.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates quasi-stationary and quasi-ergodic distributions for a substochastic Markov chain derived from an open variant of the Pelikan random map.

**Main result.** The system exhibits infinitely many QSDs for generic parameters, and a normalizable QED only exists in the R-recurrent regime; otherwise, theoretical calculations diverge or fail to match simulations.

**Method.** Analysis involves solving eigenvalue problems related to the transfer operator ($M\mu^* = \lambda\mu^*$) and using generating functions (Vere-Jones formalism) to determine recurrence/transience properties.

**Model / system.** The model is a biased random walk on the half-line, described by a substochastic discrete-time Markov chain with specific transition probabilities incorporating sub-stochastic resetting at state 0.

**Key observables.** Quasi-stationary distributions (QSDs), Quasi-ergodic distributions (QEDs), and the associated escape rate ($\gamma_{esc} = -\ln \lambda$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The bias parameter $p$, the resetting strength $\epsilon$, and the system's dynamical regime (R-recurrent vs. R-transient).

**Assumptions / limitations.** It is assumed that the principal escape rate eigenvalue ($\lambda_u$) yields the only normalizable QED, and the analysis relies on formalisms for countable absorbing Markov chains.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the open Pelikan map and its associated Markov chain. Figure 2 maps out R-recurrence/R-transience regimes based on parameters $(p, \epsilon)$. Figure 9 compares theoretical QED calculations against numerical simulations, confirming agreement only in the R-recurrent region.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from establishing the general framework for QSDs in open chains to analyzing recurrence properties using generating functions. It then derives explicit functional forms for the QED in different regimes, culminating in a comparison between theory and numerical simulation to delineate the boundaries of stable dynamics.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

In this paper we present a concrete example of a substochastic discrete-time Markov chain on a countable state space producing a spectrum of infinitely many quasi-stationary distributions (QSDs) for generic parameter values, with each QSD supporting a distinct escape rate. Our system is motivated by an open variant of the Pelikan dynamical system, a random map introduced in the 1980s. These QSDs, and their stability to perturbative random noise, are tested in numerical simulations. The existence of unique QEDs is also established for some parameter values.

</details>

**Summary.** This work develops a general estimation theory for continuous-variable quantum systems to determine stable state reconstruction from limited measurements. It proves that informational completeness is insufficient, establishing the 'measurement frame' condition as necessary and sufficient for stable reconstruction. The framework unifies tomography methods by using sigma-regularization to handle infinite-dimensional measurement data.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a rigorous mathematical framework unifying continuous-variable tomography, quasiprobability representations, and estimation theory by addressing fundamental limitations of standard quantum information tools in infinite dimensions.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To develop a general estimation theory for continuous-variable quantum systems that determines when and how quantum state properties can be stably reconstructed from finite measurement data.

**Main result.** Stable reconstructibility requires the POVM effects to form a 'measurement frame,' which is a stricter condition than mere informational completeness in infinite dimensions.

**Method.** The theory utilizes measurement frames within a sigma-regularized operator geometry, incorporating prior information via a reference state $\sigma$.

**Model / system.** Continuous-variable quantum systems are analyzed using Positive Operator Valued Measures (POVMs), generalizing concepts from finite dimensions to continuous outcomes like those from homodyne detection.

**Key observables.** Quantum states ($ho$), expectation values of observables ($O$), and quasiprobability distributions (e.g., Glauber-Sudarshan P representation).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The reference state $\sigma$, the range of the POVM synthesis operator, and the lower frame bound.

**Assumptions / limitations.** Standard finite-dimensional frame theory breaks down in infinite dimensions; the framework must account for observables whose reconstruction map is unbounded.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the sigma-regularized reconstruction problem, showing how the POVM defines an analysis operator $T_\sigma$ and a synthesis operator $T^*_\sigma$, with the range $R_\sigma$ defining $\sigma$-admissible observables.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes that informational completeness is necessary but insufficient for stable reconstruction. It then develops the measurement frame condition, characterizing reconstructibility via the analysis/synthesis operators in a sigma-regularized geometry, leading to practical estimator construction methods.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We show that informational completeness, while sufficient to have a bijection between ideal measurement probabilities and quantum states, does not guarantee statistically stable reconstruction from finite measurement data. To address this problem, we develop a general estimation theory for continuous-variable systems in which stable reconstructibility is characterized by the POVM effects forming a measurement frame. Informational completeness is therefore necessary, but not sufficient, for stable reconstruction. Our framework is based on measurement frames in a $σ$-regularized operator geometry, where the reference state $σ$ encodes prior information about relevant features of the measured states. For any fixed measurement scheme, observables may be inaccessible, weakly reconstructible only through estimators with divergent variance, or stably reconstructible by finite-variance unbiased estimators. The relevant regime is determined by the range of the POVM synthesis operator. Our framework provides practical methods for constructing estimators and gives an operational interpretation of singular quasiprobability distributions, including the Glauber-Sudarshan $P$ representation: quasiprobabilities act as unbiased estimators for associated observables, and their singularities reflect a pathological feature of the corresponding measurement: its lack of loewr frame bound. We furthermore show how this formalism naturally provides operational regularization procedures tied to prior information. Overall, our framework provides a unified view of continuous-variable tomography, quasiprobability representations, and classical-shadow estimation.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper establishes a quantitative complementarity relation between predictability and quantum coherence in multi-path interferometry. It defines these quantities using advanced metrics like the Bures distance and proves that this duality has an operational meaning: coherence quantifies the portion of measurement uncertainty irreducible by any classical side information. This provides a tight bound relevant for quantum random number generation.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a fundamental resource theory perspective on wave-particle duality, linking geometric measures (Bures distance) directly to information-theoretic concepts like classical randomness and quantum source security.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The work investigates a complementarity relation between predictability (particle-like behavior) and quantum coherence (wave-like behavior) in multi-path interferometry.

**Main result.** A trade-off relation, $eC + eP \le 1$, is established for mixed states, which endows the coherence measure with an operational interpretation as the classically irreducible part of measurement randomness.

**Method.** The analysis defines predictability using the Bures distance on dephased states and derives a complementary bound involving this metric and a convex-roof construction applied to coherence measures.

**Model / system.** The theoretical framework is built around multi-path interferometry setups, analyzing quantum states ($ho$) undergoing measurement in a specified basis $\{\Pi_a\}$.

**Key observables.** Predictability ($P$), Quantum Coherence ($C$), Bures distance ($D_B$), and the classical-label conditional min-entropy.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The dimensionality of the system ($d$) and the specific measurement basis $\{\Pi_a\}$ are key parameters.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on defining coherence as a monotone satisfying properties like convexity, faithfulness, and monotonicity under decoherence.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate plots showing normalized coherence ($\epsilon_C$) versus polar angle for qubits, and geometric interpretations relating coherence to the Bures distance between dephased states.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds from defining predictability via Bures distance in multi-path setups, deriving a complementarity relation for pure states as an equality, extending this to mixed states using convex roofs, proving mathematical properties of the coherence measure, and finally establishing operational bounds related to quantum randomness.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

The complementarity principle is a cornerstone of quantum mechanics. In this work, we investigate a complementarity relation between the predictability and quantum coherence, respectively, representing the particle-like and wave-like behaviour in wave--particle duality, in a multi-path setup. We introduce a basis-dependent predictability defined by the Bures distance between the state dephased in the chosen basis and the maximally mixed state. The predictability depends only on the observed basis statistics and admits a closed form in terms of the Bhattacharyya overlap. We derive a trade-off relation between the predictability and a coherence measure defined based on the nonclassicality of the Kirkwood--Dirac quasiprobability. For pure states, the trade-off relation is an exact equality. Remarkably, this wave--particle duality relation endows the coherence measure with an operational interpretation as the classically irreducible part of measurement randomness, yielding a tight worst-case bound on the guessing probability in source-independent QRNG.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces an exact variational theory to describe the non-equilibrium dynamics of interacting many-body systems undergoing Brownian motion. It minimizes a generalized free power functional, which systematically decomposes into entropic and energetic contributions. The resulting coupled equations provide a complete description of how density and pair correlations evolve away from equilibrium.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a rigorous, generalized framework for non-equilibrium statistical mechanics in complex fluids, extending standard DFT/power methods by incorporating explicit entropy superpower terms relevant for far-from-equilibrium dynamics.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Developing a formally exact variational scheme for the overdamped Brownian dynamics of pairwise interacting many-body systems in general spatiotemporal nonequilibrium.

**Main result.** The full dynamics are determined by two coupled, formally exact functional equations derived from minimizing an extended joint free power functional with respect to one-body and pair current distributions.

**Method.** The theory uses an extended joint free power minimization principle, splitting the free power functional into entropic and energetic rates, and employing functional differentiation to derive force balance relationships.

**Model / system.** The system is a many-body ensemble undergoing overdamped Brownian dynamics with pairwise interactions, governed by the Smoluchowski equation. Key variables include one-body density ($ho$), pair distance histogram ($G$), and their corresponding currents ($J$, $JG$).

**Key observables.** One-body current density ($J(\mathbf{r}, t)$), global distance current distribution function ($JG(r, t)$), and the universal entropy superpower functional ($\Sigma_{	ext{exc}}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Temperature (implied via $k_B T$), interaction potential $\phi(r)$, and the system's non-equilibrium state.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The method is presented as a formally exact variational scheme, though future work suggests approximations like 'superpower approximations'.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the problem by defining the dynamics via the Smoluchowski equation and then develops the theory using the joint free power minimization principle. It systematically derives force fields from functional derivatives, leading to two coupled equations of motion that close the dynamical description.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We present a formally exact variational scheme for the overdamped Brownian dynamics of pairwise interacting many-body systems in general spatiotemporal nonequilibrium. A joint free power minimization principle determines instantaneously the one-body current and the global interparticle distance flux. The intrinsic free power functional splits into entropic and energetic rates, where the latter are treated explicitly. The adiabatic contribution to the entropy rate is the time derivative of the equilibrium entropy metadensity functional. Genuine nonequilibrium effects originate from a universal entropy superpower functional. Two continuity equations close the dynamical description.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper applies concepts from quantum information theory to Deep Inelastic Scattering (DIS) within the framework of high-energy QCD dipoles. It calculates the entanglement entropy by analyzing how tracing over unobserved color degrees of freedom affects the system's density matrix. The results provide a theoretical prediction for entanglement in hadronic structure, comparing it against experimental measurements.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on QCD, the core mathematical machinery—calculating entanglement entropy from reduced density matrices derived from complex field theory dynamics—is highly relevant to open quantum systems and many-body physics simulations.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates how entanglement entropy can be calculated in Deep Inelastic Scattering (DIS) by treating the process using the dipole formulation of high-energy Quantum Chromodynamics (QCD). The goal is to quantify information loss due to tracing over unobserved color degrees of freedom.

**Main result.** The authors derive entanglement entropy directly from von Neumann entropy using dipole multiplicities, finding that the Double Leading Logarithmic Approximation (DLLA) yields a larger entropy compared to other methods for the same initial conditions. These results are compared against empirical measurements from the H1 collaboration.

**Method.** The methodology involves deriving the reduced density matrix by tracing over unobserved color degrees of freedom and solving low-$x$ evolution equations governing dipole multiplicities, considering both transverse-size and azimuthal-angle dependence.

**Model / system.** The physical system is DIS ($	ext{electron} + 	ext{proton}$), modeled using the QCD dipole formulation in the high-energy limit. The theoretical framework relies on solving generalized BFKL-type evolution equations for color dipoles, leading to a description of the proton as an entangled quantum state.

**Key observables.** Entanglement entropy ($S$), dipole multiplicities ($\langle n angle$), and comparison with Shannon entropy measured by the H1 collaboration.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Low Bjorken $x$, transverse size, azimuthal angle dependence, and the evolution scale $Q^2$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The derivation assumes that a reduced density matrix accurately describes the system after tracing over unobserved degrees of freedom. Approximations include using DLLA and assuming high-energy factorization.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 schematically illustrates the scattering process involving two color dipoles, demonstrating coherence loss as the color charge is modified during the interaction.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops from establishing the formalism of entanglement entropy in DIS using the dipole basis to solving the relevant low-$x$ evolution equations (including DLLA) and finally comparing the calculated entropy with existing experimental data.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We study entanglement entropy in Deep Inelastic Scattering (DIS) using the dipole formulation of the high-energy limit of QCD. We argue that a reduced density matrix arises in low $x$ DIS due to a trace over unobserved color degrees of freedom and we obtain entanglement entropy in terms of dipole multiplicities, directly from the von Neumann entropy.   %In the present study we explicitly construct a density matrix representing the entangled state of the photon and proton and perform a trace over unobserved degrees of freedom. Dipole multiplicities are obtained from a solution to low $x$ evolution equations, which we solve numerically. Unlike previous studies, we take into account both transverse-size and azimuthal-angle dependence in the dipole evolution kernel. We study both the exact solution of the equation as well as its double leading-logarithmic approximation (DLLA). We find that for the same initial dipole size, the DLLA solution generates a larger entropy. %We interpret this as information loss due to erasing data on daughter dipole sizes. Finally, we calculate the dipole multiplicities and entanglement entropy and compare our results to the Shannon entropy of hadron multiplicities, as measured by the H1 collaboration.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper introduces quantum-enabled spintronic antennas that overcome the size limitations of traditional antennas by utilizing artificial multiferroic magnonic crystals. By coupling phonons (from acoustic waves) to magnons and subsequently to photons, these structures achieve radiation efficiencies orders of magnitude higher than classical predictions. This breakthrough enables highly miniaturized devices for applications like medical implants and covert communication.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work demonstrates how engineered material couplings (phonon/magnon) can circumvent fundamental classical electromagnetic limits to achieve highly efficient energy radiation from nanoscale devices, which is a key concept in open quantum systems and quantum optics applied to solid-state physics.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Traditional antennas fail to maintain high radiation efficiency when miniaturized below fractions of the radiated wavelength due to classical electromagnetic limitations (Harrington limit).

**Main result.** The use of nanomagnets in magneto-elastic structures successfully generates strong, highly efficient electromagnetic emission from extreme sub-wavelength antennas, significantly exceeding conventional theoretical limits.

**Method.** Measurements involve analyzing radiation efficiency ($\eta = 	ext{Rrad} / (	ext{Rrad} + 	ext{Rloss})$) by comparing samples with and without nanomagnets, alongside characterizing resonant coupling via $S_{11}$ spectra measurements.

**Model / system.** The system is an Artificial Multiferroic Magnonic Crystal (AMMC), comprising a 2D periodic array of nanomagnets deposited on a piezoelectric substrate. Energy transfer relies on tripartite phonon-magnon-photon coupling, exciting hybrid magneto-dynamical modes.

**Key observables.** Radiation efficiency ($\eta$), $S_{11}$ spectra dips indicating resonant energy transfer, and measured receiving gain ($G_r$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The Harrington limit ($\eta \propto (A/\lambda)^2$); the acoustic wavelength ($\lambda_{	ext{ac}}$) which can be much smaller than the electromagnetic wavelength ($\lambda_{	ext{EM}}$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The efficiency enhancement is attributed to utilizing acoustic resonances and non-classical coupling mechanisms, rather than classical magnetic dipole radiation.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate theoretical limitations (dipole formation), basic magneto-elastic principles, the nanomagnet array structure, and experimental results showing signal presence only with nanomagnets at various frequencies.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from identifying the miniaturization challenge to proposing the AMMC system, detailing the underlying physics of phonon-magnon-photon coupling, presenting measurements confirming resonant enhancement, and finally discussing advanced applications like beam steering and stealth communication.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Antennas transmit information wirelessly from one location to another via electromagnetic waves. Miniaturizing them, however, is challenging since the radiation efficiencies of all traditional antennas, based on the principles of classical electromagnetics, plummet when their dimensions are shrunk to tiny fractions of the radiated wavelength. Lately, a new generation of antennas whose operations are underpinned by non-classical principles have been demonstrated and they can overcome this limitation. This enables embedded applications that were hitherto inaccessible. In addition, beam steering, which normally requires a large phased array (multiple antenna elements each much larger than the wavelength), can now be accomplished with a single element much smaller than the wavelength. Some of these antennas also have stealth attributes for secure and covert communication, which makes this new genre a disruptive new technology.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper addresses the scalability challenge in modular trapped-ion quantum computing by tackling the rate and fidelity limitations of remote entanglement links. The authors propose a comprehensive architecture that integrates single-photon heralding with compact photonics and source-level error correction. They conclude that these advancements allow the system to overcome the entanglement bottleneck, suggesting future improvements must focus on local gate operations.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a concrete roadmap for scaling quantum processors by addressing the critical interface problem—the reliable, high-rate connection between independently operating quantum modules using established AMO platforms like trapped ions.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary challenge is overcoming the bottleneck of generating shared entanglement at a rate, density, and fidelity sufficient for fault-tolerant modular quantum computation.

**Main result.** By synthesizing single-photon heralding, integrated photonics, and source-level error correction, the authors show that remote entanglement generation can reach rates compatible with local operations, shifting the computational bottleneck elsewhere.

**Method.** The approach involves developing a comprehensive architecture combining advanced quantum optics techniques (single-photon heralding) with physical integration (trap-integrated photonics) and theoretical protocols (projective distillation).

**Model / system.** The work focuses on modular trapped-ion Quantum Processing Units (QPUs) linked by 'Bell factories' using surface-electrode ion traps. Entanglement is generated via photonic links connecting these modules.

**Key observables.** Projected remote Bell-pair rate density ($10^5 	ext{ s}^{-1}	ext{cm}^{-2}$), and achieved fidelity ($\geq 99.9\%$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Target rates for fault tolerance ($10^4 - 10^6 	ext{ s}^{-1}$ per module); the rate is shown to be limited by the slowest local operation block in a pipeline.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The argument relies on successfully implementing and synthesizing several mature enabling technologies: single-photon heralding, coherent recoil correction, projective distillation, and compact integrated photonics.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the overall remote entanglement generation architecture. Figure 3 compares heralded entanglement rates for different encoding schemes (e.g., Time-bin vs. Polarization). Figure 4 details the pipelined process flow for generating entangled pairs.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the need for modularity, analyzes current limitations in trapped-ion links compared to local gates, and then proposes a synthesized architecture incorporating multiple advanced techniques (heralding, correction, integration) to overcome the entanglement bottleneck.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Modularity underpins classical computing; as quantum processors encounter limits on fabrication yield, reliability, and size, they will need it just as acutely. The bottleneck to linking modules is producing shared entanglement at sufficient rate, density, and fidelity. Trapped ions hold the best demonstrated photonic links, yet they rely on bulky collection optics that cap how densely links can be packed, and their performance trails local gates by two orders of magnitude in rate and fidelity. We synthesize several enabling results $\unicode{x2014}$ single-photon heralding, coherent recoil correction, projective distillation, and trap-integrated photonics $\unicode{x2014}$ into one comprehensive architecture that substantially narrows this gap. Single-photon heralding leads to linear scaling of success probability with detection efficiency, allowing compact integrated photonics to saturate the entanglement rate at a local-operation limit in dense, easy-to-parallelize channels. Addressing its inherent error mechanisms at their source, we project a Bell-pair fidelity of 99.9\% at rates and densities compatible with fault-tolerant operations. Remote entanglement then need not remain the bottleneck for modular trapped-ion computing; the limit shifts to the local operations that must improve regardless.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper experimentally demonstrates a robust Quantum Key Distribution (QKD) system using single photons from semiconductor quantum dots. Crucially, it advances the field by providing rigorous security analysis that accounts for unavoidable imperfections in real-world components like sources and detectors. The results show competitive performance, paving the way for practical, loophole-free quantum communication.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a concrete bridge between theoretical quantum information security proofs and practical experimental realization, which is crucial for advancing photonic quantum communication technologies.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary challenge is rigorously proving the security of Quantum Key Distribution (QKD) protocols when using real-world devices whose performance cannot be perfectly characterized.

**Main result.** The authors demonstrate that a BB84 implementation using semiconductor quantum dots can achieve competitive, loophole-free performance by explicitly accounting for source imperfections and detector noise margins.

**Method.** The work combines experimental measurement of key metrics (QBER, g^(2)(0)) with advanced theoretical security proofs that incorporate statistical bounds on unobservable quantities like the phase error rate.

**Model / system.** The system is an experimental implementation of the BB84 protocol using single photons generated by a semiconductor quantum dot source and analyzed by superconducting nanowire single photon detectors (SNSPDs).

**Key observables.** Quantum Bit Error Rate (QBER) ($\approx 3.51\%$), second-order correlation function g^(2)(0), and the calculated secure key length in bits.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Source mean photon number ($\mu = 0.005$), detector relative efficiencies ($\vec{\eta}$), and error margins associated with device imperfections.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The security analysis relies on recent theoretical advances that allow for imperfect characterization of devices, modeling the real source via Source Map arguments.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the experimental setup (BB84 diagram), confirm single-photon emission statistics using g^(2)(2) histograms, and compare simulated vs. measured key rates considering device imperfections.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from establishing the need to address device loopholes in QKD security proofs, detailing the physical implementation using QD sources, presenting measurements of performance metrics (QBER), and concluding with a rigorous analysis bounding the achievable secure key rate under realistic noise models.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Quantum key distribution (QKD) allows for provably secure key distribution between two trusted parties. Because the security and performance of QKD protocols rely on devices that behave according to specific assumptions, idealized or inaccurate assumptions about device behavior can introduce security loopholes. Real devices can never be perfectly characterized, and their performance metrics are always subject to certain error margins, which must be accounted for in a rigorous theoretical analysis. Only recently have rigorous finite-size results allowed for imperfect characterizations of devices (where device parameter have uncertainty margins) - an advance yet to be considered in experimental implementations of the BB84 protocol. In this work, we prove the security and analyze the performance of an implementation of the BB84 protocol using single photons generated by a semiconductor quantum dot light source in combination with dynamic polarization-state encoding. We consider the presence of incompletely characterized devices by accounting for imperfections in the single-photon source (in terms of finite g(2)(0)) as well as the receiver (non-ideal beam-splitters, finite detector efficiencies, and dark counts), all with error margins. The resulting protocol implementation shows competitive performance, paving the way towards practical and loop-hole free implementations of QKD.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper reports the successful realization of an electron Paul trap fabricated entirely on a monolithic printed circuit board (PCB). This novel design provides mechanical stability for trapping electrons, which serve as qubits. The experiment characterized the trap's performance by measuring lifetimes and determining high secular frequencies using RF/DC field analysis.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses hardware engineering challenges in quantum computing platforms. Using a single-PCB approach significantly enhances scalability and robustness compared to complex multi-component traps, making it highly relevant for solid-state or chip-based AMO quantum systems.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The goal is to develop a practical, scalable platform for quantum information processing using qubits encoded in trapped electron spins, moving away from traditional Penning traps.

**Main result.** The authors successfully demonstrated trapping electrons on a monolithic PCB Paul trap, achieving measurable lifetimes of 2.13 ms and high secular frequencies up to 90 MHz.

**Method.** The experiment involves fabricating the trap entirely on a single printed circuit board (PCB) and characterizing electron confinement using RF/DC electric fields and measuring decay rates.

**Model / system.** The physical system is an electron Paul trap built monolithically on a PCB substrate, utilizing both RF pseudopotentials for radial confinement and DC electrodes for axial confinement. The trapping potential depth was characterized to be around 0.6 eV.

**Key observables.** Trapped electron lifetime ($	au = 2.13$ ms), secular frequencies ($\omega_x \approx 2\pi 	imes 158$ MHz, $\omega_y \approx 2\pi 	imes 161$ MHz), and motional frequency resonances.

**Important parameters / regimes.** RF drive frequency ($\Omega_{	ext{rf}} = 2\pi 	imes 1.732$ GHz), trap depths ($0.59-0.68$ eV), and vacuum pressure ($\approx 5 	imes 10^{-8}$ Torr).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The short lifetime is attributed to field imperfections, lack of active cooling, and difficulty compensating excess micromotion compared to ion traps.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the 3D model of the PCB trap structure, finite-element simulations showing pseudopotentials and calculated frequencies, and experimental setups detailing signal generation for lifetime and motional frequency measurements.

**Paper structure.** The paper details the design of a monolithic Paul trap on a PCB, presents characterization results including measured electron lifetimes and spectral fitting to determine axial and radial secular frequencies by varying DC potentials and RF power.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Qubits encoded in the spin of trapped electrons have been proposed as a promising novel platform for quantum information processing. While trapping of electrons has been largely carried out in Penning traps for precision measurement purposes, it is desirable to use linear Paul traps instead, leaning on the successes of trapped ion quantum processors. Here we present a Paul trap for electrons made of a single printed circuit board. Our approach requires no assembly and the rigid design minimizes manufacturing intolerances. We characterize the trap performance and observe trapped electron lifetimes of 2.13 ms and secular frequencies of up to 90 MHz.

</details>

### [How to improve the discrimination power of classically simulable measurements?](http://arxiv.org/abs/2607.19070v1)

**Authors:** Yiran Wang, Yongming Li  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** quantum information and computing · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2607.19070v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks
**Topic relevance:** 🔥 `quantum measurements` **4/5** · `entanglement & information structure` **1/5** · `measurement-induced transitions` **1/5**

<div class="figure-strip-hint">↔ Scroll figures horizontally</div>

<figcaption class="paper-figure-caption"><b>Fig 2.</b> FIG. 2. General state discrimination framework under CPWP protocol assisted by quantum memories and catalysts. In each round, the input state ρA Z j and the memory state µC j are jointly processed by a CPWP instrument, producing a classical guess Y j and an updated memory state µC j+1. The updated memory is reused in the next round, thereby allowing information from previous rounds to affect later discrimination strategies. In the catalytic setting, the memory state remains unchanged in every round, i.e., µC 1 = µC j for all j ∈{2, · · · , n}.</figcaption>
</figure>
</div>

**Summary.** This paper investigates methods to boost the discrimination power of measurements restricted by classical simulation constraints. By framing this as a channel simulation problem, it derives theoretical bounds and proves that while magic resources can help in some cases, they cannot improve performance beyond the optimal PWF measurement limit when using catalysts or memories for specific state pairs.

**Why it may be interesting.** It rigorously quantifies the gap between what can be achieved classically (CSMs) versus globally, providing fundamental limits on quantum information tasks that are crucial for understanding resource theories in open quantum systems.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The central problem is determining how to enhance the discrimination power of Classically Simulable Measurements (CSMs) beyond their inherent limitations.

**Main result.** The paper establishes a no-go theorem proving that for distinguishing certain states, neither finite quantum catalysts nor quantum memories can improve the optimal success probability achievable with CSMs.

**Method.** The authors relate the measurement problem to channel simulation costs and utilize supermartingale analysis (Azuma's inequality) to derive bounds on the success probability in resource-assisted discrimination protocols.

**Model / system.** The study is set in odd-prime-dimensional quantum systems, focusing on distinguishing pairs of states ($\mu_C$ and $\gamma_C$) using measurements characterized by positive discrete Wigner functions (PWF POVMs).

**Key observables.** Optimal success probability of discrimination ($p_{	ext{opt}}$), the magic cost ($\mathcal{E}_E^c$), and the simulation cost required to simulate quantum channels.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Odd-prime dimension $d=j^n$, number of resources/rounds ($n$), and the degree of classical simulability (PWF property).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis is restricted to distinguishing pairs of states with positive Wigner functions (PWF states) and assumes the use of CPWP measurement channels.

**Paper structure.** The paper first defines CSMs via PWF POVMs, relates this to channel simulation costs, derives bounds on this cost using magic resources, provides a concrete example where enhancement occurs, and concludes with the no-go theorem for catalysts/memories.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Classically simulable measurements (CSMs) constitute an important class of restricted measurements in the odd-prime-dimensional magic resource theory, referring to those measurements with positive discrete Wigner functions. Since their discrimination power is weaker than that of global measurements, it is necessary to study how to improve the discrimination power of CSMs. In this paper, we consider three methods to improve the discrimination power of CSMs, including adding magic resources, using quantum catalysts, and using quantum memories. Specifically, we relate measurements with positive discrete Wigner functions to completely positive Wigner-preserving measurement channels, thereby transforming the problem of improving the discrimination power of CSMs into the problem of determining how many magic resources are required to simulate quantum channels using free operations. Based on this, we derive the lower and upper bounds of the simulation cost. Moreover, we provide a concrete example for which these bounds coincide and prove that consumable magic resources can enhance the discrimination power of CSMs. Finally, we establish a no-go theorem, which shows that for discriminating a pair of states with positive Wigner functions, neither finite-dimensional quantum catalysts nor finite-dimensional quantum memories can improve the optimal success probability of discrimination using CSMs.

</details>

At the crossing point β0 (dotted line) Fβ0β0 is maximal while Fϵϵ is minimal; squares mark the Fϵϵ peak. (c) DRAPE measurement on the |j = 1⟩↔|j + 1 = 2⟩transition of an IBM transmon: P|j=1⟩versus β for k = 1, . . . , 11; solid curves are fits to Eq. (9). Inset: zoom on the crossing locus that...</figcaption>

<figcaption class="paper-figure-caption"><b>Fig 2.</b> FIG. 2. Validation of the DRAPE corrections. (a) The APE and ARE circuits. (b) Pulse shapes: bare Gaussian versus DRAPE-calibrated Gaussian, whose amplitude is rescaled to cancel ˆϵ and to which a DRAG quadrature Ωy = −(ˆβ0/∆2) ˙Ωx is added to cancel the phase error. (c) APE result: P|j=1⟩ versus the final-axis azimuth ϕ for k = 1, 3, 5, 7; the Ramsey fringes fan out with k before correction and collapse onto a single curve after. (d) ARE result: P|j=1⟩after ℓrepetitions of X(π/2)X(π/2); the population drifts from the ideal 1/2 before correction and stays near 1/2 after.</figcaption>

**Summary.** The paper introduces the DRAPE protocol to overcome the limitations of sequential error characterization in superconducting qubits. By designing a single Ramsey-type measurement sequence, it simultaneously calibrates both rotation and phase errors ($\epsilon$ and $\delta$). This method significantly improves gate fidelity by achieving superior scaling for phase estimation compared to traditional iterative techniques.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses a critical bottleneck in quantum computation: characterizing complex, coupled gate errors without resorting to slow, iterative calibration routines. The ability to achieve Heisenberg scaling for one error while maintaining high precision on another simultaneously is highly valuable.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Coherent control errors, specifically over/under-rotation ($\epsilon$) and phase error ($\delta$), in weakly anharmonic qubits require simultaneous calibration to reach fault-tolerance thresholds.

**Main result.** The proposed DRAPE protocol allows for the single-shot, simultaneous calibration of both $\epsilon$ and $\delta$, achieving Heisenberg scaling for phase estimation while saturating the standard quantum limit for rotation error.

**Method.** A Ramsey-type diagnostic sequence (DRAPE) is used to measure population probabilities $P_{|jangle}$ based on a composite unitary operator, allowing simultaneous fitting of both error parameters via Fisher information analysis.

**Model / system.** The study uses weakly anharmonic qubits, experimentally demonstrated on the $|1angle \leftrightarrow |2angle$ transition of an IBM transmon superconducting qubit. The underlying physics is modeled using effective Hamiltonians derived via Schrieffer-Wolff and Magnus expansions.

**Key observables.** Population probabilities $P_{|jangle}$ (or $P_{|j+1angle}$) measured after the gate sequence; Fisher Information ($F_{\xi\xi}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Error parameters ($\epsilon$, $\delta$); Number of repetitions ($N$) leading to Heisenberg scaling for phase estimation.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis is restricted to the weak-driving regime, and derivations rely on truncations (e.g., $O((\Omega/\Delta^2)^2)$) in Hamiltonian expansions.

**Figures summary.** Figures numerically confirm that the Fisher information for phase ($\delta$) shows a maximum at the crossing point $eta_0$, while rotation error ($\epsilon$) is estimated by analyzing slopes away from this point.

**Paper structure.** The paper first derives the coupled error parametrization, then introduces the DRAPE protocol based on measuring population probabilities. It analyzes the scaling of the Fisher information for both parameters at the optimal crossing point $eta_0$, culminating in experimental demonstration and independent validation using established protocols (ARE/APE).

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

In weakly anharmonic qubits, coherent control errors take two generic forms -- over/under-rotation and phase errors -- whose suppression normally requires iterated experiments. We show that, for any symmetric $π/2$ pulse in the weak-driving regime, both follow from a single parametrization, $\mathcal{X}(π/2)=Z(δ)X(π/2+ε)Z(δ)$, that ties the rotation error $ε$ and the phase error $δ$ directly to the system parameters. The parametrization enables DRAPE, a Ramsey-type protocol in which sweeping the phase-error correction reveals a crossing point that fixes both corrections at once. The phase correction is estimated with Heisenberg scaling while the rotation error saturates the standard quantum limit. We experimentally demonstrate DRAPE by calibrating a $π/2$ gate on the $|1\rangle\leftrightarrow |2\rangle$ transition of an IBM transmon, reducing the over-rotation from $0.997^\circ$ to $-0.007^\circ$ and the phase error from $2.52^\circ$ to $0.0052^\circ$ per gate, validated independently by phase- and rotation-error amplification protocols.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces 'magical entanglement' as an invariant measure of bipartite entanglement protected from simplification by Clifford gates. It establishes a framework based on Clifford orbits to classify states into T-magic and W-magic regimes. This distinction helps characterize the fundamental, irreducible quantum correlations present in complex quantum systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a powerful, resource-theoretic diagnostic tool for quantifying genuine quantum correlations that cannot be simulated or removed by local Clifford operations, which is central to understanding quantum computation complexity.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper develops a framework to study 'magic-protected entanglement' (magical entanglement), which quantifies the bipartite entanglement remaining after optimal simplification by Clifford operations.

**Main result.** It identifies two distinct regimes: the T-magic regime, where protected entanglement is weak and state-dependent, and the W-magic regime, where entanglement is strongly self-averaging and irreducible with respect to Clifford reduction.

**Method.** The core method involves developing a Clifford-orbit framework by defining magical entanglement as the minimum bipartite entanglement achievable over all states reachable via Clifford gates.

**Model / system.** The study analyzes pure quantum states $|\psiangle$ in $n$ qubits, focusing on how their entanglement structure is preserved or destroyed under the action of the Clifford group ($C_n$).

**Key observables.** Magical Entanglement ($	ext{MS}$), which serves as an orbit-level invariant; canonical representatives and spectra characterizing the Clifford-irreducible structure.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The classification depends on whether entanglement is tied to nonstabilizerness, distinguishing between T-magic (local nonstabilizer resources) and W-magic (nonlocal nonstabilizer requirements).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes that the physically relevant quantum correlations are those protected from removal by Clifford operations.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate PDF collapse and Q-Q plots comparing standardized fluctuations of $	ext{MS}$ values for different ensembles, showing systematic deviations from Gaussian behavior in W-magic cases.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds a mathematical framework around the Clifford orbit invariance of entanglement measures. It then uses this invariant to classify states into T-magic and W-magic regimes by analyzing how nonstabilizerness protects quantum correlations against stabilizer reduction.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We develop a Clifford-orbit framework for studying magic-protected entanglement, which we refer to as magical entanglement: the part of bipartite entanglement that remains after optimal stabilizer simplification. This construction leverages residual entanglement under Clifford reduction as a state-level organizing principle for magic state space. It defines canonical representatives, spectra, and ranks that characterize the Clifford-irreducible structure of a state. We identify two regimes of the magic-entanglement interplay. In the $T$-magic regime, local nonstabilizer resources can coexist with entanglement, but the protected component remains weak and state-dependent. In the $W$-magic regime, by contrast, entanglement is Clifford-irreducibly tied to nonstabilizerness, producing typical, strongly self-averaging behavior. Analytical examples and random-circuit numerics support a crossover from broad $T$-magic fluctuations to concentrated, Haar-like $W$-magic behavior. These results identify magical entanglement as an orbit-level diagnostic of how nonstabilizerness protects quantum correlations against Clifford reduction.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper rigorously studies the monogamy relations for various entanglement measures, particularly focusing on the Concurrence of Assistance (CoA), in $2 \otimes 2 \otimes d$ quantum systems. It establishes a new explicit inequality relating bipartite concurrence, tangle, and CoA. These results provide deeper quantitative insights into how quantum correlations are distributed among multiple subsystems.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides rigorous mathematical constraints on how entanglement can be distributed among multiple parties, which is fundamental knowledge for quantum cryptography and multipartite quantum computation protocols.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates the monogamy relations governing the distribution of quantum correlations, specifically focusing on the entanglement of assistance (CoA) in multipartite systems.

**Main result.** A key monogamy inequality is established for $2 \otimes 2 \otimes d$ states: $C_2(ho_{AB}) + T(ho_{AC}) \le C_2(|\psiangle A|BC) \le C_a^2(ho_{AB}) + T(ho_{AC})$.

**Method.** The authors use detailed mathematical analysis, including examining singular values derived from state decompositions across multiple cases to prove the required inequalities.

**Model / system.** The study focuses on tripartite pure quantum states $|\psiangle ABC$ within a $2 \otimes 2 \otimes d$ dimensional Hilbert space, involving qubits (A and B) and an ancillary system (C).

**Key observables.** Concurrence ($C$), Tangle ($T$), Concurrence of Assistance ($	ext{CoA}$ or $C_a$), and the three-partite concurrence.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The dimensions are fixed to $2 \otimes 2 \otimes d$, with specific examples analyzed in $d=2$ and $d=3$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on characterizing entanglement measures for pure states and comparing them across different bipartite partitions.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the problem by defining necessary correlation measures, derives specific inequalities (like $I_{\leftarrow}^2(ho_{AB}) \le C_a^2(ho_{AB})$), and culminates in proving the general monogamy relation for the tripartite concurrence.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

The monogamy relations characterize the distribution of quantum correlations among the multipartite quantum systems. We study the monogamy relations of the entanglement of assistance in $2\otimes 2\otimes d$ systems. We present explicitly the relations satisfied by the concurrence, the tangle and the concurrence of assistance, which can be used to derive rigorous monogamy relations. Detailed examples are given to illustrate our results.

</details>

### [Observation of a power transfer controlled by the phase of a quantum superposition](http://arxiv.org/abs/2607.18513v1)

**Authors:** Nathanaël Cottet, Sébastien Jezouin, Bogdan Reznychenko, Alexia Auffèves, Benjamin Huard

**Summary.** The researchers demonstrated that a superconducting transmon qubit can transfer power directionally between two microwave transmission lines based solely on the relative phase ($\phi$) of its superposition state. By setting opposing input drives, they showed the output power oscillates as $\cos(\phi)$, proving that quantum coherence dictates the energy routing in this open quantum system.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a clean demonstration of quantum control over energy flow in an open quantum system (qubit coupled to waveguides), directly linking directional photon emission to the phase coherence ($\phi$) of the emitter, which is central to understanding quantum thermodynamics and measurement processes.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To determine if the direction of energy flow (power transfer) between two spatially separated electromagnetic modes driven by a single qubit can be controlled by the quantum phase ($\phi$) of the qubit's superposition state.

**Main result.** The power transfer exhibits a clear, continuous transition in direction as $\phi$ changes, following a $\cos(\phi)$ dependence. This directional pumping is fundamentally controlled by the qubit's coherence rather than classical drive parameters.

**Method.** Time-resolved measurement of output photon flux in one transmission line while applying opposite-amplitude drives to two coupled lines.

**Model / system.** The system uses a superconducting transmon qubit coupled to two microwave transmission lines. The dynamics are analyzed using input-output relations derived from the Hamiltonian, focusing on stimulated emission competing with reflected drive and spontaneous emission.

**Key observables.** Output power in one line ($\langle a_{	ext{out}}^\dagger a_{	ext{out}}angle$), photon routing efficiency ($\eta$), and the dependence of transfer amplitude on $\cos(\phi)$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The phase $\phi$ of the qubit superposition, the coherence parameter $\langle\sigma_xangle$, and the ratio between spontaneous vs. stimulated emission rates.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The directionality is limited by phase-independent contributions from reflected drive and spontaneous emission; the analysis assumes a lossless emitter coupled to the lines.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the setup showing power transfer driven by two lines, demonstrating that when drives cancel, power transfers via coherence ($\propto \langle\sigma_xangle$). A key figure shows the output power varying as $\cos(\phi)$, confirming directionality switching.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the physical model (qubit coupled to two lines), derives the outgoing photon flux equations, and then analyzes the directional transfer by setting input drives to cancel ($\Omega_a = -\Omega_b$), showing the dependence on $\cos(\phi)$ through time-resolved measurements.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

A driven qubit exchanges energy with the propagating modes that drive it. When two spatially separated modes drive a single qubit with opposite amplitudes, their net action on the qubit cancels. Yet the qubit can still transfer power from one mode to the other through stimulated emission. The directionality originates from opposite stimulated emission powers into each line. We realize this situation with a superconducting transmon qubit coupled to two transmission lines and show that the direction of the power flow is set by the phase $φ$ of the qubit superposition between its ground and excited states, rather than by any classical control parameter. From a time-resolved measurement of the output power in one line, we observe a transfer that varies as $\cosφ$ and hence changes direction between $φ=0$ and $φ=π$. The directionality of the total power flow is limited by the phase independent contributions of the reflected drive and of spontaneous emission, which sets a routing efficiency that we measure as a function of the input power. For an equal superposition of ground and excited qubit states (maximal coherence), the efficiency reaches $63\%$, close to the bound of $69\%$ expected from the measured qubit coherence times.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper explores how chiral coupling between transmons and a waveguide can mediate nonlinear quantum optical effects to generate momentum-anticorrelated photon pairs. By combining waveguide driving with independently tunable local drives, the researchers demonstrate precise control over the transmitted light's quantum statistics, tuning it from anti-bunched to super-bunched regimes.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work bridges superconducting circuit QED with quantum optics concepts like nonlinear light generation and photon correlation engineering, providing a platform for realizing complex quantum optical phenomena in solid-state systems.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The research investigates nonlinear optics mediated by chiral waveguide Quantum Electrodynamics (QED) to achieve the generation of momentum-anticorrelated photon pairs.

**Main result.** The interplay between waveguide driving and local driving allows for the engineering of quantum statistics in the transmitted field, enabling tunable control over $g^{(2)}(0)$ across anti-bunched, coherent, and strongly bunched regimes.

**Method.** The study employs both semi-classical simulations (using input-output theory) and full quantum wavefunction calculations to analyze light transmission through coupled transmon qubits.

**Model / system.** The system consists of multiple transmons (superconducting qubits) chirally coupled to a one-dimensional waveguide. The dynamics are governed by a Hamiltonian incorporating local driving, waveguide coupling, and dissipation via a Lindblad master equation.

**Key observables.** Second-order correlation function ($g^{(2)}(0)$), coherent transmission amplitude ($\langle\hat{\alpha}angle$), and the power spectrum density of the incoherent component.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Rabi frequencies ($\Omega_{wg}, \Omega_d$), detunings ($\Delta_n$), and the coupling strength ($\Gamma$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes $\hbar=1$, perfect coupling efficiency, and utilizes approximations like Born-Markov for some simulations.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the system setup (transmons coupled to a waveguide) and show quantitative results such as $T_{inc}$ vs. Rabi frequency, power spectra of the incoherent component, and $g^{(2)}(	au)$ confirming bunching behavior.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from defining the physical model (Hamiltonian/Master Equation) to analyzing coherent transmission via input-output theory, then detailing how single or multiple chiral transmons modify photon statistics by generating an incoherent component, which can be controlled using local driving fields.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

In recent years, chiral quantum optics has emerged as an active research area due to the promising applications in quantum information processing as well as nonlinear optics. We present results on the properties of the incoherent component of the transmitted light after transmons chirally coupled to a waveguide. The well-known resonance fluorescence of one chiral transmon resembles nonlinear quantum optics as it can convert the coherent light, with photons having spatially extensive coherence and Poisson distribution, into a field with spatially localized coherence and bunching photon statistics. However, another chiral transmon can undo the effect of the first transmon regardless of the Rabi frequency under the idealization involved in this work. A full wavefunction calculation shows that, in the weak driving limit, this incoherent light mainly comes from two-photon processes - one chiral transmon converts two independent photons into a photon pair with opposite momentum shift. In addition to the driving through the waveguide, a local driving with independently tunable amplitude and phase can address each transmon individually. The interplay between the waveguide driving and local driving modulates the contribution of the incoherent transmission and hence enables the engineering of the quantum statistics of the transmitted field, with tunable $g^{(2)}(0)$ spanning anti-bunched, coherent, and strongly bunched regimes.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces a novel continuous-variable photonic memory architecture designed to store the history of evolving information using multimode coherent states. It achieves this through metadata-assisted indexing and reversible displacement operations, allowing for reliable rollback retrieval of past states. The theoretical framework quantifies storage limits using metrics like fidelity decay and distinguishability capacity.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses quantum memory limitations by proposing a method to store temporal data streams rather than just single states, which is crucial for quantum simulation and computation.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The core problem is designing a quantum memory architecture capable of storing not just static data, but also the entire time-evolving trajectory or history of information.

**Main result.** The authors propose a metadata-assisted continuous-variable photonic memory that allows for reversible retrieval and reconstruction of past states by treating memory as an evolving phase-space trajectory.

**Method.** The framework combines multimode coherent state encoding with displacement operations, utilizing a classical 'digital twin' to track history while the physical loop handles updates.

**Model / system.** The system uses continuous-variable (CV) photonic platforms storing information in multimode coherent states within an optical loop. Updates are modeled by controlled displacement operations applied iteratively, simulating temporal evolution.

**Key observables.** Retrieval fidelity ($F_n$), Rollback Retrieval Fidelity ($FRB$), Memory Lifetime ($N_{life}$), and Rollback Distinguishability Capacity ($CRSD$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Loop gain ($\eta$), noise variance ($\sigma^2_{\epsilon}$), and the propagation factor ($\lambda$) governing state evolution.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The architecture relies on a classical metadata layer (digital twin) to index and reconstruct history, separating logical tracking from physical storage. The theoretical analysis assumes controlled noise modeling.

**Figures summary.** Not specified in notes; the focus is on deriving analytical expressions for fidelity decay and capacity metrics.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops a comprehensive framework by first defining the CV photonic system, then detailing the incremental update dynamics (forward evolution), followed by the theoretical derivation of reversible rollback mechanisms, culminating in information-theoretic characterizations like $CRSD$.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Modern information systems increasingly require memory architectures capable of storing not only static data but also the evolution of information over time. While continuous-variable photonic platforms have been extensively studied for quantum communication and computation, their potential as dynamically evolving memory systems remains largely unexplored. In this work, we introduce a metadata-assisted continuous-variable photonic memory architecture in which information is represented by latent vectors encoded across multimode coherent states and updated through reversible displacement operations. The framework combines multimode storage with metadata-based indexing, enabling navigation and reconstruction of historical memory states through rollback retrieval.   Unlike conventional approaches that focus on preserving a single quantum state, the proposed framework treats memory as a continuously evolving trajectory in continuous-variable phase space. This perspective naturally extends beyond image storage to temporal data streams, machine-learning latent representations, scientific simulations, and other forms of dynamically changing information. Although presented as a theoretical proof-of-concept, the results suggest that continuous-variable photonic systems may provide a promising foundation for future metadata-aware memory architectures capable of storing, tracking, and reconstructing the history of evolving information.

</details>
