**Summary.** This work theoretically examines how weakly interacting electrons behave in quasicrystals, showing that Non-Fermi-Liquid physics arises naturally even without strong correlations. The authors use advanced many-body techniques to analyze anomalous temperature dependencies and scattering rates dictated by the quasiperiodic structure's local electronic environment. It provides a crucial guide for interpreting experimental data from artificial or natural quasicrystalline materials.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on condensed matter, the study of electronic states in aperiodically structured materials provides insights into localization and spectral fragmentation, which are relevant concepts when modeling complex quantum systems or disordered media.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates Non-Fermi-Liquid (NFL) behaviors in weakly interacting two-dimensional quasicrystals, challenging the applicability of standard Fermi-liquid theory to aperiodic lattices.

**Main result.** NFL behavior emerges generically even with weak interactions, characterized by anomalous temperature dependences in thermodynamic quantities and a finite residual scattering rate linked to local density of states inhomogeneity.

**Method.** The analysis employs a weak-coupling perturbative framework using Hartree mean-field theory and Green's functions to calculate various electronic properties.

**Model / system.** The system is modeled by electrons hopping on quasiperiodic lattices, specifically Penrose and Ammann–Beenker tilings, governed by a Hubbard Hamiltonian with weak repulsive interactions ($U$).

**Key observables.** Sommerfeld coefficient ($\gamma$), magnetic susceptibility ($\chi$), Wilson ratio ($R_W$), self-energy ($\Sigma$), and local density of states ($ho_i$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Weak interaction strength ($U/t$), temperature ($T$), and filling fraction.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on perturbative expansions (e.g., second-order perturbation theory) and the random phase approximation for magnetic susceptibility, which are noted to have limitations in certain regimes.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate tiling patterns, real-space maps of LDOS ($ho_i$), temperature dependence of charge density ($\sigma_{n_i}$), and scaling plots comparing electronic exponents across different tilings and fillings.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first establishing the theoretical framework for quasiperiodic systems (path integrals in perpendicular space) before applying it to calculate key observables like $\gamma(T)$, $\chi(T)$, and $\Sigma(\omega)$ to diagnose NFL behavior.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Non-Fermi-liquid (NFL) behavior is usually associated with strong electronic correlations, quantum criticality, or singular fluctuations that invalidate the quasiparticle picture. Quasicrystals are known to provide a fundamentally different route to such physics: their lack of translational symmetry gives rise to critical single-particle states and fragmented spectra even in the absence of electron-electron interactions. Here, we numerically study the NFL behaviors in quasicrystals with weak interactions. Using Penrose and Ammann--Beenker tilings as representative quasiperiodic systems, we analyze thermodynamic, magnetic, and transport properties within a weak-coupling perturbative framework. The Sommerfeld coefficient and magnetic susceptibility show anomalous temperature dependences governed by the singular energy dependence of the density of states near the Fermi level. The Wilson ratio reveals that low-temperature NFL behavior in quasicrystals is accompanied by enhanced spin fluctuations over a broad range of filling fractions, while its magnitude and temperature dependence vary sensitively with filling fraction. The self-energy shows anomalous scaling and a finite residual scattering rate, indicating the breakdown of quasiparticles. Our site-resolved analysis reveals a pronounced enhancement of the residual scattering rate in regions with a high local density of states. These results serve as a guide to analyze experimental data for quasicrystals and to identify the microscopic mechanism of the NFL behaviors.

</details>

A symmetry-protected vacuum-noise fixed point appears at g = κ = 2, where the squeezing of P3 becomes independent of the evolution length. (b)–(d) are evaluated with L = 100 and κ = 2. **Summary.** This paper proposes a new paradigm for non-Hermitian quantum sensing by exploiting hidden symmetries rather than relying on exceptional points. Using a continuous-variable model unifying PT and APT symmetries, the authors demonstrate that at a symmetry-protected vacuum-noise fixed point, collective quantum noise is actively suppressed below the vacuum level. This results in enhanced quantum sensing sensitivity through noise control, establishing a distinct theoretical route for quantum metrology.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a novel theoretical framework for quantum metrology by shifting focus from spectral anomalies (EPs) to fundamental symmetries protecting the vacuum state. It connects topological concepts (hidden symmetry, Dirac spectrum) directly to noise control in open quantum systems, which is highly relevant to AMO and quantum optics.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To develop a quantum sensing mechanism that surpasses the fundamental limitation of enhanced sensitivity near Exceptional Points (EPs) being accompanied by amplified quantum noise, thereby limiting the Signal-to-Noise Ratio (SNR).

**Main result.** The authors propose an alternative paradigm where quantum sensing is enhanced through the symmetry-protected suppression of excess quantum noise, achieved at a hidden symmetry-protected vacuum-noise fixed point (VFP). This mechanism maintains finite response sensitivity while achieving superior SNR compared to conventional EP approaches.

**Method.** The study employs a fully quantum continuous-variable model and analyzes the system's eigenspectrum derived from an effective Hamiltonian exhibiting a non-Hermitian Dirac structure. The key diagnostic involves calculating the variance of collective quadratures relative to vacuum noise.

**Model / system.** The physical model is a three-mode bosonic system coupling degenerate phase-sensitive amplification and detuned non-degenerate parametric processes, unifying Parity-Time (PT) and Anti-Parity-Time (APT) symmetries. The dynamics are analyzed using Heisenberg–Langevin equations leading to an effective Hamiltonian $H_{eff}$ possessing a hidden chiral symmetry.

**Key observables.** Unitless sensitivity $S(O, 	heta)$, collective quadrature variances ($\Delta P_3^2 / 3$), and the identification of the symmetry-protected vacuum-noise fixed point (VFP).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Detuning ($\delta$), nonlinear coupling ($\kappa_{PT}$), amplification gain ($g$), and linear coupling ($\kappa_{APT}$). The critical regime is defined by balancing these symmetries.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes the system can be realized on an integrated photonic platform, maintaining standard bosonic commutation relations without needing to explicitly include additional Langevin noise terms in the Heisenberg equations.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the evolution of the non-Hermitian Dirac eigenspectrum under balanced symmetries and show that the quantum fluctuation variance ($\Delta P_3^2 / 3$) is squeezed below vacuum noise at the symmetry-protected point, while sensitivity metrics peak there.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the problem with EPs. It then develops a unified PT/APT continuous-variable model and analyzes its non-Hermitian Dirac eigenspectrum. The core of the work involves demonstrating that at a specific hidden symmetry-protected point, quantum noise is suppressed (VFP), leading to enhanced sensing performance distinct from EP enhancement.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Exceptional-point (EP) sensing has attracted considerable interest because of its anomalous response scaling. However, recent studies have shown that the enhanced response near an EP is inevitably accompanied by amplified quantum noise, fundamentally limiting the achievable signalto-noise ratio (SNR). Here, we propose a fundamentally different route toward non-Hermitian quantum sensing based on symmetry-protected noise suppression rather than response amplification. We develop a fully quantum continuous-variable model that unifies parity-time (PT) and anti-paritytime(APT) symmetries within a single framework. Exploiting the incompatibility between these two symmetries, we uncover a non-Hermitian Dirac eigenspectrum and reveal a hidden symmetryprotected phase transition embedded in the Hamiltonian spectrum. Remarkably, this hidden phasetransition simultaneously constitutes a symmetry-protected vacuum-noise fixed point, where collective three-mode quadratures exhibit suppressed quantum fluctuations despite the absence of any anomalous spectral response. As a consequence, quantum sensing is enhanced through the suppression of excess quantum noise while maintaining a finite response sensitivity, establishing a sensing mechanism fundamentally different from conventional EP-based approaches. These results reveal an unexpected connection between hidden symmetry, quantum fluctuations, and non-Hermitian quantum metrology, and establish noise suppression as an alternative paradigm for non-Hermitian quantum sensing.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper explores non-Hermitian quantum walks on lattices with spatially varying dissipation. It introduces the concept of a quasi-NHEB, demonstrating that nonuniform loss alone can create anomalous boundary loss accumulation even without the Non-Hermitian Skin Effect. This highlights that structured dissipation offers an independent and powerful control mechanism for engineering edge physics.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a generalized framework for understanding boundary effects beyond standard topological invariants, showing how engineered disorder (nonuniform loss) can independently induce robust edge phenomena.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates non-Hermitian boundary phenomena, specifically how spatially nonuniform loss influences the Non-Hermitian Edge Burst (NHEB) in quantum walks.

**Main result.** Spatially nonuniform loss alone can generate a quasi-non-Hermitian edge burst ($	ext{quasi-NHEB}$) even when the system lacks the Non-Hermitian Skin Effect (NHSE).

**Method.** The analysis involves simulating quantum walker dynamics on a tight-binding lattice, calculating the boundary loss probability ($P_x$), and comparing scaling relations between different non-Hermiticity regimes.

**Model / system.** The model is a one-dimensional non-Hermitian tight-binding chain with two coupled sublattices (A and B). Non-Hermiticity arises from imaginary on-site loss ($\gamma_x$) on sublattice B, which can be made spatially nonuniform and influenced by an artificial magnetic flux ($\phi$).

**Key observables.** Loss probability distribution ($P_x$), Mean Inverse Participation Ratio ($	ext{MIPR}$), Non-Hermitian Edge Burst (NHEB) magnitude, and the dependence of boundary loss on initial position ($x_0$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Magnetic flux ($\phi$), spatial gradient/variation of loss ($\gamma_x$), and the comparison between uniform vs. nonuniform loss regimes.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on open boundary conditions for the quantum walk, and it distinguishes between phenomena driven by NHSE versus those driven purely by inhomogeneous dissipation.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the schematic lattice setup with site-dependent loss; they show $	ext{MIPR}$ vs. flux to diagnose NHSE, and spatial maps of the loss probability ($P_x$) comparing uniform, quasi-, and conventional edge bursts under varying fluxes and loss gradients.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically investigates three regimes: (i) Quasi-NHEB induced by nonuniform loss at zero flux ($\phi=0$); (ii) Hybrid NHEB resulting from the interplay of NHSE and nonuniform loss ($\phi=-\pi/2$); and (iii) Comparison against the conventional NHEB found in uniform loss models. The structure builds the argument that dissipation gradient is a key control parameter.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Non-Hermitian quantum walks on lossy lattices with open boundaries can exhibit an anomalous peak of the loss probability at the boundary, known as the non-Hermitian edge burst (NHEB). This phenomenon has been attributed to the combined effect of the non-Hermitian skin effect (NHSE) and a gapless imaginary spectrum. Here we investigate a class of models in which the NHSE is induced by magnetic flux while the loss is spatially nonuniform. We show that the spatial distribution of loss plays a crucial role in determining the emergence and strength of the NHEB. Notably, even in the absence of the NHSE, a weak boundary accumulation of the loss probability persists. We term this effect a quasi-non-Hermitian edge burst (quasi-NHEB). By analyzing the dependence of the loss probability on the initial position, we further demonstrate that the quasi-NHEB obeys a bulk-edge scaling relation distinct from that of conventional NHEB. Our results show that spatially nonuniform loss alone can generate boundary-localized loss anomalies even without the NHSE, providing new insight into non-Hermitian boundary phenomena and a broader platform for their exploration and potential applications.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper resolves apparent conflicts between unitary quantum mechanics and the second law of thermodynamics by analyzing the system in the thermodynamic limit. It demonstrates that time-reversal symmetry breaks down naturally, leading to irreversible entropy increase and decoherence. This framework successfully derives the Born rule from fundamental principles governing large quantum systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a deep theoretical framework connecting fundamental quantum dynamics (unitary evolution) to macroscopic irreversibility and equilibrium states, which is central to open quantum systems theory and non-equilibrium statistical mechanics.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses quantum mechanics' perceived incompleteness regarding the second law of thermodynamics and the physical mechanism behind measurement-induced decoherence.

**Main result.** It shows that in the thermodynamic limit, time-reversal symmetry breaks down naturally, leading to irreversible evolution, entropy increase, and a recovery of classical statistical mechanical predictions like the Born rule.

**Method.** The analysis uses advanced mathematical techniques involving the Liouville-von Neumann equation, spectral theory (analyzing the Liouvillian spectrum), and comparing time limits ($\lim_{t	o\infty}\lim_{N,V	o\infty}$ vs. $\lim_{N,V	o\infty}\lim_{t	o\infty}$).

**Model / system.** The focus is on isolated quantum systems in the thermodynamic limit, analyzed via their time evolution governed by the Liouvillian superoperator.

**Key observables.** Entropy increase, loss of quantum coherence (pure to mixed states), and the relaxation dynamics described by Lyapunov functionals ($H_t$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The thermodynamic limit ($	ext{N}, 	ext{V} 	o \infty$), time $t$, and inverse temperature $eta$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The theory requires studying systems with absolutely continuous spectra to exhibit the necessary irreversible dynamics; finite systems are subject to Poincaré recurrence.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first identifying the conflict between QM and thermodynamics, then mathematically demonstrating how time-asymmetry emerges in the thermodynamic limit via spectral analysis of the Liouvillian, culminating in a description of measurement collapse consistent with statistical mechanics.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Quantum mechanics is widely recognised as being incomplete. It is not consistent with the second law of thermodynamics and does not provide a scientifically credible physical account of the measurement process, the means by which coherence is broken and classically observable states are recorded. This had led to many ad hoc assumptions being used to account for various properties of quantum systems, among which is the coherence time of quantum devices that determines their ability to perform computations. Here, we show that all these properties can be accommodated naturally and consistently in the context of quantum systems which exhibit continuous spectra, as arises in the thermodynamic limit of large systems. In particular, for isolated systems we show that the time-reversal symmetry associated with unitary time evolution of the quantum state gives rise to time-symmetry breaking and a semi-group evolution which attains thermodynamic equilibrium at long times. Moreover, the emergence of this non-unitary time-asymmetry leads to microcanonical equilibrium states in which all quantum coherence is lost and is accompanied by the transformation of pure states into mixtures, leading in turn to an increase in entropy. Inclusion of a macroscopic measurement apparatus shows how the outcome of a measurement corresponds to the von Neumann projection postulate, arising with probabilities in conformance with the Born rule. The mathematical structure of the theory which applies to quantum systems with continuous spectra is closely analogous to the classical ergodic theory of dynamical systems and the conditions under which they attain equilibrium states.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper develops a method based on intrinsic information compression to study quantum dynamics at criticality in various disordered models. By truncating the Hamiltonian spectrum to only essential energy levels, the authors show that the resulting simplified description accurately predicts key dynamical observables like the survival probability decay. This establishes energy-space compression as a fundamental principle governing critical behavior.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a powerful theoretical tool—energy-space compression—to analyze complex quantum dynamics near critical points in disordered systems, offering insights applicable to many condensed matter problems where full diagonalization is impossible.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study aims to understand quantum dynamics at the critical boundary of ergodicity, where highly excited states deviate from thermal behavior, by finding efficient ways to describe their complex energy space.

**Main result.** The power-law decay of the survival probability at criticality can be accurately reproduced using only a vanishing fraction of the total Hamiltonian eigenlevels, establishing intrinsic information compression in energy space.

**Method.** The core method involves an intrinsic information compression technique applied to the energy spectrum, where dynamics are approximated by truncating the Hamiltonian basis to include only the most significant eigenstates.

**Model / system.** The research investigates various quantum many-body systems exhibiting ergodicity breaking transitions, including the 3D Anderson model (3DA), Power-law Random Banded Model (PLRB), Quantum Sun Model (QSM), and Ultrametric Model (UM).

**Key observables.** Survival Probability $R(	au)$, Spectral Form Factor (SFF) $	ilde{K}(	au)$, Wavefunction Fractal Dimension ($d_2$), and the level-spacing distribution exponent ($\Delta_2$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The critical exponents, such as $d'_2$ and $	ilde{\delta}_2$, are key; the analysis focuses on the thermodynamic limit where the fraction of retained levels vanishes.

**Assumptions / limitations.** It is assumed that the dynamics can be effectively captured by a small subset of energy eigenstates (intrinsic information compression), and localized states at spectral edges must be filtered out to reveal true critical behavior.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the agreement between $R(	au)$ and $	ilde{K}(	au)$ via power-law decay, show the fractal nature ($	ilde{\delta}_2 < 1$) of the truncated spectrum, and map out gap ratios distinguishing localized, delocalized, and critical states.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically analyzes multiple disordered models (3DA, PLRB, QSM, UM) by first identifying spectral edges containing localized states. It then applies energy-space truncation to filter these edges, demonstrating that the resulting truncated Spectral Form Factor accurately mimics the power-law decay of the survival probability at criticality.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Highly excited quantum states at the critical boundary of ergodicity are known to deviate from thermal behavior, yet their dynamical properties remain poorly understood. Here, we uncover the complexity of quantum dynamics at criticality through the lens of intrinsic information compression in energy space. We show that the Hamiltonian spectrum can be systematically truncated, yielding a simplified description of the dynamics while preserving its essential features. Specifically, for both interacting and noninteracting systems, we demonstrate that a vanishing fraction of Hamiltonian eigenlevels suffices to reproduce the power-law decay of the survival probability. The resulting truncated spectrum exhibits a fractal structure characterized by a level-spacing distribution with a power-law tail, while its spectral form factor displays the same asymptotic power-law decay as the survival probability.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical paper derives the quantum state of light emitted from randomly oriented chiral sources, accounting for electric dipole, magnetic dipole, and quadrupole contributions. It shows that measuring correlations between photons collected in different directions or polarizations allows experimental separation of these underlying physical coupling parameters. This advances understanding of symmetry breaking in spontaneous emission.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a rigorous quantum optical framework for characterizing chiral emitters by exploiting spatial coherence, which is crucial for understanding fundamental symmetries in light-matter interactions beyond simple dipole approximations.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper derives the quantum state of light emitted via spontaneous optical emission from a randomly oriented chiral source, aiming to characterize the multipolar nature ($\mu, m, Q$) of this emission.

**Main result.** By analyzing spatial coherence and collecting photons in specific polarization/direction combinations (e.g., opposite directions), one can isolate parameters related to electric dipole, magnetic dipole, and quadrupole moments.

**Method.** The analysis uses quantum electrodynamics formalism, deriving density matrices ($ar{ho}$) for the emitted photon state by averaging over random source orientations and projecting onto specific polarization/propagation modes.

**Model / system.** The system is a quantum emitter (modeled as a two-level system) undergoing spontaneous emission into the electromagnetic field. The interaction Hamiltonian includes electric dipole, magnetic dipole, and electric quadrupole terms.

**Key observables.** The one-photon state density operator ($ar{ho}$), which depends on polarization basis states (L/R circular polarization) and collection directions ($\hat{z}, \hat{x}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Unitless parameters $\epsilon_\mu, \epsilon_m, \epsilon_Q$ characterizing the relative strengths of dipole, magnetic dipole, and quadrupole contributions.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The emitter is treated as a two-level system; rotational averaging is performed over all source orientations; approximations are made in the continuum limit for quadrupole terms.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate collection geometries using polarizers and apertures, showing how measurements can be restricted to specific polarization/direction combinations (e.g., opposite $\hat{z}$ propagation).

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from deriving the general one-photon state ($ho$) including all multipoles, simplifying this for random orientation averages, and finally developing measurement protocols using circular polarizations and directional correlations to separate the underlying physical parameters.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We derive the form of the one-photon state of the electromagnetic field resulting from spontaneous optical emission of a randomly oriented chiral quantum source, including electric dipole, magnetic dipole, and electric quadrupole contributions to the radiation. We describe how spatial coherence in the emission can be exploited to reveal hidden information about the emitter. This work serves as a prelude and companion piece to our concurrently submitted work \cite{Backlund2026Letter} in which we present the classical and quantum error bounds associated with assigning the handedness of such an emitter.

</details>

**Summary.** This work theoretically analyzes how time evolution under a Hamiltonian can generate state k-designs, which are quantum states exhibiting maximal randomness. Using the frame potential as a diagnostic tool, the authors derive scaling relations and propose efficient M-step quench protocols to suppress finite-time errors. This provides a unified framework for understanding design generation in both random matrix theory settings and physically relevant local Hamiltonians.

**Why it may be interesting.** The connection between quantum dynamics (Hamiltonian evolution) and achieving highly random states (state $k$-designs) is fundamental to understanding thermalization and scrambling in many-body systems. The explicit derivation of quench protocols provides concrete tools for experimental realization.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates the conditions under which time evolution generated by a Hamiltonian can produce an ensemble that approximates a state $k$-design, a property related to maximal randomness.

**Main result.** It derives relations showing that for GUE Hamiltonians, generating state $k$-designs requires only that the initial state ensemble forms a state 1-design. Furthermore, it proposes quench protocols to suppress finite-time errors exponentially.

**Method.** The analysis heavily relies on calculating and analyzing the 'frame potential' ($F^{(k)}_E$) for both state and unitary ensembles, using techniques involving Weingarten calculus and recursion relations derived from moment matching.

**Model / system.** The study considers quantum systems evolving under a fixed Hamiltonian $H$, initially modeled by Hamiltonians drawn from the Gaussian Unitary Ensemble (GUE). It extends this to physically relevant models like the mixed-field Ising Hamiltonian.

**Key observables.** State ensemble $k$-th frame potential ($F^{(k)}_E$) and relative error ($\delta F^{(k)}_E$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Evolution time $T$, design order $k$, system size $N$, and the quench step number $M$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis often assumes the large evolution time limit ($T 	o \infty$) or relies on specific no-resonance conditions for the Hamiltonian eigenvalues.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the setup of state ensemble generation via time evolution, show the scaled frame potential matching Haar values for the Ising model, and demonstrate the exponential suppression of finite-$T$ errors using quench protocols.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from analyzing the general GUE case to deriving recursion relations for the frame potential. It then applies these methods to a physically realistic mixed-field Ising Hamiltonian, detailing the necessary quench protocols to achieve high precision in state $k$-design generation, and finally unifying the results for unitary ensembles.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We study the generation of state $k$-designs from time evolution under a fixed Hamiltonian. Specifically, we consider the ensemble $\mathcal{E}=\left\{e^{-iHt}|ψ_0\rangle | \ t\sim \mathrm{Unif}[0,T],\, |ψ_0\rangle\sim \mathcal{E}'\right\}$, where the initial states are sampled from an ensemble $\mathcal{E}'$. For Hamiltonians drawn from the Gaussian unitary ensemble, we derive a simple relation between the frame potential of the evolved ensemble $\mathcal{E}$ and that of the initial ensemble $\mathcal{E}'$ in the large evolution time limit. This relation shows that $\mathcal{E}$ forms an exact state $k$-design in the thermodynamic limit as long as $\mathcal{E}'$ forms a state 1-design. Remarkably, we further show, both analytically and numerically, that time evolution under a simple nonintegrable mixed-field Ising Hamiltonian can generate approximate state $k$-designs with high precision, starting from product states in an appropriately chosen Pauli basis. We also analyze the finite-$T$ correction and find it scales as $O(1/T)$. To reduce the evolution time, we propose an $M$-step quench protocol that suppresses this correction to $O(1/T^M)$, which is also verified numerically. We then extend our analysis to unitary ensembles, deriving an analogous recursion relation for the unitary frame potential. Our results elucidate the mechanisms underlying recent proposals for generating unitary $k$-designs through sequential quantum quenches in a unified manner.

</details>

**Authors:** Yvan Castin, Ralph Dum  
**Type:** both · **Category:** quantum gases · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2607.18944v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks
**Topic relevance:** `analog quantum simulation` **3/5** · `non-equilibrium dynamics` **3/5** · `methods for driven-dissipative` **2/5** · `non-equilibrium universality` **2/5** · `Keldysh / 2PI / non-Gaussian methods` **1/5** · `driven-dissipative phase transition` **1/5**

**Summary.** This paper develops an analytical theory to model Bose-Einstein condensates expanding from time-dependent harmonic traps. By using a unitary scaling transform on the Gross-Pitaevskii equation, the complex dynamics are reduced to solving for three key scaling parameters. This method allows researchers to quantitatively analyze experimental measurements of cloud expansion and collective excitations to determine material properties like the scattering length.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a powerful analytical framework for analyzing non-equilibrium dynamics in trapped quantum gases, connecting macroscopic observables (like aspect ratio) directly to fundamental interaction parameters ($a$) without needing direct measurement of trap frequencies.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To derive analytical results for the macroscopic wave function of a Bose-Einstein condensate (BEC) evolving within a time-dependent harmonic potential.

**Main result.** The BEC dynamics are entirely characterized by the evolution of three scaling parameters ($\lambda_j(t)$), allowing for *ab initio* calculation of time-of-flight signals and fitting experimental data to determine scattering lengths.

**Method.** The authors employ a unitary quantum scaling transform applied to the Gross-Pitaevskii equation, reducing the complex dynamics to solving coupled differential equations for the scaling factors.

**Model / system.** The system is a BEC governed by the time-dependent Gross-Pitaevskii equation in an anisotropic harmonic trap. The initial state uses the Thomas-Fermi approximation, and the evolution is analyzed via classical gas analogies.

**Key observables.** Spatial density profile, aspect ratio of the cloud width ($W_z/W_\perp$), and scattering length ($a$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The three scaling factors $\lambda_j(t)$, the trapping frequencies $\omega_j(t)$, and the interaction strength $g$ (related to scattering length $a$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** Approximations include neglecting terms of order 2 in the modulation parameter ($\epsilon$) and assuming linear response for small excitations. The mean-field treatment is noted to fail for long free oscillation times.

**Figures summary.** Figure 2 compares theoretical predictions (solid line) with experimental data (diamonds from MIT) showing the aspect ratio versus free oscillation time ($	au_o$).

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the GPE and introduces the scaling transform. It derives the differential equations for $\lambda_j(t)$, analyzes specific cases like sudden trap opening, and then details the linear response theory to calculate excitations after modulation and time-of-flight.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We present analytical results for the macroscopic wave function of a Bose-Einstein condensate in a time dependent harmonic potential. The evolution of the spatial density is a dilatation, characterized by three scaling factors which allow a classical interpretation of the dynamics. This approach is an efficient tool for the analysis of recent experimental results on the expansion and collective excitation of a condensate.

</details>

**Authors:** Sumedha, Abhishek Singh

**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks
**Topic relevance:** `Keldysh / 2PI / non-Gaussian methods` **3/5** · `methods for driven-dissipative` **2/5** · `analog quantum simulation` **1/5** · `correlated / nonlocal dissipation` **1/5** · `correlated cavity matter` **1/5** · `driven-dissipative phase transition` **1/5** · `entanglement & information structure` **1/5** · `non-equilibrium dynamics` **1/5** · `non-equilibrium universality` **1/5**

We have taken ek = 1 and hence γ = α. a) for Hopfield model,b) k = 4 DenseAM. In both cases the function R(m) is plotted near, above and below the transition, In (c) k = 4 basin of m = 0 state is shown to illustrtate that it is always present. In (d) R(m) for LSE model above and below the transition is shown.</figcaption>

**Summary.** This paper develops a powerful theoretical framework using large deviations theory to calculate the free energy landscape for various dense associative memory models. It shows that increasing the interaction order leads to first-order phase transitions, meaning successful memory retrieval critically depends on starting near the correct initial state basin. This provides systematic tools for analyzing complex, high-dimensional energy minimization problems.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on classical neural networks, the use of advanced statistical mechanics tools like large deviations theory to characterize complex energy landscapes is highly relevant for understanding metastable states in quantum many-body systems or optimization problems involving quantum circuits.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is deriving a general expression for the free energy landscape of dense associative memories (DenseAMs) and analyzing how memory retrieval dynamics depend on initial conditions.

**Main result.** The method provides an exact free energy functional for polynomial interactions, revealing that for higher-order networks ($k>2$), retrieval exhibits first-order transitions dependent on the starting state's basin of attraction.

**Method.** The authors employ large deviations theory and a tilted large deviations principle to derive the free energy functional $I_eta(\mathbf{m})$, followed by self-consistency equations for fixed points in the extensive limit.

**Model / system.** The system models Dense Associative Memories (DenseAMs) using Ising spins ($\pm 1$) and higher-order polynomial interactions. The analysis compares results across different interaction orders ($k=2, 3, 4$) and includes the Log-Sum-Exponential (LSE) activation model.

**Key observables.** Free energy functional $F(\mathbf{m})$, ground state energy $R(\mathbf{m})$, susceptibility $\chi(m)$, and retrieval thresholds ($\alpha_c$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Interaction order ($k$), disorder parameter ($\gamma$), inverse temperature ($eta$), and the ratio of stored patterns to system size.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on the large $N$ limit, uses approximations like Gaussian distributions for overlaps in the LSE model, and assumes specific forms for the cost function.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the free energy landscape $R(m)$ for both Hopfield and DenseAM ($k=4$) near/below transition points, showing the persistence of the $m=0$ basin. It also shows the LSE model's landscape.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically develops the large deviations framework to find the free energy functional for general polynomial interactions. It then applies this to specific models (Hopfield, LSE) to analyze phase transitions and retrieval dynamics based on the overlap vector $\mathbf{m}$.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Using large deviations theory, we solve and obtain a general expression for the free energy functional for a broad class of associative memories, including dense associative memories. We illustrate the method by reproducing classical results for the Hopfield model. For a finite number of patterns, we derive the temperature-dependent free energy functional for dense associative memories featuring polynomial interactions and Log-Sum-Exponential (LSE) activation. We also evaluate the disorder-averaged ground-state energy of these systems in the extensive limit. Our analytical framework reveals how memory retrieval depends on the initial state in higher-order dense networks, and gives the exact full-retrieval threshold for the LSE model. This method provides a systematic procedure for analyzing diverse, complex architectures in associative memory.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper develops the mathematical framework for multi-species Symmetric Simple Exclusion Processes incorporating reactive particles. By leveraging techniques from integrable systems theory (YBE), the authors prove the model's integrability and derive detailed combinatorial formulas to count the system's stationary state sectors. This work provides deep theoretical insights into non-equilibrium statistical mechanics models with chemical reaction dynamics.

**Why it may be interesting.** The connection between integrable quantum field theory structures (YBE solutions) and non-equilibrium statistical mechanics models like SSEPs is highly relevant to understanding complex many-body dynamics far from equilibrium, which is a key area of modern condensed matter physics.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The research investigates integrable multi-species Symmetric Simple Exclusion Processes (SSEP) that incorporate reactive particle species, generalizing standard SSEPs.

**Main result.** Integrability is proven for the general $(p, q)$-SSEP in the periodic case and for specific boundary conditions in the open case. Furthermore, explicit combinatorial formulas are derived for counting the sectors of the system's stationary state.

**Method.** The authors employ methods rooted in quantum integrable systems theory, specifically using set-theoretical solutions to the Yang-Baxter Equation (YBE) to construct the Markov matrix and prove integrability via transfer matrices.

**Model / system.** The model is the $(p, q)$-SSEP on a 1D lattice, which describes interacting particles where some species are 'reactive' and can undergo pair transformations or evaporation/condensation. The dynamics are governed by a continuous-time Markov process defined on the configuration space.

**Key observables.** Non-equilibrium stationary state (NESS) physical quantities, including mean lattice currents, particle densities, and reaction rates.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The number of singlet species ($p$), the number of reactive pairs ($q$), and the system size $L$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** Integrability is assumed or proven using YBE solutions; calculations for physical quantities are performed assuming specific integrable boundary types.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates particle transformations (e.g., $a^-a^- 	o a^+a^+$), and Figure 2 shows the evaporation-condensation interpretation of the $(p, 1)$-SSEP using empty sites ($	ext{0}$) and impurities ($ar{	ext{0}}$).

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first introducing the generalized model ($(p, q)$-SSEP) and proving its integrability. It then details the analysis of the $(p, 1)$-SSEP, providing physical interpretations (evaporation vs. reaction) and deriving invariants for characterizing sectors in the periodic case before tackling open boundary conditions.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We investigate integrable exclusion Markov processes constructed from set-theoretical solutions of the Yang-Baxter Equation (YBE) that generalise the multi-species Symmetric Simple Exclusion Process (SSEP). We first introduce the process called the $ (p,1) $-SSEP that is analogous to the multi-species SSEP but with an extra particle species qualified as reactive. Reactive species are able to evaporate and condensate by pairs or to transform by pairs depending on the interpretation. We provide a full combinatorial study of the sectors in the periodic case. We prove the integrability of the process in the periodic case and in the open case for two types of boundaries that we introduce using Baxterisations of solutions of the reflection equation. Next we move on to the process called $ (p,q) $-SSEP, i.e. the multi-species SSEP with an arbitrary number of reactive particle species. We prove its integrability in the periodic case and, for the open case, we introduce integrable boundaries generalising the ones considered for the $ (p,1) $-SSEP. Finally, we define physical quantities relevant to the models and we compute them in the non-equilibrium stationary state of the open $ (p,q) $-SSEP for one type of integrable boundaries.

</details>

**Summary.** This work develops a comprehensive field-theoretical framework using the functional renormalization group (fRG) to analyze the phase diagram of 2D Dirac semimetals near a quantum critical point. The study confirms that the system exhibits universal critical behavior belonging to the chiral Ising and 2D Ising universality classes at different temperature limits. It provides crucial insights into how relativistic symmetry can emerge in these strongly interacting electronic systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on condensed matter criticality, the use of advanced non-perturbative RG techniques to map out phase boundaries and emergent symmetries is highly relevant for understanding many-body dynamics in strongly correlated systems studied in AMO physics.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study aims to understand finite-temperature effects and the phase diagram of two-dimensional Dirac semimetals near a quantum critical point (QCP). This requires analyzing the Gross--Neveu--Yukawa model in both semimetallic and insulating regimes.

**Main result.** The authors find that at zero temperature, the QCP exhibits critical exponents matching the chiral Ising universality class, and relativistic symmetry emerges. At finite temperatures, a classical phase transition into the disordered phase belongs to the 2D Ising universality class.

**Method.** The primary method employed is the non-perturbative functional renormalization group (fRG) approach applied to the effective field theory describing the system's dynamics.

**Model / system.** The physical system involves two-dimensional Dirac semimetals, modeled by the Gross--Neveu--Yukawa (GNY) model, which simplifies to the chiral Ising model. The Lagrangian describes interacting fermions coupled to a scalar order parameter field $\phi$.

**Key observables.** Critical exponents ($\alpha$), phase diagram boundaries, quasiparticle weight, and the extent of the quantum critical fan.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Temperature ($T$), coupling constants (e.g., $h\Lambda$), and proximity to the QCP.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on a non-perturbative fRG treatment, which is used to map out the phase diagram across different temperature regimes.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the GNY phase diagram and the Quantum Critical Fan (QCF), with color coding representing the calculated scaling exponent ($\alpha$) as a function of coupling ($h\Lambda$) and temperature ($T$).

**Paper structure.** The paper develops a unified field-theoretical framework by analyzing the system's behavior across different regimes: zero temperature QCP analysis, finite temperature classical transition analysis, and finally determining the scaling properties within the quantum critical fan.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Two-dimensional Dirac semimetals near a quantum critical point can be described by Gross--Neveu--Yukawa models. In view of recent experimental advances exhibiting a transition from Dirac semimetal to insulator in highly-tunable van-der-Waals heterostructures, a better understanding of finite-temperature effects is mandatory. Here, we study the Gross--Neveu--Yukawa phase diagram of the chiral Ising model with a non-perturbative field-theory approach at zero and finite temperature, both in the semimetallic phase and in the insulating phase with spontaneously broken $\mathbb{Z}_2$ symmetry. At zero temperature, we find a quantum critical point with critical exponents that are close to the ones of the chiral Ising universality class, and show that relativistic symmetry is emergent close to the quantum critical point. At finite temperature, the ordered phase survives up to a finite critical temperature, at which we observe a classical phase transition into the disordered phase. We confirm that this transition lies in the two-dimensional Ising universality class. Finally, we determine the extent and scaling properties of the quantum critical fan, and the behavior of the quasiparticle weight, therein. In summary, we present a unified field-theoretical framework for the phase diagram of the chiral Ising model in the surroundings of its quantum critical point.

</details>

**Authors:** Shehbaz Tariq, Muhammad Talha, Arshid Ali, Muhammad Diyan, Symeon Chatzinotas

**Summary.** This survey establishes a unified system model for Quantum Reservoir Computing (QRC), detailing the necessary components from input encoding to classical readout across diverse hardware platforms. It critically analyzes that computational power depends on the entire processing chain, not just Hilbert space size. The work aims to define rigorous benchmarking standards needed to substantiate claims of quantum advantage in this emerging field.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a rigorous framework for analyzing how physical implementations of quantum dynamics can be mapped onto machine learning tasks, which is highly relevant for understanding resource requirements in open quantum systems modeling.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Quantum Reservoir Computing (QRC) aims to leverage quantum dynamics for feature extraction from time-series data in machine learning, while overcoming limitations related to hardware constraints and establishing clear benchmarks.

**Main result.** The paper develops a comprehensive system model connecting all components of QRC—from input encoding through reservoir evolution to classical readout—and provides a framework for evaluating true quantum advantage.

**Method.** It proposes a hybrid computational approach where the quantum processor (QPU) handles the non-linear dynamics, and classical high-performance computing (HPC) manages preprocessing, optimization, and final readout.

**Model / system.** The system is modeled through distinct stages: input encoding ($ho_{t,0} = E(\dots)$), reservoir evolution ($ho_{t,v} = R(\dots)$), measurement ($z_t$), and classical readout. Platforms examined include spin, photonic, superconducting, bosonic, and neutral atom systems.

**Key observables.** Feature vector $z_t$ (from measurement) and prediction $b_{yt}$ (from readout); memory, nonlinearity, and expressivity are key diagnostic properties.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Fixed or selected parameters ($\lambda_p, \lambda_e, \lambda_H$) governing preprocessing, encoding, and dynamics; finite sampling rates and hardware noise levels are critical constraints.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The computational power depends jointly on the input encoding, quantum evolution, observables, measurement, and readout, not just Hilbert space dimension. Current results do not guarantee a broad quantum advantage over classical methods.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 outlines the survey's organization across foundations to benchmarking; Figure 2 illustrates the integrated QRC stack connecting classical preprocessing, reservoir dynamics, measurement, and classical readout.

**Paper structure.** The paper organizes QRC by developing a common system model that connects all processing stages. It then surveys computational properties, discusses various reservoir architectures (spin, photonic, etc.), outlines operating protocols, and addresses benchmarking standards to evaluate quantum advantage claims.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Quantum reservoir computing (QRC) uses the dynamics of a fixed or weakly tuned quantum system to transform temporal and sequential inputs into measured features, while training is typically confined to a classical readout. This separation reduces reliance on repeated quantum parameter updates and avoids the barren plateaus associated with variational circuit training. Its computational power is often attributed to the exponentially large Hilbert space of the quantum system. However, the memory, nonlinearity, and expressivity that determine what a reservoir can actually compute depend jointly on the input encoding, quantum evolution, observables, measurement, and readout, not on Hilbert space dimension alone. On hardware, these capabilities are further constrained by finite sampling, hardware noise, measurement backaction, and the cost of estimating observables, so a large state space alone does not guarantee useful computation. In this survey, we develop a common system model that connects these components and use it to organize QRC foundations, computational properties, reservoir architectures, operating protocols, and physical implementations. We examine spin, photonic, superconducting, bosonic, neutral atom, and other analog platforms, together with applications, software and high performance computing support, benchmarking, and reproducibility. The analysis distinguishes hardware demonstrations from simulations and identifies the assumptions and resources that govern comparisons across implementations. Current results do not establish a broad quantum advantage over well matched classical reservoirs. We therefore specify the resource accounting, benchmark standards, and theoretical criteria needed to evaluate claims of quantum advantage.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper presents a novel method to reconstruct unknown coupling strengths within networked dynamical systems using only one snapshot of node measurements. By assuming the system operates near a relative equilibrium state, the complex inverse problem is linearized into an algebraic system solvable via linear algebra techniques. This breakthrough shows that spatial structure alone can reveal hidden interaction parameters previously requiring time-series data.

**Why it may be interesting.** The technique provides a powerful tool for analyzing complex dynamics where time-series data is unavailable, making it highly relevant for characterizing hidden interactions in physical or biological networks modeled by differential equations.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The core problem is inferring hidden, class-based coupling strengths within a complex network from only a single snapshot of the system's node states.

**Main result.** The authors demonstrate that this inference is possible if the system is observed near a relative equilibrium, allowing spatial heterogeneity to substitute for necessary temporal sampling.

**Method.** They transform the inverse problem into a homogeneous linear system by exploiting the common velocity shared by all nodes at relative equilibrium. This allows parameter recovery using Total Least Squares.

**Model / system.** The study analyzes dynamical systems on networks, including linear consensus dynamics and nonlinear models like Kuramoto oscillators. The dynamics are governed by coupling functions $f$ between classes of nodes.

**Key observables.** Reconstruction error (Frobenius norm) for couplings ($\| \hat{W}^* - W^* \|_F$) and the standard deviation of node velocities ($	ext{std}(\dot{\mathbf{x}})$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The relative equilibrium state, class-specific biases ($b_z$), coupling function $f$, and network topology (ER, BA, SBM).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The system must be observed close to a relative equilibrium ($\dot{\mathbf{x}} = k\mathbf{1}$), which linearizes the otherwise nonlinear inverse problem. The recovered parameters are only determined up to an unknown scale and additive shift.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate successful recovery of couplings using Total Least Squares on Kuramoto dynamics, show rank sufficiency across different network topologies (ER, BA, SBM), and demonstrate information loss if class biases are absent.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the limitations of prior methods requiring time-resolved data. It then introduces the relative equilibrium assumption to linearize the problem into a homogeneous system solvable via Total Least Squares. Validation is performed on multiple dynamical systems, comparing reconstruction errors across different network topologies and assessing necessary conditions like class biases.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Many real-world systems can be modelled as complex networks whose collective behaviour is governed by hidden interactions between nodes. Existing methods for inferring these interactions typically require controlled perturbations, time-resolved observations or multiple independent snapshots, all of which are often unavailable in practice. Here we show that class-based coupling strengths can be inferred from a single snapshot of node states when the system is observed close to a relative equilibrium. In this regime, all nodes share a common velocity, which can be absorbed into an effective class bias, transforming the inverse problem into a homogeneous linear system. The coefficients of this linear system are determined entirely by the observed local neighbourhoods and their coupling mechanism, enabling the application to arbitrary known coupling functions. We validate the approach on three different linear and nonlinear dynamical systems, recovering relative class-based couplings and, in special cases, absolute couplings. These results show that spatial heterogeneity can substitute for temporal sampling, enabling single-snapshot inference of hidden coupling strengths in networked dynamical systems.

</details>

**Summary.** The authors introduce the Al1-center defect as a breakthrough material platform for quantum photonics in silicon. Through rigorous optical and magnetic characterization, they prove it acts as a bright, high-purity single-photon source operating at telecom wavelengths. This establishes the Al1 center as a promising solid-state spin-photon interface for integrated quantum network applications.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses the hardware requirements for scalable quantum networks by demonstrating a robust, integrated solid-state source of single photons with controllable spin states in silicon, which is crucial for photonic quantum information processing.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The use of silicon for quantum photonics is limited by the lack of bright, telecom-band emitters with optically addressable spin states suitable for building integrated quantum networks.

**Main result.** The Al1-center defect in silicon was demonstrated to be a highly efficient, bright single-photon emitter operating in the telecom band, exhibiting high purity and narrow spectral linewidths.

**Method.** Characterization involved time-resolved photoluminescence spectroscopy, second-order correlation measurements ($g^{(2)}(0)$), and magneto-optical spectroscopy on isolated defects within silicon nanophotonic waveguides.

**Model / system.** The physical system is the Al1-center defect (an aluminum–carbon complex) embedded in isotopically purified silicon-on-insulator nanophotonics. It functions as a solid-state quantum emitter with addressable spin states, allowing for initialization and readout.

**Key observables.** Single-photon purity ($g^{(2)}(0)=0.04$), excited-state lifetime ($\sim 135 	ext{ ns}$), homogeneous linewidth ($\sim 47 	ext{ MHz}$), and Zeeman splitting via magneto-optical measurements.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Telecom S-band emission, $	ext{C}_{2	ext{v}}$ symmetry, fast decay rates compared to T-centers, and measurable spin-dependent transitions.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The defect structure is stable and its optical properties are dominated by the Al1 center; comparisons are made against established centers like the T-center.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the PL spectrum showing characteristic emission peaks ($	ext{(Al1)}_0$), time-resolved decay curves, $g^{(2)}(	au)$ measurements confirming single photons, and magneto-optical spectra revealing spin splitting.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from identifying the limitation in silicon photonics to proposing the Al1 center. It then presents detailed characterization using multiple spectroscopic techniques (time-resolved PL, $g^{(2)}$, magnetospectroscopy) to confirm its high performance as a quantum emitter and spin qubit platform.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Silicon is an attractive host for scalable quantum photonics, but the absence of bright telecom-band emitters with optically addressable spin states has limited its use for spin-photon interfaces. Here we demonstrate the Al1-center, an aluminum--carbon defect in silicon, as a bright waveguide-integrated single-photon emitter with a ground-state spin. Using isotopically purified silicon-on-insulator nanophotonic devices, we isolate individual Al1-centers and observe high-purity single-photon emission with $g^{(2)}(0)=0.04$ without background subtraction. Time-resolved photoluminescence spectroscopy reveals a fast excited-state lifetime of 135 ns, nearly an order of magnitude shorter than the benchmark provided by the well-studied T-center. Resonant photoluminescence excitation measurements further resolve the zero-phonon transition and reveal a narrow homogeneous linewidth reaching 47 MHz, threefold narrower than the T-center under comparable temperature. Through magneto-optical spectroscopy, we resolve the spin-dependent transitions of the bound-exciton manifold and achieve spin-selective optical pumping, fulfilling the prerequisite for quantum state initialization and readout. These results establish the Al1-center as a bright telecom-band spin-photon interface in silicon photonics and introduce a promising platform for integrated quantum networks.

</details>

**Summary.** This work theoretically explores exotic electronic phases in a parabolic Kagome semimetal using sophisticated methods like FRG and SBMF. It demonstrates that the competition between band structure effects, repulsive interactions, and quantum geometry leads to multiple unconventional orders, such as loop-current and altermagnetic states. The findings establish this class of semimetals as a rich platform for studying symmetry breaking in correlated electron systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on condensed matter magnetism/superconductivity, the use of advanced many-body renormalization group techniques (FRG) and slave-boson formalisms provides methodological insights applicable to strongly interacting quantum systems studied in AMO physics.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates exotic electronic order in a parabolic Kagome semimetal by mapping its phase diagram across varying interaction strengths.

**Main result.** The interplay of band kinematics, quantum geometry, and interactions dictates the emergence of unconventional orders, including loop-current order and spontaneous altermagnetism.

**Method.** The research employs advanced theoretical tools like Functional Renormalization Group (FRG) for weak/intermediate coupling and Slave-Boson Mean-Field (SBMF) techniques for strong coupling regimes.

**Model / system.** The system is modeled using a generalized Kagome Hubbard model Hamiltonian, tuned to a semimetallic regime at 2/3 filling with onsite ($U$) and nearest-neighbor ($V$) repulsive interactions.

**Key observables.** Loop-current order (LCO), spontaneous altermagnetism, spin-loop-current order (SLCO), and various susceptibilities ($\chi$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Filling $
u = 2/3$, onsite repulsion $U$, nearest-neighbor repulsion $V$, and the hopping parameter $t'$ which controls band structure.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The FRG analysis utilizes approximations such as discarding frequency dependencies of two-particle vertices, while SBMF methods are limited in describing certain bond orders independently.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the non-interacting band structure at $
u=2/3$. Figure 2 presents an FRG phase diagram mapping out various instabilities (SDW, SC, LCO, SLCO) based on $t'$ and interaction strengths. Figure 3 details diagrams showing how interactions promote specific loop current orders.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically analyzes the system using FRG for intermediate coupling to identify initial instabilities, then extends the analysis into strong coupling regimes using SBMF techniques to characterize the full hierarchy of electronic orders.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We study an interacting kagome-lattice realization of a quadratic band-touching semimetal at 2/3 filling with onsite and nearest-neighbor repulsive interactions. Combining functional renormalization group and slave-boson approaches, we map its phase diagram from intermediate to strong coupling and uncover a hierarchy of unconventional electronic orders. The leading instabilities comprise loop-current order, spontaneous altermagnetism arising from a spin-Pomeranchuk instability, and spin-loop-current order with distinctive and largely unexplored properties. We demonstrate how the interplay of band kinematics, electronic interactions, and quantum geometry governs the selection of these phases. Our findings establish quadratic band-touching semimetals as a promising platform for unconventional symmetry breaking and suggest analogous phenomena in other parabolic semimetals.

</details>

**Summary.** The authors developed a unified theoretical framework to compare various thermodynamic efficiencies—including those related to energy conversion and fluctuation suppression—for the kinesin molecular motor. By applying this model to analyze performance under different conditions, they conclude that while multiple metrics can be calculated, kinesin does not appear strongly optimized for maximizing these specific thermodynamic efficiencies.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work applies advanced non-equilibrium statistical mechanics tools (like generalized fluctuation theorems) to a complex biological motor, providing quantitative benchmarks for energy transduction efficiency in mesoscopic systems.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To systematically compare multiple thermodynamic efficiencies characterizing kinesin's performance, as previous studies used different models and assumptions, making quantitative comparisons difficult.

**Main result.** The calculated thermodynamic efficiencies generally remain low, suggesting that kinesin is not optimized for maximizing the considered energy conversion or fluctuation suppression metrics.

**Method.** Analytical derivation of four types of thermodynamic efficiencies (thermodynamic, information-thermodynamic, TUR, and bipartite TUR) using a thermodynamically consistent two-state model framework.

**Model / system.** The system is Kinesin, a molecular motor transporting cargo along microtubules. The dynamics are modeled by a master equation describing the joint probability of kinesin's internal state, position, and the cargo's spatial degree of freedom.

**Key observables.** Thermodynamic efficiency ($\eta$), Information-thermodynamic efficiency ($\eta^{	ext{info}}$), TUR efficiency ($\eta_{	ext{TUR}}$), and Bipartite TUR efficiency ($\eta_{BTUR}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Force (F), ATP concentration (low vs. high), Temperature (T).

**Assumptions / limitations.** A thermodynamically consistent two-state kinesin–cargo model is used, assuming a separation of time scales between the motor and cargo degrees of freedom.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the force dependence of various efficiencies (TUR and bipartite TUR) for different currents under low-ATP conditions, comparing these to energy conversion metrics.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes a unified framework by deriving analytical expressions for multiple efficiencies. It then calculates how these efficiencies vary with external forces and ATP concentrations, confirming consistency across different regimes.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Kinesin is a molecular motor that transports intracellular cargoes along microtubules. Recent studies have quantified kinesin performance using various efficiencies within the framework of stochastic thermodynamics; however, quantitative comparisons remain difficult because different models and assumptions have been employed. As a result, it remains unclear which aspect of kinesin performance, if any, is thermodynamically optimized. Here, we systematically compare multiple thermodynamic efficiencies within a single kinesin-cargo model. To this end, we construct a thermodynamically consistent two-state kinesin-cargo model that retains both the discrete stepping of kinesin and its coupling to the cargo. Assuming a separation of time scales between the motor and the cargo, we derive analytical expressions for the thermodynamic efficiency, the information-thermodynamic efficiency, the thermodynamic uncertainty relation (TUR) efficiency, and the bipartite TUR efficiency, and compare them with numerical simulation results. We find that these efficiencies generally remain low, suggesting that kinesin is not optimized for maximizing the thermodynamic efficiencies considered here. Our results suggest that thermodynamic efficiencies alone may not fully characterize kinesin performance and motivate further investigation of complementary kinetic perspectives for assessing molecular motor function.

</details>

**Summary.** This work pioneers the use of Gaussian Boson Sampling (GBS) to cluster assets within financial portfolios for statistical arbitrage. By mapping correlation matrices into photonic graph structures, the authors propose quantum algorithms that outperform classical methods during volatile market conditions. It establishes a novel quantum heuristic framework linking quantum optics to quantitative finance.

**Why it may be interesting.** While applied to finance, the core mechanism involves simulating complex many-body interactions (photon interference) via GBS to solve a combinatorial optimization problem (graph clustering), which is relevant to understanding resource limitations in quantum simulation for structured data analysis.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper applies Gaussian Boson Sampling (GBS) to solve the Max-Agree Correlation Clustering Problem (MACCP) for constructing robust Statistical Arbitrage (StatArb) portfolios.

**Main result.** Quantum clustering methods using GBS generate superior alpha during high volatility periods compared to classical algorithms, establishing a quantum foundation for quantitative finance applications.

**Method.** The authors map asset correlation data into adjacency matrices and use specialized quantum algorithms (GBS Boost, GBS Roots) that leverage GBS sampling to iteratively find optimal dense subgraphs representing co-moving assets.

**Model / system.** The physical model is based on Gaussian Boson Sampling using squeezed vacuum states interfering in a linear interferometer. The process simulates the measurement of photon statistics proportional to the hafnian of the correlation submatrix.

**Key observables.** Alpha generation, Sharpe Ratio (ShR), Sortino Ratio (SoR), Weighted Density (WD), and Jaccard similarity across rolling time windows.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Universe size ($N$), photon loss factor ($\eta$), required sample budget $H = O(N \log N)$, and the correlation matrix derived from S&P 500 residuals.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary assumption is that maximizing intra-cluster correlations (MACCP) directly translates to profitable arbitrage signals. Furthermore, quantum advantage demonstrations are heuristic, not complexity-theoretic proofs.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the GBS-driven StatArb framework, showing data processing, iterative clustering procedures (GBS Roots), and performance comparisons across varying stock universes ($N$).

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the problem by linking graph clustering to StatArb. It details mapping correlation matrices to GBS inputs, presents quantum algorithms like GBS Roots for MACCP, benchmarks these against classical methods using financial metrics, and analyzes scaling limits.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Gaussian Boson Sampling (GBS) provides a native photonic quantum heuristic for sampling dense subgraphs from adjacency matrices, offering a scalable physical approach to combinatorial graph search problems. Simultaneously, correlation matrix clustering algorithms, such as Spectral and SPONGE, have established robust benchmarks for identifying co-moving assets from correlation matrices in statistical arbitrage (StatArb) strategies. In this work, we map S&P 500 residual correlation data into GBS-compatible adjacency matrices. We benchmark those classical clustering algorithms against two quantum clustering algorithms, GBS Boost and our novel GBS Roots, to construct dynamic, market-neutral portfolios over a rolling one-year window. Simulations across distinct macroeconomic regimes reveal that quantum clustering generates superior alpha within large stock universes during periods of high volatility, effectively isolating structural market idiosyncrasies. Crucially, this economic advantage persists under simulated low-loss conditions and extends into high-loss regimes via the application of coherent displacement to compensate for photon loss. Our findings underscore the efficacy of GBS-derived graph clustering in constructing robust StatArb portfolios, establishing a quantum foundation for broader quantitative finance applications.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper introduces an advanced quantum sensing technique for measuring weak, low-frequency electric fields using Rydberg atoms. By employing a modulated auxiliary AC field and leveraging Quantum Frequency Mixing (QFM), the method generates a measurable cross-term Stark shift read out via EIT. This approach significantly improves stability and sensitivity compared to older methods, establishing a robust platform for next-generation quantum sensors.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work is highly relevant as it adapts established quantum optics techniques (QFM) from RF/MW regimes into the challenging low-frequency electric field domain using Rydberg atoms, providing a stable alternative to traditional DC biasing methods.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To develop a quantum sensing scheme for low-frequency (LF) electric signals that overcomes the inherent instability and calibration issues of conventional DC bias field methods.

**Main result.** The authors demonstrated high sensitivity ($7.5 \pm 2.6 \, \mu	ext{V}/(	ext{cm} \cdot 	ext{Hz}^{1/2})$ at $5 \, 	ext{kHz}$) and achieved a minimal detectable field of $0.26 \pm 0.04 \, \mu	ext{V}/	ext{cm}$ after long integration times.

**Method.** The scheme uses Quantum Frequency Mixing (QFM) by modulating an auxiliary AC field ($	ext{E}_{	ext{aux}}$), allowing the incoming LF signal ($	ext{E}_{	ext{sig}}$) to mix with it. This shift is then read out via Electromagnetically Induced Transparency (EIT).

**Model / system.** The physical platform is Rydberg atoms in an atomic vapor cell, utilizing a second-order Stark shift induced by the total electric field ($	ext{E}_{	ext{tot}}$). The detection relies on mapping the resulting energy shift onto the probe laser intensity via EIT.

**Key observables.** Transmitted light intensity $I(t)$, which is related to the cross-term Stark shift $\delta_{	ext{cr}}$, and the reconstructed signal amplitude $|Z|$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Sensitivity of $7.5 \pm 2.6 \, \mu	ext{V}/(	ext{cm} \cdot 	ext{Hz}^{1/2})$ at $5 \, 	ext{kHz}$; minimal detectable field $\propto T^{-0.48}$ with integration time $T$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The method assumes the cross-term ($\delta_{	ext{cr}}$) dominates and is linearly proportional to the signal field, allowing for robust reconstruction.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the experimental setup, the principle of modulated auxiliary fields and QFM, and the signal reconstruction process. Figure 2 details the verification of the cross-term using combined fields and the subsequent signal reconstruction method.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the problem with conventional methods, proposes the AC modulation/QFM scheme, details the theoretical modeling (cross-term generation), presents experimental validation for LF signals, extends the concept to multi-frequency analysis via Maximal Ratio Combining (MRC), and concludes by establishing a robust framework.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Rydberg atoms have emerged as a versatile and efficient platform for high-sensitivity quantum sensing of free-space electric fields, with remarkable progress in detecting low-frequency signals. To date, low-frequency Rydberg receivers have relied on a constant bias field, typically realized via intra-cell electrodes or Rydberg plasmas generated by photoelectric effects or inter-atomic interactions. While these approaches improve sensitivity, they suffer from inherent challenges in calibration, long-term stability, and robustness, hindering practical deployment. Here, we propose, design, and experimentally demonstrate a quantum sensing scheme for low-frequency electric signals using modulated auxiliary fields in Rydberg atoms. Unlike conventional methods that employ external DC electric fields that are often fully shielded by adsorbed atom layers on the cell walls, we introduce an AC-field modulation strategy. The incoming low-frequency signal mixes with the auxiliary field, and together they induce Stark shifts of the Rydberg level. These shifts are mapped onto the probe laser via electromagnetically induced transparency (EIT), in a manner analogous to heterodyne detection. We demonstrate a sensitivity of $7.5 \pm 2.6~\mathrm{μV/(cm\cdot Hz^{1/2})}$ at 5 kHz and a minimal detectable field of $0.26 \pm 0.04~\mathrm{μV/cm}$ with an integration time of 1000 s. Furthermore, we extend this approach to systematically analyze the performance of generalized auxiliary fields containing multiple frequency components. By virtue of modulated auxiliary field and quantum frequency mixing, our results establish a robust and systematic framework for quantum sensing of low-frequency electric fields with Rydberg atoms, offering improved sensitivity, stability, and immunity to environmental drifts.

</details>
