The sign change sits within the polar-phonon scale window (nodes at 30, 71, and 102 meV for the three densities; dotted lines mark the LO poles); short colored vertical ticks mark EF (solid) and !pl...</figcaption>
</figure>
</div>

**Summary.** This work develops a sophisticated theoretical framework to model unconventional superconductivity in doped SrTiO3. It shows that standard theories fail when carrier density is low, necessitating a fully self-consistent GW treatment incorporating phonon renormalization. The key finding pinpoints the pairing mechanism as being governed by incipient ferroelectricity and full momentum dependence.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on solid-state superconductivity, the rigorous treatment of strong electron-phonon coupling and the necessity of full momentum/frequency dependence mirrors challenges in modeling open quantum systems where simple approximations fail near critical points.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study addresses superconductivity in doped SrTiO3 when the Fermi energy is below the polar LO phonon scale, a regime where conventional Migdal-Eliashberg theory assumptions break down.

**Main result.** The pairing mechanism is identified as the Fröhlich phonon interaction screened by incipient ferroelectricity. Full self-consistent calculations are necessary to accurately predict the superconducting transition temperature ($T_c$).

**Method.** The authors solve the finite-temperature GW equations with full momentum and frequency dependence, solving a linearized pairing eigenvalue problem.

**Model / system.** The system is doped SrTiO3 modeled by a single isotropic parabolic conduction band interacting via Coulomb repulsion and coupling to three polar lattice vibrations. The analysis uses effective masses and phonon dielectric functions derived from ab initio calculations.

**Key observables.** Pairing-onset temperature ($T_c$), pairing channel, renormalization factor ($Z$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Carrier density (especially the dilute limit $n 	o 0$), Fermi energy relative to LO phonon scale, and self-consistency level ($	ext{G}_0	ext{W}_0$ vs. $	ext{scGW}$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary approximations involve comparing different levels of self-consistency ($G_0W_0$, $GW_0$, fully self-consistent GW) and assuming a single isotropic parabolic band model.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops the theory by first identifying the failure points of existing models in the low-density regime, then systematically comparing results across different levels of theoretical approximation (self-consistency), leading to the final identification of the dominant pairing interaction.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Superconductivity in doped SrTiO$_3$ occurs over a wide range of carrier densities, including those for which the Fermi energy is below the polar longitudinal optical phonon scale. In this regime, the assumptions underpinning conventional implementations of Migdal-Eliashberg theory, including frequency cutoffs at the phonon scale and a Coulomb pseudopotential $μ^\ast$, are not valid. We solve the finite-temperature $GW$ equations with full momentum and frequency dependence, without cutoffs or $μ^\ast$, for polar one-band models of SrTiO$_3$, using effective masses and three-phonon dielectric functions parameterized from ab initio calculations. Comparing different self-consistency levels, namely $G_0W_0$, $GW_0$, and fully self-consistent $GW$, we find that the one-shot ($G_0W_0$) kernel overestimates the pairing-onset temperature by one to two orders of magnitude. The dominant suppression comes from replacing $G_0$ by $G$, thereby incorporating the phonon renormalization factor in the electron Green function. Using the self-consistently computed interaction $W$ further lowers and narrows the pairing-onset dome. In the dilute limit, our calculations identify the pairing channel as the Fröhlich phonon interaction screened by the incipient ferroelectricity of the material, with plasmonic and electronic screening effects negligible. The numerical solution of the full equations reveals a pairing-onset scale that remains non-zero as the density tends to zero, whereas Fermi-surface projection or Fermi-energy frequency truncation removes it. This work highlights the relevance of incipient ferroelectricity, the importance of self-consistency, and the need for a full momentum- and frequency-dependent treatment in modeling superconductivity in SrTiO$_3$-like doped polar semiconductors.

</details>

**Summary.** This work uses NQR/NMR measurements on CeRh2As2 to characterize its magnetic state. The findings reveal a complex magnetic structure involving both A-type antiferromagnetism and an incommensurate modulation. Crucially, the study establishes a strong link between this magnetic ordering and the superconducting phase, suggesting that magnetism plays a pivotal role in the material's electronic properties.

**Why it may be interesting.** The strong coupling between magnetism and superconductivity, particularly the observation that the onset of static AFM order coincides with or immediately follows the superconducting transition, is a key topic in strongly correlated electron physics relevant to unconventional superconductors.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates the nature of magnetic order (A-type Antiferromagnetism) and its interplay with superconductivity in the heavy-fermion superconductor CeRh2As2.

**Main result.** NQR studies reveal that the magnetic structure is a combination of a Q=0 A-type AFM component overlaid by a two-dimensional incommensurate modulation. The close proximity of the AFM transition temperature (TN) to the superconducting transition temperature (TSC) is highlighted.

**Method.** The research employs high-quality single-crystal measurements using Nuclear Quadrupole Resonance (NQR) and Nuclear Magnetic Resonance (NMR) spectroscopy on the As site.

**Model / system.** The physical system is CeRh2As2, a heavy-fermion superconductor with a noncentrosymmetric crystal structure. The magnetic state is modeled as having both a Q=0 A-type AFM component and an incommensurate modulation.

**Key observables.** NQR spectral splitting (at As(2)), NQR intensity evolution, internal magnetic field strength (H_int), and the temperature dependence of these quantities near T0 and TN.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Superconducting transition temperature (T_SC), Antiferromagnetic transition temperature (T_N), and fluctuation onset temperature (T_0).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes that the observed magnetic transitions are robust features of the material, though early-stage sample inhomogeneity is noted as a limitation.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare NQR spectra between different crystal quality samples; others show the temperature evolution of spectral features and internal fields across multiple transition points (T0, TN, TSC).

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by characterizing the superconducting phases, then analyzing the magnetic transitions using NQR/NMR data. It establishes the coexistence of incommensurate modulation on top of A-type AFM order, linking these magnetic features to the SC state.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We performed $^{75}$As nuclear quadrupole resonance (NQR) and nuclear magnetic resonance (NMR) measurements on a higher-quality single-crystalline CeRh$_2$As$_2$, a heavy-fermion superconductor exhibiting multiple superconducting (SC) phases under magnetic fields along the $c$ axis. This SC multiphase is believed to originate from staggered Rashba spin-orbit coupling associated with locally broken inversion symmetry. In addition to superconductivity, CeRh$_2$As$_2$ exhibits phase I below $T_0\sim0.5$ K and an antiferromagnetic (AFM) state below $T_{N}\sim 0.25$ K in the early-stage samples. In the higher-quality sample, the AFM transition becomes more pronounced, and $T_{N}$ increases to nearly coincide with $T_{SC}$. The NQR spectra at the As(1) site imply an internal field with an incommensurate distribution, indicating a two-dimensional incommensurate modulation of the magnetic structure superimposed on a $Q=0$ A-type AFM component. Moreover, a pronounced decrease in the NQR intensity at $T_0$ well-above $T_{N}$ and an abrupt increase in the internal field at $T_{N}$ suggest the emergence of a slowly fluctuating AFM order at $T_0$ which becomes static at $T_{N}$.

</details>

**Summary.** This experimental work synthesizes and characterizes $	ext{Mn}_{1-x}	ext{Ge}_x	ext{Sb}_2	ext{Te}_4$ solid solutions to study the interplay between magnetism and topology. By systematically varying Ge content, the researchers mapped out phase equilibria and observed complex magnetic transitions, including spin-flop behavior. These findings are crucial for engineering novel magnetic topological materials.

**Why it may be interesting.** While primarily condensed matter magnetism/materials science, the study of competing magnetic orderings, field-induced transitions (spin-flop), and topological insulators touches upon complex many-body ground states relevant to correlated electron physics.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates the interplay between magnetism and electronic topology by examining how substituting Germanium (Ge) into the $	ext{MnSb}_2	ext{Te}_4$ lattice affects its magnetic and structural properties.

**Main result.** Ge substitution successfully creates single-phase solid solutions, leading to complex magnetic behavior characterized by multiple transitions, including spin-flop transitions, whose dependence on Ge concentration is systematically mapped out.

**Method.** The research employed a combination of high-temperature solid-state synthesis followed by comprehensive characterization using SQUID magnetometry, PXRD, and DTA.

**Model / system.** The physical system is the $	ext{MnSb}_2	ext{Te}_4-	ext{GeSb}_2	ext{Te}_4$ solid solution series. The structure is layered (rhombohedral), and the study focuses on how substituting $	ext{Mn}^{2+}$ with $	ext{Ge}^{2+}$ modifies magnetic coupling within this lattice.

**Key observables.** Saturation magnetization, effective magnetic moment, temperature-dependent magnetic transitions ($	ext{PM} 	o 	ext{FIM} 	o 	ext{FM}$), and the presence of spin-flop transitions.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Ge substitution concentration ($x$), Temperature (2 K to 300 K), Applied Magnetic Field (up to $\pm7$ T).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The structural integrity is maintained across the full composition range, allowing for the study of magnetic dilution effects.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate temperature profiles during synthesis and magnetization curves showing distinct transitions and spin-flop features as a function of Ge content.

**Paper structure.** The paper follows a logical progression: first establishing phase equilibria via thermal analysis (DTA) and structure confirmation (PXRD); second, characterizing the magnetic response using SQUID magnetometry across varying compositions; finally, interpreting these results to map out the competition between different magnetic ordering types.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

As a sister compound of the antiferromagnetic topological insulator MnBi2Te4, MnSb2Te4 is also a candidate for exotic magnetic topological phases. On the other hand, the structurally analogous but nonmagnetic phase-change material GeSb2Te4 is also known to exhibit nontrivial band topology. Motivated by their shared crystal structure, the similar ionic radii of Mn2+ and Ge2+, and the opportunity to explore the interplay between magnetism and topology, here we investigate the effects of Ge substitution at Mn sites in MnSb2Te4. The Mn1-xGexSb2Te4 solid solutions were synthesized via high-temperature solid-state reaction and characterized for composition, structure, phase behavior, and magnetism using SEM-EDS, PXRD, DTA, and SQUID. Ge substitution was successful across the full composition range, producing homogeneous, single-phase samples that melt via peritectic reactions, as confirmed by the MnSb2Te4GeS-b2Te4 phase diagram. Ge substitution strengthens the sample's paramagnetism, but with ferrimagnetic ordering up to x = 0.75, with both effective moment and saturation magnetization decreasing with increasing Ge content. Two distinct magnetic transitions - high-temperature paramagnetic to ferrimagnetic and low-temperature ferrimagnetic to ferromagnetic - were identified, with a dome-like shape dependence of the low-temperature magnetic transition on Ge substitution. A negative magnetization was observed in the pristine MnSb2Te4 and x = 0.12 substituted samples, while two distinct spin-flop transitions appeared in the samples with x = 0.32 and x = 0.55 Ge substitutions as a result of competing magnetic orderings. These findings facilitate future selective single-crystal growth of homogeneous, magnetic phases, paving the way for magneto-transport and topological surface states investigations.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper theoretically investigates the pure spin photovoltaic effect in an altermagnetic topological insulator heterostructure. By developing a non-Abelian theory, the authors show that specific magnetic symmetries can suppress charge currents entirely, leading to a purely spin-generating response. This establishes the material as a versatile platform for controlling nonlinear spin transport using light.

**Why it may be interesting.** The work provides a detailed theoretical framework linking magnetic order parameters (Néel vector) to specific quantized electronic responses (pure spin photocurrents) in topological materials, which is highly relevant for spintronics and quantum optics applications.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates the Pure Spin Bulk Photovoltaic Effect (BPVE) in a complex magnetic heterostructure to understand spin current generation without accompanying charge currents.

**Main result.** The system realizes an intrinsically pure spin photovoltaic effect when the Néel vector is in the $xy$-plane, generating dc spin currents controllable by light polarization and magnetic symmetry.

**Method.** The authors develop a non-Abelian formulation of the spin BPVE, extending theory to PT-degenerate manifolds, and analyze response tensors using symmetry constraints.

**Model / system.** The model is a PT-symmetric 2D heterostructure combining a topological insulator with a d-wave altermagnet. The Hamiltonian incorporates SOC and magnetic exchange fields, leading to FOTI or SOTI phases depending on the Néel vector orientation.

**Key observables.** Pure spin photocurrent ($\sigma_{s,\mu;
u\lambda}$), charge photocurrent, spin shift current (driven by linear polarization), and spin injection current (driven by circular polarization).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Orientation of the Néel vector ($\hat{n}_s$), local Dirac mass sign change, and symmetry protection ($C_{4z}T$ vs $C_{2z}$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The theory extends conventional BPVE to PT-degenerate manifolds using a non-Abelian formalism; approximations are made regarding the spin-current operator's applicability in nonlinear optical processes.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 schematically shows optically induced spin transport components based on Néel vector orientation. Figure 2 illustrates energy spectra showing FOTI and SOTI topological phases under open boundary conditions.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by defining the physical system (TI/Altermagnet heterostructure), developing the generalized non-Abelian theory for spin BPVE, analyzing how symmetry dictates the pure spin response in different topological regimes (SOTI vs FOTI), and concluding with the platform's potential for controlling spin currents.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We investigate the bulk photovoltaic effect (BPVE) in a $PT$-symmetric two-dimensional heterostructure consisting of a topological insulator coupled to a $d$-wave altermagnet. To describe the symmetry-enforced degenerate bands of this system, we develop a non-Abelian formulation of the spin BPVE, extending the conventional theory from isolated nondegenerate bands to $PT$-degenerate manifolds. We show that the orientation of the Néel vector controls both the topological phase and the character of the nonlinear optical response. When the Néel vector lies in the $xy$-plane, the heterostructure realizes a second-order topological insulator (SOTI) protected by $C_{4z}T$ symmetry. The system also retains $C_{2z}$ symmetry, which completely suppresses second-order charge photocurrents while allowing finite spin photocurrents. As a result, the BPVE becomes an intrinsically pure spin photovoltaic effect, generating a dc spin current without an accompanying charge current. We find that linearly polarized light drives a spin shift current, whereas circularly polarized light generates a spin injection current. Both responses undergo a sign reversal whenever the local Dirac mass changes sign. As the Néel vector is rotated toward the $z$-axis, the SOTI phase transforms into a first-order topological insulating phase, leading to the coexistence of charge and spin photocurrents. Our results establish $PT$-symmetric altermagnetic topological-insulator heterostructures as a versatile platform for generating and controlling pure spin photocurrents and reveal nonlinear spin transport as a sensitive probe of topology and magnetic symmetry.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper addresses the critical issue of 'subspace trapping' in optimizing Neural Quantum States (NQS) for many-body physics. The authors introduce Annealed Gradient Descent (AGD), a novel, sampling-aware optimization technique. AGD stabilizes training by artificially boosting the gradient contribution from rarely sampled, but physically important, configurations.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a crucial algorithmic fix for modern quantum simulation techniques, ensuring that the computational power of expressive neural networks is not undermined by statistical limitations inherent in Monte Carlo sampling.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Neural Quantum States (NQS) optimization suffers from 'subspace trapping' due to finite-sample instability, where important low-probability configurations are rarely sampled and receive insufficient gradient feedback.

**Main result.** The proposed Annealed Gradient Descent (AGD) method successfully suppresses metastable trapping, allowing simple NQS architectures to achieve chemical accuracy across molecular and spin model benchmarks.

**Method.** AGD is a sampling-aware update rule that temporarily reweights the influence of low-probability configurations during optimization, guiding the search away from restricted subspaces.

**Model / system.** The work applies to general quantum many-body systems, including molecular electronic structure calculations (using Gaussian basis sets) and quantum spin models like the 1D and 2D J1-J2 Heisenberg model.

**Key observables.** Variational energy expectation value ($\langle \hat{H} angle$), local energy ($E_{	ext{loc}}(\sigma)$), and convergence diagnostics showing trapping above the true ground state energy.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The annealing factor, learning rates (e.g., $\eta$, decay schedules), and system-specific coupling constants ($J_1/J_2$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** Standard gradient estimation methods fail when sampling coverage is insufficient; the authors derive enhanced gradient forms using logarithmic weight functions to correct for this bias.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate subspace trapping convergence (GD vs. AGD), showing AGD forces exploration toward lower energy states, and compare different weighting schemes ($w(p)=p$ vs $w(p)=-p \log p$).

**Paper structure.** The paper first identifies the theoretical failure mode (subspace trapping) in VMC/NQS optimization. It then develops AGD as a sampling-aware update rule, deriving enhanced gradient estimators that incorporate low-probability information before benchmarking on molecular and spin systems.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Neural quantum states offer expressive representations of quantum many-body wave functions, yet their practical accuracy can be limited by stochastic optimization rather than representational capacity. Here we identify a finite-sample instability, termed subspace trapping, in which physically important configurations become strongly underestimated, remain absent from successive sampling batches and receive insufficient gradient feedback. This self-reinforcing loss of sampled support can confine optimization to an effective subspace and produce apparently stationary states above the true ground state energy. To address this problem, we introduce annealed gradient descent (AGD), a sampling-aware update with annealing factor that temporarily increases the relative contribution of sampled low-probability configurations while limiting the dominance of high-probability ones. We establish the connection between finite-sample support loss and effective subspace optimization, and then evaluate the method across molecular systems, one and two-dimensional $J_1$-$J_2$ models. Annealed gradient descent suppresses metastable trapping, preserves physically relevant configurations and enables compact neural quantum states to attain chemical accuracy and competitive state-of-the-art performance. These results establish AGD as a lightweight complement to expressive neural architectures, improved sampling strategies for scalable quantum many-body optimization.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper develops a rigorous theoretical framework to determine the best possible error rate for distinguishing between enantiomer pairs using quantum light emitted from chiral molecules. By comparing optimal quantum measurement strategies against classical ones, the authors demonstrate that collecting photons collectively provides significant, potentially orders-of-magnitude, improvements in discrimination accuracy. This has implications for developing highly sensitive chiroptical sensors.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses quantum metrology applied to molecular properties (chirality), providing quantitative benchmarks for how entanglement or collective measurements can surpass classical limits in sensing fundamental physical symmetries.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To quantitatively establish error bounds for classifying the handedness (chirality) of a quantum optical emitter by discriminating between enantiomer pairs.

**Main result.** Optimal collective measurements on $N$ photons can provide an orders-of-magnitude improvement in the achievable error rate compared to separable measurement strategies.

**Method.** The problem is framed as a quantum symmetric binary hypothesis test, comparing error bounds derived from Helstrom and Chernoff information for different measurement schemes.

**Model / system.** The system involves chiroptical emission, modeled using density matrices ($ar{ho}_\pm$) describing the emitted photon state, which depends on dipole moments and polarization contributions.

**Key observables.** Dissymmetry factor ($G$), minimum error probability ($P_e^{\min}$), Quantum Chernoff Information ($D_{QC}$), and Classical Chernoff Information ($D_{CC}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The number of photons ($N$), the dissymmetry factor ($G^2$), and system parameters like $\epsilon_\mu, \epsilon_m, \epsilon_Q$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes a narrow spectral bandwidth (e.g., using an etalon) and focuses on emission/collection processes.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the ratio of quantum to classical Chernoff information for different collection geometries (single vs. opposite sides), demonstrating the advantage scaling with $N$.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops the theoretical framework by defining the chiral state, applying hypothesis testing bounds, and comparing the asymptotic error scaling ($e^{-ND}$) for various measurement types.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Discriminating enantiomer pairs is of central importance in the molecular sciences. The preferential interaction between chiral light and matter is commonly employed in this discrimination, despite fundamental challenges. Here we present quantitative error bounds for classifying the handedness of a chiral, randomly oriented, quantum optical emitter from the perspective of classical and quantum hypothesis testing. Most interestingly, we find that a collective measurement on $N$ photons can provide an orders-of-magnitude improvement in error rate relative to a corresponding separable measurement.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper provides a critical analysis of Adlam's attempt to reconcile quantum theory with 'local friendliness' theorems using Quantum Reference Frames. The authors demonstrate that Adlam's resolution requires three non-standard modifications to physics, suggesting QRF formalism alone is insufficient. Ultimately, the work concludes that standard quantum mechanics cannot evade these fundamental no-go theorems.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work is highly relevant for theoretical physicists working on quantum foundations, open quantum systems, or relativistic quantum information, as it tackles foundational issues regarding measurement contextuality and observer dependence in a rigorous mathematical framework.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper critically assesses Adlam's proposal attempting to resolve tensions between quantum theory and 'local friendliness' (LF) theorems arising from Extended Wigner's Friend Scenarios (EWFS). It aims to show that standard Quantum Reference Frame (QRF) formalism cannot evade these no-go theorems.

**Main result.** The apparent resolution offered by Adlam relies on three independent modifications—definite observer degrees of freedom, spin-orientation entanglement with the lab frame, and altered superobserver recording protocols—rather than solely QRF principles. Within standard quantum theory, QRFs do not evade EWFS no-go theorems.

**Method.** The authors employ critical assessment, comparing Adlam's modified mechanics (AMQM) against standard QM predictions using formalisms involving relational observables and group averaging techniques derived from QRF theory.

**Model / system.** The core model is the Wigner's Friend scenario (EWFS) involving entangled bipartite quantum systems (spin-1/2 particles), analyzed through the lens of Quantum Reference Frames (QRFs). The analysis uses abstract mathematical tools like group representations and projection operators to define frame-independent observables.

**Key observables.** Local Friendliness (LF) inequalities, spin outcomes ($AI$, $AE$), lab orientations ($AR$), and relational observables defined via reduction/relativisation maps.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The structure depends on the underlying symmetry group G used for frame transformations; specific calculations involve time evolution parameters like $\pi/(2g)$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The critique assumes standard quantum theory holds, and it highlights that Adlam's proposal rests on misconceptions regarding QRFs and EWFS protocols.

**Figures summary.** Not specified in the notes; however, the structure implies comparisons between expectation values calculated under different theoretical frameworks (QM vs AMQM) for Bell-type tests.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically critiques Adlam's proposal by first defining the tension (LF theorems), then detailing Adlam's three modifications, and finally using advanced QRF formalism (including appendices on recoupling theory and relational observables) to demonstrate that these modifications are necessary but insufficient to resolve the fundamental conflict within standard QM.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Recent no-go theorems based on extended Wigner's Friend scenarios (EWFS) reveal a deep tension between the universal validity of quantum theory and local friendliness (LF), the conjunction of three natural assumptions: the absoluteness of observed events, locality, and no-superdeterminism. In a recent work, Adlam argues that this tension can be dissolved by appealing to quantum reference frames (QRFs). We present and critically assess her proposal. We show that her proposed resolution does not arise from QRFs, but instead relies on three independent modifications: certain degrees of freedom are always definite, observers can flip upon measuring spins, and the outcomes the superobservers record and use to test the inequalities are not their friends' observed results. The proposal and the arguments for the plausibility of these modifications rest on several misconceptions about QRFs and EWFS, which we rectify. We conclude that, within standard quantum theory, quantum reference frames do not evade the EWFS no-go theorems.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper simulates the dissociation of H2+ driven by strong orthogonal laser fields to understand ultrafast quantum dynamics. Key findings include the emergence of an additional high-energy proton peak, which is controllable via the relative phase between the field components. This demonstrates that the spatial geometry of multi-dimensional laser fields offers a powerful new degree of freedom for controlling molecular fragmentation.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses non-equilibrium dynamics in molecular physics using intense laser fields, providing detailed insights into how field geometry (spatial/temporal) acts as a control knob for electron-nuclear energy partitioning and fragmentation pathways.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates the quantum dynamics of H2+ dissociative ionization when driven by strong, orthogonal laser fields.

**Main result.** Orthogonal fields introduce a high-energy proton kinetic-energy-release (KER) peak ($\approx 4-5$ eV) absent in single-color calculations, and allow coherent control over this yield via the relative carrier-envelope phase.

**Method.** The research employs full-dimensional quantum simulations using time-dependent methods to calculate Joint Energy Spectra (JES) for dissociative ionization.

**Model / system.** The system is the hydrogen molecular ion (H2+), modeled with a Hamiltonian including electronic, nuclear, and electron-proton interaction terms. The dynamics are driven by intense laser fields composed of orthogonal components ($\vec{E}(t) = E_z(t)\hat{z} + E_x(t)\hat{x}$).

**Key observables.** Proton Kinetic-Energy-Release (KER) spectrum, electron–proton energy-sharing pattern, and fragment angular distributions.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Laser field geometry (orthogonal components), relative carrier-envelope phase ($\phi$), and pulse duration/frequency (e.g., 800-nm, cos^8 pulses).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The simulation uses the tSurff method, which assumes neglect of particle interactions beyond large radii; full-dimensional simulations are computationally demanding.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare JES under linear vs. circular polarization, show the dependence of a secondary KER peak yield on the relative phase ($\phi$), and illustrate how angular distributions rotate with field phase for short pulses.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops the theoretical framework using full-dimensional quantum simulations to model H2+ ionization. It then presents results comparing single-color vs. orthogonal fields, highlighting the secondary peak generation, and finally discusses the control mechanisms afforded by varying the relative phase of the perpendicular field component.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We present full-dimensional quantum simulations of $H_2^+$ dissociative ionization driven by strong orthogonal laser fields. We consider equal-frequency orthogonal components, which generate elliptical or circular polarization depending on their relative phase and amplitude, as well as orthogonal $800$- and $400$-nm two-color fields. These two-dimensional fields strongly modify the fragmentation dynamics. Most notably, we identify a high-energy peak in the proton kinetic-energy-release (KER) spectrum at approximately $4-5$ eV that is absent from the corresponding single-color, linearly polarized calculations. The yield of this peak can be coherently controlled by varying the relative carrier-envelope phase of the perpendicular field component. The perpendicular field also disrupts the clear electron-proton energy-sharing pattern observed in the main $3-3.5$ eV dissociation channel, indicating more complex multichannel dynamics. Time-dependent state projections and calculations initiated from individual excited states attribute the additional peak to laser-induced vibrational excitation of $H_2^+$. Furthermore, the perpendicular field rotates the fragment angular distributions, causing the most probable proton and electron emission directions to deviate substantially from the principal $z$ axis. These findings demonstrate that the spatial and temporal geometry of orthogonal laser fields provides an additional degree of freedom for controlling ultrafast electron-nuclear dynamics.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical paper investigates what specific properties of entanglement are responsible for exponential speedups in quantum machine learning. By analyzing Pauli-channel and conjugate-state learning tasks, the authors prove that violating a condition called the 'reduction criterion' is necessary to maintain such an exponential advantage. This work provides a precise resource constraint characterizing quantum computational power.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on quantum learning theory, the rigorous identification of necessary resource constraints (like RC violation) provides fundamental mathematical tools applicable to characterizing entanglement utility in complex physical processes beyond simple channel estimation.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper aims to determine which specific properties of entangled quantum resources are necessary for achieving an exponential advantage in quantum learning tasks.

**Main result.** Violation of the 'reduction criterion' (RC) is identified as a necessary condition for realizing an exponential sample-complexity advantage in Pauli-channel and conjugate-state learning tasks.

**Method.** The authors use information-theoretic tools, including conditional min-entropy and Total Variation Distance bounds derived from hypothesis testing, to establish lower bounds on the required number of samples (sample complexity).

**Model / system.** The study analyzes quantum channel estimation/learning tasks using $n$-qubit Pauli channels ($	ext{P}_n$) and conjugate-state learning involving joint measurements on $ho \otimes ho^*$. The resource restriction is formalized by the reduction criterion (RC).

**Key observables.** Sample complexity ($N$), Total Variation Distance ($	ext{TVD}$), conditional min-entropy, and entanglement measures.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The dimension $n$ of the system, the error tolerance $\epsilon$, and a parameter $\eta$ quantifying the allowed violation of the reduction criterion.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes that restricting input states or measurement effects to satisfy the RC is equivalent to losing the exponential advantage in the considered learning tasks.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate resource hierarchies (SEP, PPT, RC) for Pauli-channel learning and compare sample complexity bounds between unrestricted vs. RC-restricted joint measurements in conjugate-state learning.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically analyzes three scenarios: 1) Incoherent adaptive Pauli-channel learning under RC restrictions; 2) One-sided coherent adaptive protocols under RC restrictions; and 3) Copywise adaptive conjugate-state learning, establishing the necessary role of violating RC in each case.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

While entanglement is known to enable exponential improvements in the sample complexity of quantum learning, it remains unclear which properties of entangled resources are responsible for such improvements. We address this question through the reduction criterion, a condition obeyed by all bound-entangled states. In $n$-qubit Pauli-channel learning, we show that restricting either the input states or the measurement effects to satisfy this criterion rules out an exponential advantage for incoherent adaptive protocols. An exponential lower bound persists for the one-sided coherent adaptive protocols considered here, even when the unrestricted side retains quantum correlations across channel uses. Using conditional min-entropy, we further quantify how the sample-complexity lower bounds weaken as larger violations of the reduction criterion are allowed. Finally, we show that the same obstruction appears in conjugate-state learning: restricted joint measurements cannot reproduce the logarithmic-sample advantage of unrestricted joint measurements on $ρ\otimesρ^*$. These results identify violation of the reduction criterion as a necessary condition for an exponential advantage in the learning tasks considered here.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper introduces Q-Cliff, a novel Hamiltonian derived Clifford transformation, to improve the simulation of molecular ground states. This technique efficiently maps complex fermionic Hamiltonians onto qubit circuits while significantly reducing entanglement. The resulting methods provide state-of-the-art performance in terms of energy accuracy and circuit resource requirements for variational quantum algorithms.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses resource estimation and circuit optimization for chemistry simulations, providing a potentially superior ansatz construction method compared to standard VQE approaches, which is highly relevant for near-term quantum hardware.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Developing efficient quantum algorithms for simulating ground states of chemical systems by refining techniques based on the Qubit Coupled Cluster (QCC) algorithm.

**Main result.** The Hamiltonian derived Clifford transformation (Q-Cliff) generates highly efficient, low-depth circuits that achieve energy approximations comparable to state-of-the-art VQE methods while significantly reducing qubit entanglement.

**Method.** The core methodology involves using Q-Cliff to deterministically transform the Hamiltonian into a form amenable to variational quantum circuit construction, yielding an efficient classical and quantum approximation scheme.

**Model / system.** The work focuses on simulating ground states of quantum chemical systems (e.g., N2, LiH) by mapping fermionic Hamiltonians onto qubit representations.

**Key observables.** Energy accuracy (approaching MP2 to CISD), reduction in entanglement entropy between qubits, and circuit metrics like CNOT count and depth compared to existing VQE algorithms.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The scaling of the variational method is $O(N^6)$; performance is benchmarked against state-of-the-art ADAPT-VQE methods.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The quality of initial state preparation is crucial for achieving quantum advantage; the process involves viewing the wavefunction approximation as a rank-1 CP decomposition in qubit space.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare QMF and Q-Cliff derived energies against MP2 for molecules like N2, show entanglement entropy reduction via DMRG calculations, and illustrate the circuit structure used for VQE benchmarking across several molecules.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces Q-Cliff to refine QCC techniques, details the Hamiltonian transformation process using Pauli words and Clifford tableaux, presents benchmarks on molecular systems (N2, LiH) comparing energy accuracy and circuit efficiency against existing quantum algorithms, and concludes by outlining future directions for combining this method with DMRG.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Recently (Physica Scripta, 100(10):105401, 2025), an algorithm was introduced that deterministically generates a Clifford transformation from the Qubit Coupled Cluster (QCC) algorithm which we call Q-Cliff (QCC+Clifford). There, it was shown that Q-Cliff could be utilized to generate a hardware efficient version of the QCC ansatz. Here, we examine and refine these techniques and show that Q-Cliff can be utilized to generate efficient classical and quantum approximations to the ground states of chemical systems. The algorithm generates an efficient variational method that generally has accuracy between MP2 and CISD with $O(N^6)$. Furthermore, we show through DMRG calculations that the entanglement between qubits is reduced significantly and therefore the accuracy for a given bond dimension can be vastly improved (up to an order of magnitude). Finally, we refine the previously reported algorithm to generate low-depth and CNOT efficient circuits that can be optimized with a comparable number of energy evaluations to state-of-the-art VQE algorithms. All these results show that this Hamiltonian derived Clifford transformation should be a tool used for many classical and quantum algorithms.

</details>

**Summary.** This work develops a theoretical framework linking advanced concepts in discrete probability distributions—such as infinite divisibility and scalability—to the statistical mechanics of small systems. By applying these mathematical tools, the authors model phase transitions and derive equations of state for interacting gases. It provides a powerful combinatorial approach to understanding critical phenomena beyond standard assumptions.

**Why it may be interesting.** While the focus is on classical statistical mechanics, the rigorous mathematical treatment of distribution properties (like infinite divisibility and scaling) has deep parallels with quantum many-body theories involving correlation functions or reduced density matrices.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates the mathematical connections between discrete probability distributions and the statistical mechanics description of small, interacting systems.

**Main result.** It establishes that the particle-number distribution at a critical point converges to the Discrete Stable distribution, and it extends these concepts to non-extensive thermodynamics while maintaining scalability properties.

**Method.** The analysis heavily relies on probability generating functions (p.g.f.) to develop theories for infinitely divisible and scalable distributions, applying these tools within the grand canonical ensemble framework.

**Model / system.** The study focuses on the particle-number distribution $p_k$ in small systems, modeling phenomena like liquid-vapor phase transitions using kinetic models and analyzing equations of state (e.g., van der Waals).

**Key observables.** Particle-number distribution ($p_k$), Equation of State ($eta p$ vs $ho$), Virial coefficients, and the Discrete Stable distribution.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Critical point parameters ($ho_c, T_c$), temperature ratios ($	au = T/T_c$), and non-extensivity parameter $q$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis often assumes infinite divisibility in the grand canonical ensemble, though it also explores regimes where this fails (e.g., Tsallis thermodynamics).

**Figures summary.** Not specified.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from developing general mathematical tools using p.g.f.s for discrete distributions to applying these tools to construct equations of state and analyze phase transitions in statistical mechanics.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We study connections between discrete probability distributions and the statistical mechanics of small systems. Using probability generating functions, we develop the theory of power series, infinitely divisible, and scalable distributions of non-negative integer-valued random variables, and introduce the class of Markovian distributions that arise naturally in stationary solutions of birth-death processes and in scalable infinitely divisible distributions.   These results are applied to the grand canonical ensemble description in statistical mechanics: the infinite divisibility leads to a quasiparticle picture of an interacting gas, and the virial expansion is linked to the combinants of the distribution. A kinetic model of the liquid-vapor phase transition is presented, in which the particle-number distribution at the critical point converges to the Discrete Stable distribution - the fixed point of a renormalization semi-group transformation. We also show that the scalability of the particle-number distribution is preserved in Tsallis non-extensive thermodynamics, even though infinite divisibility fails. Moreover, deformed discrete distributions, including the negative binomial as a q-deformation of the Poisson, arise naturally in this setting.

</details>

**Summary.** This work designs a novel interactive music system using quantum agents that communicate through quantum teleportation. It treats noise and decoherence not as failures, but as controllable parameters defining the 'interpretative distance' between musical ideas. This frames quantum communication in an artistic context, suggesting new ways to model complex, non-ideal interactions in quantum computation.

**Why it may be interesting.** The focus on interpreting noise/decoherence as controllable, expressive parameters ($\theta$) provides an analogy for studying non-ideal quantum channels or open system dynamics where the 'error' itself carries information about the interaction.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To develop an interactive music system where quantum agents communicate via teleportation, supporting ambiguous interactions akin to free Jazz improvisation.

**Main result.** The paper demonstrates that agent interaction can be modeled as a continuous space of interpretative distance, rather than binary success/failure, by tuning the fidelity of state transfer.

**Method.** It uses quantum teleportation protocols combined with Single Qubit Probability Amplitude Modulation (SQPAM) and Quantum Phase Estimation (QPE) to encode musical features into quantum states.

**Model / system.** The system operates on NISQ-era quantum computers, simulating multi-agent communication where the core mechanism is controlled unitary evolution ($U(	heta, \phi, \lambda)$) mediated by teleportation.

**Key observables.** Melodic correlation, pitch-set distance, state fidelity ($\mathcal{F}$), and Hamming Distance are used to quantify agent interaction quality.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The rotation angle $\theta$ in the interpreter module is crucial for tuning interpretative behavior; NISQ constraints themselves are treated as creative affordances ('quantum whispers').

**Assumptions / limitations.** The system assumes that continuous audio features can be mapped effectively to discrete quantum gate parameters, and it embraces noise rather than treating it solely as an error.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the overall pipeline (Audio $\rightarrow$ Circuit $\rightarrow$ Teleportation $\rightarrow$ Output), single-agent circuits, multi-agent teleportation schemes, and fidelity distributions showing how varying parameters affects state overlap.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from establishing the full quantum agent system pipeline using feature extraction (Chunk 1) to implementing the core teleportation mechanism (Chunk 2), and finally refining the model by introducing a tunable interpreter module to analyze continuous interpretative behavior in multi-agent cascades (Chunks 3 & 4).

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

This paper introduces an interactive music system with quantum musical agents that communicate by teleporting quantum states to one another. Human performers interact in real time with agents whose melodic and rhythmic behaviours are encoded as quantum states using Single Qubit Probability Amplitude Modulation (SQPAM) and structured through Quantum Phase Estimation (QPE). Up to three agents are combined within a single quantum circuit, with directed communication via quantum teleportation. We are interested in supporting ambiguous, transformative interactions reminiscent of free Jazz improvisation. Therefore, rather than treating noise and decoherence as limitations, the system embraces NISQ-era constraints as creative affordances, framing agent communication as quantum whispers, that is, deliberate, musically expressive imperfections in state transfer. We provide demonstrations and analyses based on melodic correlation, pitch-set distance, and state fidelity, where a continuum between imitation and divergence can be observed. We developed a tunable interpretation method to assess how agents reinterpret teleported states. This work positions teleportation as a promising interaction mechanism for agent-based quantum computer music and outlines future directions toward distributed ensembles connected via the Quantum Internet.

</details>

**Summary.** The research demonstrates the successful incorporation and spectroscopic characterization of perylene molecular emitters into hexagonal boron nitride (hBN) stacks. By analyzing narrow zero-phonon-line transitions and vibronic spectra at cryogenic temperatures, the authors confirm that while hBN offers a promising platform, molecular binding is critically dependent on structural defects rather than pristine lattice sites. This provides key guidelines for deterministically engineering such quantum emitter devices.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses the integration challenge for solid-state quantum emitters by demonstrating high-fidelity optical characterization within a 2D van der Waals heterostructure, which is crucial for realizing scalable quantum devices.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To investigate the feasibility of directly embedding narrow-linewidth molecular quantum emitters (perylene) into van der Waals hexagonal boron nitride (hBN) stacks and characterizing their photophysical properties.

**Main result.** The study successfully observed gigahertz-narrow zero-phonon-line transitions, confirming that while pristine hBN tends to expel molecules, morphological defects stabilize the binding. The spectral analysis provides insights into the host-molecule interaction mechanism.

**Method.** A combination of cryogenic optical spectroscopy (measuring ZPL and vibronic spectra) and advanced theoretical calculations (quantum chemistry/DFT) was employed for analysis.

**Model / system.** The physical system is perylene molecules embedded within multi-layered hexagonal boron nitride (hBN) stacks, mimicking a solid-state quantum emitter platform. The analysis involves modeling excited-state relaxation and vibrational coupling between the molecule and the host lattice.

**Key observables.** Zero-phonon-line (ZPL) transition linewidth ($\approx 2.8\ 	ext{GHz}$), vibronic spectral peaks, phonon sideband shapes, and spatial mapping of emission intensity.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Cryogenic temperatures (e.g., 3.8 K); the electronic structure parameters derived from quantum chemistry calculations; defect concentrations in hBN.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes that spectral matching techniques like LNCC can reliably identify molecular fingerprints even when broadened by the host environment, and that environmental stability is governed by surface defects rather than bulk properties.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate sample fabrication, compare fluorescence spectra from hBN vs. anthracene hosts showing vibronic peak agreement but sideband differences, and map ZPL wavelengths across samples to show spatial heterogeneity.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from initial characterization of the ZPL transition in the hBN stack, through detailed spectral comparisons with reference molecules (anthracene), to advanced techniques like LNCC matching for site-specific analysis, concluding with structural insights regarding molecular binding sites.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Single polycyclic aromatic hydrocarbon molecules embedded in organic matrices have proven to be an excellent family of narrow-linewidth quantum emitters. Extending this host-guest setting to van der Waals materials offers the opportunity to combine the preeminent properties of molecular emitters with the access to the versatility of two-dimensional hetero-structures and devices. In this work, we incorporate perylene molecules into multi-layered hexagonal boron nitride stacks and observe gigahertz-narrow zero-phonon-line transitions at cryogenic temperatures. We unambiguously verify the origins of photon emission through vibronic spectra analysis. By combining hyperspectral localization measurements with quantum chemistry calculations, we examine the insertion mechanisms of perylene molecules in the hexagonal boron nitride stacks, and conclude that pristine hBN layers tend to expel molecules from the sandwich, while extended morphological defects, hydroxyl groups and unpassivated boron and nitrogen atoms assist to stabilize molecular bindings to hBN. Our work provides valuable insight for future work to deterministically integrate narrow-linewidth molecular emitters into van der Waals devices.

</details>

**Summary.** This work characterizes a family of topological surface bands in 3D TCIs featuring symmetry-protected cubic band touching. By tuning parameters related to particle-hole asymmetry, the system's dispersion can be continuously morphed between several distinct forms. This tunability allows for precise engineering of electronic properties, particularly the density of states singularities, which is crucial for realizing strongly correlated quantum phases.

**Why it may be interesting.** The explicit connection between crystal symmetries, topological band degeneracies (cubic touching), and the resulting tunable density of states makes this highly relevant for designing novel correlated electronic phases in solid-state systems.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study proposes a class of topological surface bands in 3D Topological Crystalline Insulators (TCIs) that exhibit symmetry-protected cubic-order band touching.

**Main result.** The research demonstrates that the band dispersion can be continuously tuned between cubic, moat, and multi-mini-valley structures by adjusting particle-hole asymmetry and anisotropy. This results in a versatile platform for engineering tunable density-of-state singularities ranging from power-law to logarithmic divergences.

**Method.** The analysis uses symmetry constraints (T, P, M, $J_z$) applied to a k.p Hamiltonian derived from a prism-lattice tight-binding model to characterize the surface band structure and its degeneracies.

**Model / system.** The physical system is modeled using a bulk tight-binding approach on a prism lattice for angular-momentum-$3/2$ electrons, analyzed via effective 1D models at high-symmetry points ($\Gamma$, M).

**Key observables.** Band dispersion types (cubic, moat, mini-valley), Density of States (DOS) singularity order ($\sim E^{-1/3}$, $\sim \ln E$), and the topological invariant (winding number).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Particle-hole asymmetry parameters ($\alpha, eta$) and anisotropy terms ($t_{R2}$), which control the transition between different band structures.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on assuming specific symmetry representations for T, P, M, and $J_z$, and uses a k.p expansion approximation.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate schematics of various cubic-touching surface bands tuned by parameters $\alpha$ and $eta$; one figure shows the band structure evolution in an xy-slab under symmetry breaking; another details the prism lattice model.

**Paper structure.** The paper proceeds by establishing the general symmetry constraints on the Hamiltonian, deriving effective 1D models at high-symmetry points ($\Gamma$, M), analyzing how specific symmetries protect cubic touching, and finally demonstrating tunability via parameter variation to achieve different DOS singularities.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We propose a class of topological surface bands in three-dimensional topological crystalline insulators that have symmetry-protected cubic-order band touching. Within the symmetry constraint, the band dispersion can continuously vary between cubic dispersion, moat band and multi-mini-valley structure with van Hove singularities by adjusting particle-hole asymmetry and anisotropy. Thus, there is a family of tunable density-of-state singularities ranging from power-law to logarithmic divergences. This offrs a versatile platform for engineering strongly correlated phases of matter in topological surface states. We provide an example realization in a prism-lattice tight-binding model of angular-momentum-3/2 electrons.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper compares the statistical geometries of three types of random quadrangular surfaces (GKNS, DQ, GQ) used as models for 2D quantum gravity. By analyzing observables like Hausdorff dimension and degree distributions, the authors demonstrate that GKNS defines a unique universality class distinct from standard triangulations. This suggests new geometric frameworks applicable to non-critical string theories.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on geometry, the rigorous statistical mechanics approach to characterizing universality classes and critical phenomena in random manifolds has parallels with understanding phase transitions or emergent order in complex quantum many-body systems.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates and compares the statistical geometric properties of three different ensembles of random quadrangular surfaces: GKNS, Dynamical Quadrangulations (DQ), and General Quadrangulations (GQ).

**Main result.** The analysis reveals that while DQ and GQ approach a Hausdorff dimension similar to standard DT ($\Delta_H \approx 4$), the GKNS ensemble defines a distinct class of random geometry with $\Delta_H \approx 2$.

**Method.** The authors use a unified graph-theoretic framework, analyzing underlying Markov chains, degree distributions, and distance statistics for all three ensembles.

**Model / system.** The system consists of discrete surfaces modeled as quadrangulations (random graphs), which are used to study concepts related to 2D quantum gravity and non-critical string theories. The specific models include the GKNS construction and generalizations of dynamical triangulations (DQ/GQ).

**Key observables.** Degree distributions (scale-free vs. exponential decay), Hausdorff dimension ($\Delta_H$), critical exponents ($
u, \gamma$), and scaling relations for surface area versus diameter.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The critical probability ($p_0$) for the GKNS percolation process; the size/area ($S$); and the crossover diameter ($D_0$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes that the different randomization procedures (GKNS, DQ, GQ) define distinct but related ensembles of random surfaces.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate surface representations derived from a disordered potential (ES, SN, dSN, PL), show surface modifications via specific operations ($A_\eta$, $R$), and depict scaling relationships between area and diameter for the different models.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes a unified graph-theoretic framework to relate elementary operations defining the ensembles. It then analyzes percolation properties (GKNS) and Markov chain convergence/scaling laws (DQ/GQ), culminating in a comparison of geometric observables like Hausdorff dimension across all three classes.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We investigate the statistical properties of random quadrangular surfaces generated by different randomization procedures: the Gruzberg-Klümper-Nuding-Sedrakyan (GKNS) construction, and two newly introduced generalizations of dynamical triangulations (DT), dynamical (DQ) and general quadrangulations (GQ). We formulate these surfaces within a unified graph-theoretic framework and establish the relationships between the elementary operations defining the different ensembles. For GKNS surfaces, we demonstrate that the construction is equivalent to two mutually constrained percolation processes and determine the associated critical point and critical exponents, revealing deviations from ordinary percolation. For DQ and GQ, we analyze the underlying Markov chains and determine the scaling of mixing and relaxation times. We further analyze all three ensembles through their degree distributions, degree correlations, distance statistics, and Hausdorff dimensions. While DQ exhibits exponentially decaying degree distributions and geometric properties similar to DT, GKNS and GQ display broad, scale-free degree distributions. Moreover, GKNS surfaces possess an asymptotic Hausdorff dimension $Δ_H\approx 2$, whereas DQ and GQ approach $Δ_H\approx 4$, similarly to DT. This indicates that DQ is compatible with the universal behavior of DT, while GKNS and GQ define distinct classes of random geometry, implying a different underlying measure in the space of random surfaces and a possible geometric framework for a new class of noncritical string theories.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper presents a novel Variational Monte Carlo approach for fermionic ground state estimation using Continuous Normalizing Flows (CNFs). By implementing the flow as a permutation-equivariant neural ODE, it achieves exact sampling from the learned wavefunction distribution without slow MCMC steps. This method demonstrates high accuracy and excellent GPU scaling for simulating interacting quantum particles.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a powerful, scalable alternative to traditional quantum Monte Carlo methods for strongly correlated systems by integrating continuous flow theory with fermionic symmetries, which is highly relevant for simulating complex quantum matter.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The core problem is accurately estimating the ground state energy of fermionic many-body systems, which requires sampling complex, high-dimensional wavefunctions.

**Main result.** The authors achieved ground-state energies for harmonically trapped spinless electrons that surpass established CISD reference values and demonstrated near-ideal strong GPU scaling up to 128 A100 GPUs.

**Method.** They introduce a framework using Continuous Normalizing Flows (CNFs) to refine an antisymmetric base wavefunction via a permutation-equivariant neural ODE, enabling exact sampling without MCMC during training.

**Model / system.** The system involves fermionic particles governed by a general Hamiltonian. The method uses CNFs built upon fixed antisymmetric bases like Slater or Jastrow-Slater determinants, which are refined by learning the flow's vector field.

**Key observables.** Ground state energy ($E_0$), Born distribution ($\Pi_	heta$), and local energy variance (used for convergence diagnostics).

**Important parameters / regimes.** System size $N$ (up to 48 particles), number of dimensions $D$, and training hyperparameters like learning rates and ODE tolerances.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The method assumes the existence of an efficiently sampleable, antisymmetric base wavefunction; it also relies on the mathematical framework of CNFs applied to fermionic symmetries.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 visualizes how a trained CNF refines a base wavefunction (e.g., Slater determinant) into a more correlated target state via ODE trajectories. Figure 9 compares the convergence of CISD reference energies against increasing basis set cutoff ($n_{	ext{max}}$).

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the VMC framework, proposes using CNFs to overcome MCMC sampling limitations by leveraging offline base sample generation. It details novel equivariant vector field architectures (PDS, FVF, PDSG) and an augmented dynamics formulation for efficient kinetic energy calculation.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We introduce a framework for fermionic variational Monte Carlo (VMC) in which a continuous normalizing flow (CNF) refines a fixed antisymmetric base wavefunction. The flow is implemented as a permutation-equivariant neural ODE, a smooth, topology-preserving map that learns correlations not captured by the base; equivariance preserves the antisymmetry of the base, so the flow can in principle improve any antisymmetric ansatz that can be sampled efficiently. We demonstrate this using Slater and Jastrow-Slater bases, though more expressive choices are admissible. Exact samples from the flow's Born distribution are obtained by pushing pre-cached base samples through the forward ODE, requiring no Markov chain Monte Carlo (MCMC) at training time. The base samples are generated offline and reused across training batches and runs, decoupling sample generation from parameter optimization and enabling embarrassingly parallel training across multiple GPUs. We introduce three novel permutation-equivariant vector field architectures: Pairwise Deep Sets (PDS), FermiNet Vector Fields (FVF), and Pairwise Deep Sets Gradient (PDSG), each offering a different balance of expressivity and computational cost. We further introduce an augmented dynamics formulation for kinetic energy computation that co-evolves the required derivative quantities as ODE state variables, eliminating differentiation through the ODE trajectory and yielding significant reductions in wall-clock time and memory. Training runs on systems of harmonically trapped spinless electrons demonstrate ground-state energies below CISD reference values. Scaling experiments demonstrate near-ideal strong scaling from 1 to 128 NVIDIA A100s using 32 GPU nodes of NERSC's Perlmutter supercomputer for systems of up to $N = 48$ particles in three dimensions.

</details>

**Authors:** Ahmed Azaz Humdoon, Cheng Chu, Lei Jiang, Qian Lou, Mengxin Zheng  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** quantum information and computing · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2607.19318v1>

**Summary.** This paper introduces VQE-AdvBench, the first comprehensive benchmark for testing adversarial robustness in Variational Quantum Eigensolver (VQE) algorithms. By systematically applying various attacks—including noise manipulation targeting Zero-Noise Extrapolation—the authors reveal a clear hierarchy of risk. They conclude that manipulating the error mitigation pipeline is the most severe threat to VQE accuracy.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides crucial benchmarking for quantum algorithm security, which is vital for the practical deployment of quantum chemistry and materials science simulations on noisy hardware.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study addresses the security vulnerability of Variational Quantum Eigensolver (VQE) algorithms when deployed in cloud-based services, making them susceptible to adversarial attacks from compromised components or malicious users.

**Main result.** Noise-induced attacks that manipulate the Zero-Noise Extrapolation (ZNE) pipeline are found to be the most damaging, causing up to an 8.84$	imes$ error amplification, surpassing circuit-level backdoors like QTrojan.

**Method.** The authors introduce VQE-AdvBench, a unified red-teaming benchmark that systematically evaluates seven representative attacks (including backdoor and noise variants) across black-, gray-, and white-box access models.

**Model / system.** The primary model is the Variational Quantum Eigensolver (VQE), used to estimate molecular ground-state energies for $	ext{H}_2$ and $	ext{H}_3^+$. The evaluation is performed across five noise-calibrated IBM quantum backends.

**Key observables.** Absolute error ($E_{	ext{abs}} = |E_{	ext{adv}} - E_{	ext{ideal}}|$) and relative error amplification factor compared to the clean baseline.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Molecule ($	ext{H}_2$, $	ext{H}_3^+$), ansatz (Hardware-Efficient Ansatz), five specific noise-calibrated IBM backends, and attack parameters like $\epsilon$ for FGSM/PGD.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The benchmark is scoped to a controlled setting with fixed molecules and ansatz families to ensure a clean comparison of attack severity across different vulnerability types.

**Figures summary.** Table I compares the effectiveness (absolute error and relative amplification) of QTrojan, QDoor, FGSM, and PGD. Table II details the performance of three noise-induced attacks ($	ext{QF}, 	ext{QM}, 	ext{QS}$) on the ZNE pipeline.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the security gap in VQE evaluation by introducing VQE-AdvBench. It then systematically tests various attack types (circuit backdoors, parameter perturbations, noise manipulation) under a unified protocol to quantify their relative severity.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

The Variational Quantum Eigensolver (VQE) is a leading algorithm for estimating molecular ground-state energies on near-term quantum hardware, with applications spanning quantum chemistry, materials science, and drug discovery. As VQE workloads are increasingly deployed through cloud-based ``VQE-as-a-service'' pipelines, they become exposed to adversaries such as compromised service components, malicious co-tenants, or insiders in the transpilation stack, any of which can corrupt results before they reach the user. A range of attacks on variational quantum circuits has been proposed, but each has been studied in isolation: some on quantum classifiers with accuracy-based metrics, others on variational quantum algorithms with energy-error metrics. This lack of a common evaluation setup makes their relative severity difficult to compare and leaves the security of VQE poorly characterized. In this work, we present \textbf{VQE-AdvBench}, the first unified red-teaming benchmark for the Variational Quantum Eigensolver, systematizing these attacks under a single evaluation protocol to rigorously assess VQE's adversarial robustness. We organize attacks along a black-, gray-, and white-box access taxonomy, and evaluate seven representative attack scenarios -- the QTrojan circuit backdoor, the QDoor parameter backdoor, parameter-space adaptations of FGSM and PGD, and three QNBAD noise-induced variants -- over a fixed molecule-ansatz-backend-metric configuration, on H$_2$ and H$_3^+$ across five noise-calibrated IBM backends. Our results reveal a clear severity ordering: noise-induced attacks that manipulate the Zero-Noise Extrapolation (ZNE) pipeline are the most damaging (up to 8.84$\times$ error amplification), followed by the QTrojan circuit-level backdoor (7.52$\times$), while the QDoor parameter-level backdoor is the least effective, yielding only marginal amplification (up to 1.37$\times$).

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical study analyzes the electronic structure of a hypothetical nickelate polymorph, T'-La2NiO4. Using advanced many-body techniques, the authors predict it is a charge-transfer insulator analogous to cuprates. The work details how doping affects carrier localization between oxygen and nickel orbitals, providing new insights into correlated layered materials.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on solids, the methodology of using DMFT/SIC to model correlated insulators with clear orbital differentiation is highly relevant for understanding strongly interacting quantum systems in general.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates charge-transfer physics in the hypothetical T'-La2NiO4 polymorph, drawing parallels with established cuprate superconductors.

**Main result.** The T'-structure is predicted to be a manifest charge-transfer insulator with a cuprate-like gap of ~1.8 eV, exhibiting doping-dependent carrier asymmetry between O(2p) and Ni-d states.

**Method.** First-principles many-body theory calculations incorporating correlation effects via the DFT+SIC-DMFT approach were employed.

**Model / system.** The system is single-layer nickelate oxide, La2NiO4, modeled using a five-orbital Slater-Hamiltonian parameterized by Hubbard U and Hund exchange J. It compares the T' structure to the bulk T structure.

**Key observables.** Charge gap energy, local orbital occupations (Ni 3d), spectral functions (projected vs. local), and magnetic ordering moments.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Correlation strength (U), charge transfer energy (Delta), doping level ($\delta$), and temperature ($T=200$ K).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The use of DFT+SIC-DMFT formalism to capture strong electronic correlations, and the comparison relies on structural analogies with known cuprates.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate crystal structures, band dispersions showing orbital contributions, Fermi surfaces for doped states (hole/electron), and magnetic moments comparing T vs. T' structures.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the physical motivation by comparing to cuprates, details the theoretical framework using DFT+DMFT, presents results on charge-transfer insulating nature and doping asymmetry, and contrasts the magnetic ordering between the two polymorphs (T vs. T').

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

The La$_2$NiO$_4$ compound stabilizes in nature in the so-called T structure with octahedral oxygen coordination of the Ni site. Motivated by a similarly existing polymorph for La$_2$CuO$_4$ in the cuprate system, we here study La$_2$NiO$_4$ in the T$^\prime$-structure with square-planar oxygen coordination of Ni by means of first-principles many-body theory. The hypothetical T$^\prime$-La$_2$NiO$_4$ compound turns out to be a manifest charge-transfer insulator with a cuprate-like charge gap of $\sim 1.8$ eV. Upon doping, a strong carrier asymmetry is revealed, i.e. while holes dominantly enter O$(2p)$-derived states, electrons may become itinerant majorly within effective Ni-$d_{x^2-y^2}$ states. Nearest-neighbor antiferromagnetic ordering at stoichiometry appears absent in the T$^\prime$ structure, in stark contrast to the strong antiferromagnetism in the T structure. Our theoretical study opens up new pathways for correlation physics in layered nickelates with challenging charge-transfer signatures, awaiting experimental inquiries.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical paper rigorously demonstrates that approximating the exact renormalization group flow using a derivative expansion results in a divergent series. However, it establishes that this divergence implies the series is asymptotic, meaning accurate physical predictions can still be extracted by summing up to a specific optimal number of terms before the breakdown occurs.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on QFT methods, understanding the limitations and convergence radii of systematic approximations like derivative expansions is crucial for developing reliable non-perturbative tools applicable in complex many-body systems studied by AMO physicists.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates the convergence properties of applying the derivative expansion technique within the functional renormalization group (ERG) flow equation.

**Main result.** It proves that the derivative expansion is a divergent series in any dimension for both exponential and general smooth cutoffs, but it behaves as an asymptotic series.

**Method.** The authors use perturbative calculations involving one-, two-, and three-loop contributions to analyze how derivatives ($\partial^{2n}$) appear in the effective action approximation.

**Model / system.** The analysis is performed using massless lambda phi^4 theory, a standard model in quantum and statistical field theory studied via FRG techniques.

**Key observables.** Convergence behavior of the derivative expansion series; $eta$-functions and self-energies ($\Gamma(p, -p)$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Spacetime dimension (d); loop order ($\ell$); number of derivatives ($2n$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes the use of an additive IR cutoff function for the FRG flow equation.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops arguments by analyzing loop contributions and comparing convergence rates across different vertex types (2-point, 4-point, etc.) to establish the asymptotic nature of the expansion.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We show that the derivative expansion of the exact (functional) renormalization group is a divergent series in any dimension, both for an exponential cutoff and more general smooth cutoffs. We prove this by showing that within massless $λ\varphi^4$ perturbation theory, such divergences arise first at two loops. From several lines of theoretical argument and by analysing infinite classes of two- and three-loop contributions, we conclude that the derivative expansion is an asymptotic series that initially converges towards the exact result before divergent behaviour takes over.

</details>
