**Summary.** This paper presents significant methodological improvements to calculating lower bounds on the spectral gap of parent Hamiltonians derived from Matrix Product States. By introducing an efficient method to compute local ground space overlaps, the authors provide stronger quantitative bounds that outperform existing techniques. This work is vital for theoretically characterizing the stability and gapped nature of quantum many-body systems modeled by MPS.

**Why it may be interesting.** The rigorous bounding of spectral gaps is crucial for understanding the stability and correlation decay in strongly correlated quantum materials described by tensor network representations.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The central difficulty is deriving rigorous, quantitative lower bounds on the spectral gap of parent Hamiltonians associated with Matrix Product States (MPS).

**Main result.** The authors improve the martingale method by devising an efficient technique to compute the overlap of local ground spaces, yielding stronger quantitative lower bounds for the spectral gap.

**Method.** They revisit and enhance the Fannes-Nachtergaele-Werner martingale method, utilizing a new formalism that transforms the key overlap calculation into a fixed-dimension eigenvalue problem.

**Model / system.** The study focuses on parent Hamiltonians ($H_N$) constructed from MPS tensors. These models are used to characterize quantum many-body systems whose ground states are efficiently represented by MPS.

**Key observables.** Spectral Gap ($\Delta$), Overlap of local ground spaces, Bond Dimension ($D$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Bond Dimension ($D$), Interaction range ($n$), Blocking size/length ($\ell$ or $r$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes the MPS structure is 'well-behaved' and relies on specific properties of normal or block-normal tensors.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first establishing the general difficulty, then detailing the improved martingale method for computing gap bounds, followed by applying this to specific MPS structures (like block-normal) and deriving explicit spectral gap inequalities.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Proving spectral gaps above the ground space is a central problem in quantum many-body physics. Yet, finding lower bounds on the gap is notoriously difficult. We revisit the martingale method, originally developed by Fannes, Nachtergaele, and Werner [Fannes '92, Nachtergaele '96] to prove the existence of spectral gaps of parent Hamiltonians of Matrix Product States (MPS), and provide improvements to the different steps of the method. Most importantly, we devise a new technique which allows to compute the key quantity in the martingale method -- the overlap of local ground spaces -- exactly and efficiently. This enables a clear improvement of the method, allowing it to outperform other existing techniques to lower bound gaps, which we demonstrate by benchmarking on several models. Remarkably, our -- numerically motivated -- approach at the same time also yields a significantly simplified proof of the fact that parent Hamiltonians of any (well-behaved) MPS are always gapped.

</details>

**Type:** theory · **Category:** strongly correlated electrons · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2607.19224v1>

**Summary.** This work investigates the magnetic exchange interactions in NaV2O5 by treating it as a molecular orbital spin system using a distributed moment approach. By calculating couplings via GW band structures, the authors show that oxygen-mediated interactions are vital for accurately predicting the material's critical temperature. The results provide insights into the complex interplay between metal centers and lattice oxygens in determining magnetic ordering.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on solid-state magnetism, the detailed calculation of effective Hamiltonians from first principles and the analysis of collective excitations (spin waves) are relevant for understanding quantum dynamics in strongly correlated systems.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study aims to understand the magnetic exchange interactions within NaV2O5, particularly focusing on the behavior above the charge disproportionation temperature.

**Main result.** The paper finds that exchange interactions involving oxygen sites are crucial, being of a similar magnitude and even larger than direct V-V interactions, which is necessary for correctly estimating the critical Néel temperature.

**Method.** Exchange interactions are calculated using a linear response approach based on quasiparticle-self-consistent GW band structures to derive a classical Heisenberg type Hamiltonian.

**Model / system.** The system is NaV2O5, treated as a molecular orbital spin system where magnetism arises from doping into the V2O5 conduction band. The analysis employs a distributed moment approach assigning spins to individual atomic sites, including oxygen atoms.

**Key observables.** Magnetic moments ($\mu_B$), exchange interactions ($J_{a,b}$), mean-field Néel temperature ($T_{	ext{N}}^{	ext{MF}}$), and spin wave spectra (including high-energy optic modes).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Critical Temperature ($\sim 320 \pm 50 	ext{ K}$), magnetic moments on V, O$_v$, and O$_b$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The use of a classical Heisenberg model is assumed based on adiabatic decoupling; the analysis focuses on the high-temperature regime above charge disproportionation.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the electronic band structure, the crystal structure with spin ordering and labeled exchange interactions, and the dependence of exchange interactions on distance.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from establishing the physical model (distributed moment view) to calculating exchange couplings using GW methods. It then derives a Heisenberg Hamiltonian and uses it to predict magnetic properties like $T_{	ext{N}}$ and spin wave excitations.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

The magnetism in NaV2O5 results from doping of the narrow split-off conduction band of V2O5, which becomes half-filled and leads to a Mott-insulating splitting of spin resolved bands with anti-ferromagnetic order of the spins along the zigzag chains. In the Pmmn structure the spin of this S = 1/2 system is equally shared between two vanadium atoms, residing in a molecular orbital type state. While below 34 K a charge disproportionation occurs into V4+ and V5+, the situation above this temperature is less clear and amounts to a fluctuating moment with equal probability of occupancy of each V. While traditionally described as a quarter-filled ladder system with electron spin localized on the rungs of the ladder, we here take a distributed moment approach in terms of the spin density lumped into individual atomic magnetic sites, including the small induced moments on the oxygen atoms. Exchange interactions are calculated between these sites using a linear response approach based on quasiparticle-self-consistent GW band structures. Surprisingly we find the exchange interactions between the small magnetic moments induced on the vanadyl and bridge oxygen sites to be of the same order of magnitude and even larger than the exchange interactions between vanadium atoms. Their role in the critical temperature is found to be crucial. The spin wave spectra obtained from this classical Heisenberg type Hamiltonian extracted from first-principles contains unusual optic spin wave type collective excitations of high energy.

</details>

**Summary.** This work develops an analytical framework to estimate error thresholds in quantum error correction codes and Bayesian inference problems. It achieves this by projecting critical points from clean models onto their disordered Nishimori counterparts using Fourier-Walsh techniques. The resulting formulas provide accurate, closed-form estimates for a wide range of stabilizer codes, offering deep theoretical insight into noise resilience.

**Why it may be interesting.** The connection between quantum error correction thresholds (a physical problem) and the analytical tools of disordered statistical mechanics (like Nishimori points) provides a powerful theoretical bridge for understanding fault tolerance limits.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper aims to provide analytical estimates for error thresholds in quantum error correction and Bayesian inference problems.

**Main result.** A Fourier-Walsh projection scheme is introduced that yields closed-form estimates for these thresholds, accurately reproducing known numerical results for various disordered models like Ising and $\mathbb{Z}_q$ codes.

**Method.** The core method involves using a minimal replica theory approach combined with a Fourier-Walsh projection to map the critical point of a clean statistical mechanics model onto its Born-disordered Nishimori critical point.

**Model / system.** The analysis applies to quantum error correction codes, specifically $\mathbb{Z}_q$ surface codes (mapped to the $q$-state clock model), as well as general disordered spin systems like Ising and Potts models on lattices.

**Key observables.** Error threshold ($\beta_{\text{Nishimori}}^c$), critical temperatures ($T^*_1, T^*_2$), and the satisfaction of the Gilbert-Varshamov self-dual entropy relation.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Measurement precision $\gamma$, Potts variable $q$ (especially $q \ge 5$), and spatial dimension $d$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The method relies on projecting the exact replicated single-bond weight using a minimal replica ansatz, which is highly successful but has limitations for first-order transitions ($q \le 4$).

**Figures summary.** Tables compare projection-based threshold estimates against numerical benchmarks for various disordered models (RBIM, RPGM, Potts, $\mathbb{Z}_q$ clock).

**Paper structure.** The paper develops the analytical framework by first analyzing the general mapping via Fourier-Walsh projection in the context of Bayesian inference. It then applies this scheme systematically to specific lattice models, detailing results for Ising, Potts ($q=3, 4$), and extending to $\mathbb{Z}_q$ clock models, culminating in discussions about multiple BKT transitions.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

In quantum error correction, the error threshold provides essential quantitative guidance for the ability to bring about fault-tolerance through decoding the effects of incoherent noise, weak measurement or inference. However, the numerical value of an error threshold is typically only accessible through large-scale numerical simulations of the underlying noise model. Here we introduce an analytical estimate of error thresholds falling into the Nishimori universality class via a Fourier--Walsh projection scheme that maps the critical point of the underlying disorder-free statistical-mechanics model to the Born-disordered Nishimori critical point. Using a minimal replica theory approach, this closed-form estimate is obtained from a projection of the exact replicated single-bond weight which we find to reproduce (within a percentage point) the known numerical thresholds of random-bond and random-plaquette Ising models / $\mathbb Z_2$ stabilizer codes in spatial dimensions $d=2-5$, and extends to Potts variables with $q\le4$. The main application of our projection scheme is to $\mathbb Z_q$ surface codes, whose decoding problem maps to the disordered $q$-state clock model. For $q\ge5$ the clean clock model has \textit{two} Berezinskii--Kosterlitz--Thouless transitions, which the projection maps to two Nishimori temperatures that bound an intermediate information-critical phase. The resulting threshold values not only accurately agree with recent decohered-$\mathbb Z_q$-toric-code numerics, but are found to satisfy the Gilbert--Varshamov self-dual entropy relation $\ln q \simeq H_q(T_1^\ast)+H_q(T_2^\ast),$ although no duality condition is imposed in the construction. Our approach thereby points to a deeper connection between the clean and Born-disordered models, while allowing for instant analytical estimates of error thresholds for a variety of stabilizer codes.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper introduces a sixteen-state energy mapping technique to extract the crucial four-spin ring exchange coupling ($J_{	ext{ring}}$) from first-principles calculations. By applying this method to $	ext{La}_2	ext{CuO}_4$ and $	ext{SrFeO}_2$, the authors demonstrate that $J_{	ext{ring}}$ is a significant, quantifiable term in the magnetic Hamiltonian for cuprates. This advances our understanding of correlated magnetism by providing a systematic way to measure multi-spin interactions.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a rigorous computational tool to quantify higher-order spin interactions ($J_{	ext{ring}}$) in strongly correlated materials, which are essential for understanding high-temperature superconductivity mechanisms beyond simple Heisenberg models.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To develop a direct, local first-principles method for extracting the four-spin ring exchange coupling ($J_{	ext{ring}}$), which is crucial for understanding cuprates and other square-lattice magnets.

**Main result.** The sixteen-state energy mapping successfully isolates $J_{	ext{ring}}$, showing quantitative agreement on $	ext{La}_2	ext{CuO}_4$ ($J_{	ext{ring}}/J_1 = 0.25$) while providing a negative control using $	ext{SrFeO}_2$ ($J_{	ext{ring}}/J = 0.006$).

**Method.** The authors generalize the four-state method to a sixteen-state scheme by calculating total energies for all collinear spin configurations on a plaquette, allowing $J_{	ext{ring}}$ to be isolated via weighted summation.

**Model / system.** The study applies to correlated magnetic insulators like $	ext{La}_2	ext{CuO}_4$ and $	ext{SrFeO}_2$, modeling interactions within a nearest-neighbor plaquette using an effective Hubbard model Hamiltonian.

**Key observables.** $J_{	ext{ring}}$ (four-spin coupling), $J$ (nearest-neighbor exchange), and the ratio $J_{	ext{ring}}/J$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The calculation relies on DFT energies from collinear GGA+U calculations, with results validated by comparing multiple independent extraction routes.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The method assumes that the system can be accurately described by an effective Hamiltonian involving pair and ring exchange terms derived from a $t/U$ expansion; it also notes limitations regarding biquadratic terms.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the materials ($	ext{La}_2	ext{CuO}_4$, $	ext{SrFeO}_2$) and show that spin models can reproduce DFT energies with high accuracy for $	ext{La}_2	ext{CuO}_4$.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the need for $J_{	ext{ring}}$, details the sixteen-state energy mapping technique, validates it on $	ext{La}_2	ext{CuO}_4$ by comparing multiple routes and correcting previous estimates, and uses $	ext{SrFeO}_2$ as a negative control.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Four-spin ring (cyclic) exchange $J_{ring}$ is an essential ingredient of the Heisenberg spin Hamiltonian of cuprates and other square-lattice magnets, yet it has lacked the kind of direct, local first-principles extraction that the four-state method provides for bilinear exchange, $J$. We supply it by generalizing that method to a sixteen-state ($2^4$) scheme. Symmetry reduces the sixteen configurations to six or eight inequivalent energies, so the cost is modest. The derivation also shows that the conventional four-state magnetic coupling, $J$, is itself ring-renormalized, by $\pm 2 J_{ring} S^2$ with the sign set by the reference state. T-La$_2$CuO$_4$ confirms this quantitatively: three independent routes agree on $J_{ring}$ to $0.2\%$, giving $J_{ring}/J_1 = 0.25$, and a four-state $J_1$ quoted without naming its reference is wrong by $12\%$ in this material. The direct sixteen-state extraction itself proves reference-dependent, the Néel and ferromagnetic baths bracketing the mapping value: a fourth-order fingerprint of interactions beyond the pair-plus-ring model, which additional reference baths resolve into a bare $J_{ring}$ and a converging tower of six- and eight-spin loop couplings. SrFeO$_2$ ($S = 2$), with the same plaquette yet $J_{ring}/J = 0.006$, provides the negative control: a plaquette is necessary for ring exchange, far from sufficient. The complete workflow, including the band-gap and local-moment diagnostics that certify any such extraction, is implemented in the openly available Mag4 package, so that $J_{ring}$ costs no more effort to obtain than $J$.

</details>

**Type:** theory · **Category:** strongly correlated electrons · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2607.18477v1>

**Summary.** This work develops a sophisticated theoretical framework using elasticity-gauge duality to model two-dimensional Wigner crystals containing various lattice defects. It systematically calculates defect interaction energies and analyzes how the presence of vacancies modifies collective excitations like phonons. The results offer measurable predictions for transport properties, linking measurements of sound speed directly to vacancy density.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on solid-state correlations, the development of 'fracton-elasticity duality' provides powerful mathematical tools for analyzing systems where long-range interactions couple to topological defects, which has parallels in quantum field theory applied to condensed matter.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To systematically study how various lattice defects (vacancies, dislocations, disclinations) affect the effective action, interaction energies, and melting transitions of two-dimensional Wigner crystals.

**Main result.** The theory provides a comprehensive framework using duality techniques to calculate defect interactions and predicts unusual phonon dispersion relations in the metallic vacancy liquid phase. This framework is applicable to other charged systems like CDWs.

**Method.** Duality techniques, specifically elasticity-gauge duality, are employed to construct an effective action incorporating elastic and electrostatic contributions from various lattice defects.

**Model / system.** The system is a two-dimensional Wigner solid (electron gas) confined between metallic gates. The model incorporates the interaction of electrons with vacancies ($ho_{vac}$) and treats defects as sources for electromagnetic and elastic gauge fields.

**Key observables.** Phonon dispersion relation, speed of sound ($c_{ph}$), interaction energies between defect pairs, and melting temperature dependence on vacancy density.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Vacancy density ($ho_v$), gate separation ($d$), lattice constant ($a$), and the effective mass/density of the vacancy fluid ($m_h$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis uses linear response theory, assumes parabolic dispersion for the Fermi liquid component, and often simplifies by neglecting spin degrees of freedom or assuming infinite bulk elastic moduli for certain limits.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the fundamental types of lattice defects: vacancies, dislocations (with Burgers vector $\mathbf{b}$), and disclinations (with Frank vector $\mathbf{F}$).

**Paper structure.** The paper builds from defining the general quadratic action incorporating elasticity and electrostatics. It then uses duality transformations to derive a dual action describing defect physics. Subsequent sections analyze specific observables like interaction energies, collective modes (phonons/plasmons), and the dynamics of the vacancy Fermi liquid.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

The physics of Wigner solids is characterized by an interplay of elasticity and long-range electrostatics, endowing crystal defects with properties distinct from those in charge-neutral crystals. Recent experiments observing lattice melting and Wigner-crystal-adjacent phases in two dimensions necessitate an examination of how defects affect the long-wavelength properties of such solids. Here, we use duality techniques to construct a comprehensive framework for studying the contribution of vacancies and interstitials, dislocations, and disclinations to the effective action of two-dimensional charged crystals. This allows for a systematic investigation of the interaction energies for the different combinations of defect pairs. We further study melting transitions due to defect proliferation, assessing and justifying some of the assumptions present in the literature. In the metallic Wigner crystal phase, characterized by a finite ground state density of vacancies, we find phonons with a dispersion relation that varies in an unusual way with the vacancy density. Consequently, we discuss how thermodynamic properties of such a vacancy Fermi liquid can be probed in measurements of the melting temperature and speed of sound. The field theory developed here can serve as a starting point in the study of anomalous Hall crystals and charge density waves with defects.

</details>

**Summary.** This work theoretically distinguishes the magnetotransport properties of 2D Dirac cones from those of gapped nodal-rings (GNRs) in the presence of impurities and strong magnetic fields. By applying the Kubo-Bastin formalism, the authors predict that GNRs will exhibit pronounced oscillatory behavior in both longitudinal and Hall conductivities, a signature absent in the smoothly varying response of 2D Dirac cones.

**Why it may be interesting.** The clear differentiation of transport fingerprints between two distinct topological/band structures (Dirac vs. GNR) under disorder provides a powerful theoretical tool to interpret complex magnetotransport experiments in condensed matter systems.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates the dc magnetoconductivity of two-dimensional Dirac cones and gapped nodal-rings (GNRs) when subjected to perpendicular magnetic fields and impurity potentials in the quantum limit.

**Main result.** The GNR exhibits distinct oscillatory transport signatures, including resonant peaks in $\sigma_{xx}$ and a sawtooth pattern in $\sigma_{xy}$, contrasting sharply with the smooth, monotonic magnetoresistivity found for 2D Dirac cones.

**Method.** The Kubo-Bastin formalism is employed to calculate transport coefficients by analyzing the Landau level spectrum derived from the respective Hamiltonians.

**Model / system.** The models include isotropic 2D Dirac cones and Gapped Nodal Rings (GNRs). The analysis considers pointlike, Gaussian, and Yukawa impurity potentials in the ultraquantum limit.

**Key observables.** Longitudinal conductivity ($\sigma_{xx}$) and Hall conductivity ($\sigma_{xy}$)

**Important parameters / regimes.** Magnetic field ($B$), dimensionless ratio $ho = 2 \epsilon_r / \epsilon_c$, and impurity potential type (pointlike, Gaussian, Yukawa).

**Assumptions / limitations.** Calculations are performed strictly within the ultraquantum limit, assuming a single-band, non-interacting picture.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the LL spectrum of GNR vs. $ho$, showing its characteristic non-monotonic 'stretched-checkmark' pattern, which dictates the transport differences from Dirac cones.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the theoretical framework using Kubo-Bastin for both systems. It then derives and contrasts the resulting magnetoconductivity signatures: smooth/zero $\sigma_{xy}$ for Dirac vs. oscillatory/sawtooth $\sigma_{xy}$ for GNRs, concluding with explicit formulas for $ho_{lx}$ based on impurity potential type.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We investigate the dc magnetoconductivity of two-dimensional Dirac cones and gapped nodal rings (GNRs) subjected to a perpendicular magnetic field, which quantises the electronic spectrum into Landau levels (LLs). Working in the ultraquantum limit, where only the lowest LL (LLL) is partially occupied, we employ the Kubo--Bastin formalism to compute the transport coefficients for pointlike, Gaussian, and Yukawa impurity-potentials. For the Dirac case, the longitudinal conductivity is field-independent for pointlike impurities and a monotonic function of $B$ for the Gaussian and Yukawa potentials, while the Hall conductivity vanishes identically owing to the particle-hole symmetry of the two neighbouring LLs. The GNR case is qualitatively different: its non-monotonic stretched-checkmark LL spectrum causes the effective LLL to migrate to successively lower indices as the field increases, producing a pronounced oscillatory structure in both conductivities. The longitudinal response develops resonant peaks at LLL degeneracies, while the Hall conductivity traces out a sawtooth pattern with sharp zero-crossings at these same points. These results establish distinct transport fingerprints for the two systems in the extreme quantum limit, and provide a theoretical framework for interpreting magnetotransport experiments on GNRs.

</details>

### [Metric completion of the Bender--Brody--Müller Hamiltonian: dilation spectrum and missing eigenstates](http://arxiv.org/abs/2607.19067v1)

**Authors:** Kejun Liu  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** other · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2607.19067v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks

(a) Local window k ∈[π, 3π] around the soft point k = 2π: the boundary symbol η0(k) = 2(1 − cos k) vanishes quadratically at k = 2πZ, while the normal- ized Cauchy-kernel probes |ϕε|2 concentrate at the soft point as ε decreases. (b) The squared η0-norm of the unit-L2-norm probe, ⟨ϕε, η0ϕε⟩= 2(1−e−ε) (exact residue evaluation), van- ishes linearly in the small-width limit, 2(1−e−ε) = 2ε+O(ε2) as ε →0: normalized states can carry arbitrarily small metric length, i.e. η0 is not coercive.</figcaption>
</figure>
</div>

**Summary.** The paper rigorously analyzes the Hilbert space completion induced by the metric associated with the BBM Hamiltonian, a model linked to the Riemann zeta function zeros. By completing the state space in an $L^2$-based norm, the authors prove that the original candidate eigenfunctions cannot exist within this mathematically defined space. This establishes a significant mathematical obstruction to the initial physical interpretation of the BBM operator.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a deep mathematical constraint on speculative quantum models derived from number theory. For theorists working on spectral problems or non-Hermitian Hamiltonians, it demonstrates how imposing standard functional analysis structures can rule out proposed physical solutions.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper analyzes the Hilbert completion induced by a candidate metric associated with the Bender--Brody--Müller (BBM) Hamiltonian, which is related to finding an operator whose spectrum yields the imaginary parts of the nontrivial zeros of the Riemann zeta function.

**Main result.** The rigorous $L^2$-based metric completion forces the state space to be equivalent to $L^2(\mathbb{R}^+)$, and this framework proves that the original BBM candidate eigenfunctions do not belong to the completed space, invalidating the initial mechanism for finding point-spectrum states.

**Method.** The analysis involves defining a pseudo-Hermitian metric $\hat{\eta}$ on the half-line $L^2(\mathbb{R}^+)$ and studying its completion. Techniques include Fourier/Mellin transforms to analyze the metric symbol, spectral theory (dilation generator), and deficiency index calculations.

**Model / system.** The model centers on the BBM Hamiltonian, formally written as $	ext{H}_{	ext{BBM}} = \Delta^{-1}D\Delta$, where $D$ is the dilation generator. The mathematical framework uses a metric completion based on $\hat{\eta} = \sin^2(\hat{p}/2) = \Delta^\dagger\Delta/4$.

**Key observables.** The spectrum of the free realization (purely continuous, $\mathbb{R}$), and the failure of the candidate eigenfunctions ($\psi_z$) to satisfy the required $L^2$ shift-difference condition.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The critical line $\operatorname{Re} z = 1/2$, and the metric degeneracy observed when analyzing the symbol $\eta_0(k) = |1 - e^{-ik}|^2$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary assumption tested is that a standard $L^2$-based Hilbert completion using the candidate metric can support the required spectral structure.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the metric degeneracy, showing $\eta_0(k)$ vanishing at specific points and the associated norm of probe functions vanishing linearly in the small-width limit.

**Paper structure.** The paper proceeds by defining the BBM Hamiltonian and its associated metric. It then analyzes the properties of this metric completion, establishing that the resulting space is $L^2(\mathbb{R}^+)$. Finally, it tests the original physical conjecture (the existence of $\psi_z$) within this mathematically rigorous framework.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

The Bender--Brody--Müller (BBM) Hamiltonian was proposed as a non-Hermitian Hilbert--Pólya operator. We analyze the Hilbert completion induced, on the standard half-line $L^2$ core, by BBM's candidate metric $\hatη=\sin^2(\hat p/2)=Δ^\daggerΔ/4$. The form $η_0=Δ^\daggerΔ$ is positive with trivial kernel but is not coercive. Completing $C_c^\infty(0,\infty)$ in the norm $\|ψ\|_{η_0}=\|Δψ\|$ gives a Hilbert space canonically unitarily equivalent to $L^2(\mathbb R_+)$. Its free self-adjoint realization is the dilation generator, with simple, purely absolutely continuous spectrum $\mathbb R$. The analysis yields two spectral statements of interest beyond the BBM problem. First, no bounded sandwich $Δ^\dagger h(D)Δ$ is boundedly invertible. Second, the transported symmetric operator has deficiency indices $(\infty,\infty)$ and an adjoint with every real point as an eigenvalue of infinite multiplicity, while its free extension is purely continuous. The realization-independent BBM conclusion concerns the candidate eigenfunctions: $Δψ_z=x^{-z}$, so for $\operatorname{Re}z=1/2$ they do not belong to the completed space. Thus the original BBM boundary-condition/eigenfunction mechanism cannot produce point-spectrum Riemann-zero states in this $L^2$-based metric completion.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces QEMOO, a novel multi-round quantum framework designed to tackle challenging multi-objective combinatorial optimization problems mapped onto Ising spin glasses. By iteratively refining the search using warm-started QAOA and adaptive direction updates, it achieves superior coverage of the true Pareto front compared to single-pass methods. This demonstrates a practical path toward efficient quantum computation for complex trade-off analysis.

**Why it may be interesting.** The use of QAOA on spin-glass Hamiltonians places this work directly at the intersection of quantum algorithms for optimization and disordered systems physics. The focus on iterative refinement (warm-start) is relevant to understanding how quantum search can overcome local minima in complex energy landscapes.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The challenge of multi-objective combinatorial optimization (MOCO), which requires finding the Pareto-optimal trade-off front among conflicting objectives.

**Main result.** The proposed QEMOO framework significantly improves the hypervolume approximation of the Pareto front compared to single-pass quantum methods, especially in resource-constrained regimes.

**Method.** QEMOO is a multi-round protocol combining Pareto-based selection with warm-started QAOA sampling and an adaptive direction update scheme inspired by PBI.

**Model / system.** The problem is mapped onto Ising spin-glass Hamiltonians, requiring the identification of trade-off solutions across multiple conflicting objectives.

**Key observables.** Hypervolume (HV) of the approximated Pareto front; Number of unique non-dominated solutions (ND).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Total shot budget ($s_{total}$); Warm-start coefficient ($c$); Bond dimension ($\chi$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The PBI method requires linearization to maintain compatibility with the two-body Hamiltonian structure of near-term quantum processors.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate QEMOO's multi-round process, performance heatmaps comparing different weight pool geometries, and scaling plots showing HV gain vs. shot budget or bond dimension.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the MOCO problem mapped to Ising models, details the limitations of single-pass quantum approaches, proposes the QEMOO framework (multi-round protocol with adaptive updates), benchmarks its performance across various weight pool geometries and resource budgets, and analyzes the role of warm-starting.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Multi-objective combinatorial optimization requires identifying Pareto-optimal trade-off solutions among conflicting objectives, often making it more demanding than its single-objective counterpart. Although quantum multi-objective optimization methods have begun to emerge, most existing quantum optimization workflows are still built around single-objective or fixed-scalarization settings. Building on existing weighted-sum QAOA approaches to quantum multi-objective optimization, we propose QEMOO, a quantum-enhanced multi-objective optimization framework that combines Pareto-based selection and warm-started QAOA sampling in a multi-round protocol under the same total shot budget. We further introduce a PBI-inspired adaptive direction-update scheme to improve coverage in strongly conflicting benchmark regimes. Across three benchmark stages, QEMOO improves Pareto-front hypervolume over the single-pass weighted-sum QAOA baseline under matched shot budgets, suggesting a practical route toward shot-efficient quantum-assisted multi-objective optimization and its future applications.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper rigorously analyzes the existence of hidden conserved quantities in hard rods confined by a harmonic trap. By imposing symmetry constraints derived from free motion and collisions, the authors demonstrate that for finite rods, only two independent conserved quantities exist. A notable exception is the point particle limit, where more integrals are found.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a rigorous, systematic methodology for determining the full set of classical integrals of motion in complex interacting systems, which is foundational knowledge applicable to understanding integrability breaking in quantum many-body physics.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates whether a system of hard rods confined in a harmonic trap possesses hidden analytic conserved quantities beyond the known total energy and center-of-mass energy.

**Main result.** For general hard rods, conservation laws impose symmetry constraints ($	ext{U}(1)$ and $	ext{S}_N$) that force any conserved quantity to be functionally dependent only on the two known integrals of motion. However, for point particles (zero length), a larger set of such quantities is found.

**Method.** The authors use systematic algebraic methods by requiring that potential conserved quantities must remain invariant under symmetries dictated by free motion ($	ext{U}(1)$) and collisions ($	ext{S}_N$).

**Model / system.** The system consists of $N$ hard rods of equal length confined in a one-dimensional harmonic trap. The dynamics are analyzed for free motion, elastic collisions, and the special case where all rods are point particles.

**Key observables.** Conserved quantities (integrals of motion), ergodicity, and thermalization behavior.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Harmonic trap frequency ($\omega$), rod lengths ($a_i$), number of rods ($N$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis is strictly limited to finding *analytic* conserved quantities. The comparison between finite-length rods and point particles highlights a crucial distinction in the symmetry requirements.

**Figures summary.** Not specified by the notes, but the text references Table 1 summarizing symmetry constraints for different dynamics (Free motion, Point particles, SMED & Rods with non-zero length).

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically analyzes four distinct dynamical regimes: free motion; collisions with finite rods; zero-length point particles; and Stochastic Momentum Exchange Dynamics (SMED), applying symmetry constraints in each case to determine the structure of conserved quantities.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Systems of hard rods of equal length in a one-dimensional harmonic trap have been observed to exhibit peculiar non-ergodic behavior that might suggest the existence of a novel hidden conserved quantity beyond the two well known ones, i.e., the total energy and the center-of-mass energy. In this work, we investigate this possibility by systematically constraining the forms of the conserved quantities, and we rigorously rule out the existence of any extra hidden conserved quantity that is analytic in the positions and momenta of the rods involved. We do so by showing two key results: conservation during free motion demands the $U(1)$ invariance of these quantities under rotations of the position and momenta of each rod, and conservation during collisions demand an $S_N$ invariance under the permutation of the momenta of the rods as long as one of the rods have non-zero length. We then show that these conditions imply that any conserved quantity is functionally dependent on the two known conserved quantities. In addition, we show that in the special case where all rods have zero length (i.e., when they are point particles), conservation under collisions only requires invariance under a smaller $S_N$ group of permutations of the labels of the rods, which leads to a much larger set of analytic conserved quantities that we explicitly write down. In all, this rigorously clarifies the structure of conserved quantities in the hard rod problem, and motivates the application of such systematic methods to other classical systems.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper develops and compares three sophisticated analytical methods—expectation values, variational optimization, and the Hellmann-Feynman theorem—to determine the bound state energies of hydrogenic atoms in a screened Coulomb potential. By achieving high accuracy relative to numerical benchmarks, it provides valuable theoretical tools for understanding atomic structure within plasma diagnostics.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides advanced analytical tools for calculating atomic energy levels in realistic plasma environments where screening effects are crucial. The combination of multiple theoretical approaches offers robust benchmarking for complex quantum systems.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates the bound state properties of hydrogen-like atoms when interacting with the radial screened Coulomb potential (RSCP). The goal is to derive approximate energy eigenvalues as a function of the screening parameter.

**Main result.** The expectation value approach using the Kratzer basis achieved relative errors of 0.63% for the first ten s-states at $c=0.1$, with further improvements possible via variational methods. The formalism is general and applicable to Positronium.

**Method.** Three complementary analytical approaches are employed: expectation values using reference states (Coulomb/Kratzer), variational optimization with a scaled Kratzer basis, and the Hellmann-Feynman theorem for error estimation.

**Model / system.** The system is hydrogen-like atoms modeled by the radial Schrödinger equation subjected to the RSCP potential, $V_{RSCP}(r) = -Ze^2/r e^{-c/r}$. This potential models short-range screening effects in plasma environments.

**Key observables.** Approximate energy eigenvalues ($E_{n,l}$) as a function of the screening parameter $c$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Screening parameter $c$, quantum numbers $(n, \ell)$, and variational parameters $eta$. The limit $c 	o 0$ recovers the standard Coulomb result.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The methods rely on using approximate wavefunctions derived from reference potentials (Coulomb or Kratzer) to evaluate expectation values of the full RSCP Hamiltonian. The Hellmann-Feynman approach requires numerical integration for certain parameters.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically details three methods—expectation value, variational, and Hellmann-Feynman—for calculating energy eigenvalues, compares their results against high-precision data, demonstrates the general applicability to Positronium, and concludes with error analysis.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We investigate the bound state properties of the hydrogen-like atoms in the radial screened Coulomb potential (RSCP). using three complementary analytical approaches - expectation values with Coulomb and Kratzer reference states, variational optimization with a scaled Kratzer basis, and the Hellmann-Feynman theorem - we derive approximate energy eigenvalues as function of the screening parameter c. Benchmarked against high-precision generalized pseudospectral data, the expectation value-approach with the Kratzer basis achieves relative errors of 0.63% for the first ten s-states at $c=0.1$, while the variational method improves this further. The formalism extends naturally to Positronium, demonstrating its generality for arbitrary reduced-mass systems. The complementary biases of the methods provide robust error estimation for plasma-embedded atoms.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces MOSAIQC, a novel framework designed to overcome limitations in running large quantum algorithms on current noisy hardware by intelligently cutting and remapping circuits. It achieves this through a hybrid optimization process that jointly considers gate/wire cuts, hardware noise profiles, and sampling overhead. The results show substantial improvements in both speed and solution quality compared to existing methods.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on quantum computation, the rigorous development of scalable heuristic optimization techniques for resource allocation (like circuit partitioning) shares methodological parallels with optimizing complex physical simulations or network mapping in AMO physics.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary challenge is developing a scalable method for quantum circuit cutting to execute large algorithms on current, resource-limited Noisy Intermediate-Scale Quantum (NISQ) hardware.

**Main result.** MOSAIQC demonstrates superior trade-offs in run speed and solution quality by jointly optimizing sampling overhead, hardware placement, and mixed gate/wire cuts, achieving significant reductions in computational overhead.

**Method.** The framework uses a hybrid approach combining global heuristics (like METIS) for initial partitioning with local refinement optimization (Tabu Search), incorporating noise-informed cost functions.

**Model / system.** The system involves mapping large quantum circuits onto smaller, potentially heterogeneous quantum processors. The circuit connectivity and hardware topology are modeled as weighted graphs to calculate cutting costs.

**Key observables.** Local fidelity improvement (+19.56% $\pm$ 6.17%), runtime speedup ($2.88 	imes$), and massive reduction in sampling overhead ($	ext{average } 5.83 \cdot 10^{11}	imes$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Qubit count (scaling from 6 to 1000+), cut types (gate vs. wire), hardware connectivity, and noise characteristics.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The framework assumes the ability to convert quantum circuit objects into lightweight graph structures for efficient computation; existing heuristics often fail to jointly optimize all necessary parameters.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate performance comparisons across various benchmark circuits (e.g., QAOA, Ising VQE) at increasing qubit scales, showing runtime and sampling overhead results for different cut placement strategies.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the problem of circuit cutting limitations, details the novel MOSAIQC framework using graph representations and a hybrid optimization strategy, presents specific algorithms for wire/gate cut cost calculation, and concludes with extensive comparative results across multiple scales and benchmarks.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Current quantum computers do not yet have the required qubit resources to meet the demands of most practical quantum algorithms. To circumvent this constraint, the practice of dividing these algorithms into parts through quantum circuit cutting has been explored. Many of these works either show exponential scaling or are far from optimal solutions. In this paper, MosaiQC is presented as a novel framework to improve upon existing circuit cutting frameworks. A hybrid warmstart with refinement optimization is used to find cutting solutions, allowing the combination of both wire and gate cuts. Additionally, MosaiQC enables hardware partitions of mixed sizes. Furthermore, the refinement stage incorporates a fast approximate quadratic assignment solver to better place hardware partitions, demonstrating a mean local fidelity improvement of $19.56 \% \pm 6.17\%$ over the baseline algorithm. In runtime and sampling overhead costs, improvements of $2.88 \times$ and an average of $16.84\%$ cut reduction (resulting in an average $5.83 \cdot 10^{11} \times$ overhead reduction) are observed. MosaiQC demonstrates a superior trade-off for run speed and solution quality, while adding fundamental features excluded by most competitors. With this, MosaiQC demonstrates that scalable heuristic optimization can substantially reduce the computational overhead of circuit-cut placement for increasingly large quantum circuits.

</details>

**Authors:** Cameron Khanpour, Samuel Talkington  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** quantum information and computing · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2607.19263v1>

**Summary.** This work proves that inherent topological features of real power grids cause the necessary matrices for solving power flow problems to become extremely ill-conditioned. This structural limitation forces both classical and quantum computation time requirements to scale poorly with grid size, effectively precluding a general end-to-end quantum advantage for these critical engineering tasks.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on electrical engineering, the core mathematical tools—analyzing computational bottlenecks via matrix conditioning and structural graph properties—are highly relevant to understanding resource limitations in simulating complex quantum many-body systems or analyzing quantum network architectures.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates whether quantum computers can achieve an end-to-end computational advantage over classical methods when solving large-scale power flow problems.

**Main result.** Structural properties of realistic electrical grids, such as graph separators and long corridors, cause the underlying mathematical matrices to become severely ill-conditioned, precluding a general quantum advantage for these tasks.

**Method.** The analysis combines rigorous graph theory (analyzing network topology) with quantum complexity theory (query and readout bounds) to establish lower bounds on computational difficulty.

**Model / system.** The model is an electrical transmission network represented by graphs $G=(V, E)$, whose core mathematical structure involves the susceptance Laplacian matrix. The problems analyzed include DC/AC Power Flow, Optimal Power Flow, and Unit Commitment.

**Key observables.** Pseudo condition number ($\kappa_+(B)$), which dictates the required computational resources for solving linear systems derived from network physics.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Network size ($n$), graph separators, corridor lengths ($\ell$), and quantum complexity bounds related to $\epsilon$ (precision).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes that realistic grid topologies exhibit structural limitations like separators or corridors, and it proves the lack of advantage at *every* readout level.

**Paper structure.** The paper proceeds by analyzing the condition number growth based on network topology (separators vs. corridors), demonstrating that this growth forces quantum query complexity to exceed classical scaling, and then generalizing these obstructions across various power flow formulations.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

This letter proves realistic grid properties limit the applicability of quantum computers for power flow. Grids that split into two large regions meeting at only a few buses, common in transmission networks, force the pseudo condition number of the DC susceptance matrix to grow polynomially in the network size, and long chains of lines bridging such regions force quadratic growth, making recent empirical observations rigorous. The bounds also hold with overwhelming probability for arbitrary bounded random line susceptances. Combined with query and tomography lower bounds, this precludes end-to-end quantum advantage for DC power flow at every readout level, and these obstructions persist through AC power flow, optimal power flow, and unit commitment. All proofs are formally verified with accompanying Lean 4 source code.

</details>

### [Perfect state transfer in Grover walks on normal Cayley graphs](http://arxiv.org/abs/2607.19309v1)

**Authors:** Koushik Bhakta, Bikash Bhattacharjya  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** quantum information and computing · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2607.19309v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks

**Summary.** This paper rigorously determines the necessary and sufficient spectral conditions for Perfect State Transfer (PST) when using Grover walks on normal Cayley graphs. By applying group theory and spectral analysis, it derives explicit criteria for various families of graphs, culminating in a complete classification showing that only four unitary Cayley graphs exhibit this phenomenon.

**Why it may be interesting.** Quantum walks are fundamental models in quantum computation and quantum communication networks. Establishing precise mathematical conditions for PST provides concrete graph-theoretic tools applicable to designing robust quantum information channels.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates the conditions for Perfect State Transfer (PST) occurring during Grover walks on a specific class of graphs called normal Cayley graphs.

**Main result.** A necessary and sufficient condition for PST is established, leading to complete characterizations for unitary Cayley graphs, proving that exactly four such graphs exhibit this property.

**Method.** The analysis heavily relies on spectral graph theory, utilizing the eigenvalues of a derived 'discriminant matrix' ($P$) and relating PST conditions to Chebyshev polynomials evaluated at these eigenvalues.

**Model / system.** The system models quantum walks governed by the Grover walk operator $U$ on a normal Cayley graph $	ext{Cay}(\Gamma, S)$ over a finite group $\Gamma$. The state space is defined over the symmetric arcs of this graph.

**Key observables.** Perfect State Transfer (PST) between vertices at time $	au$, and the periodicity of the quantum walk dynamics.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The structure of the underlying finite group $\Gamma$ (e.g., abelian, dicyclic, dihedral), the connection set $S$, and the time step $	au$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis is restricted to normal Cayley graphs, and the focus is on transfer between specific 'vertex-type states.'

**Figures summary.** Not specified in detail, but the notes mention Figure 1 illustrating examples of periodic unitary Cayley graphs ($G_{Z_{12}}$ and $G_{Z_{18}}$).

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by defining PST on normal Cayley graphs, deriving general spectral conditions using representation theory (Theorem 3.4), applying these criteria to specific group structures like abelian, dicyclic, and dihedral groups, and concluding with a complete characterization for unitary Cayley graphs.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

A Cayley graph $\operatorname{Cay}(Γ,S)$ over a finite group $Γ$ is said to be normal if its connection set $S$ is a union of some conjugacy classes of $Γ$. This paper investigates perfect state transfer in Grover walks on normal Cayley graphs. The Grover walk is a widely studied discrete-time quantum walk. We establish a necessary and sufficient condition for the occurrence of perfect state transfer on normal Cayley graphs. As applications, we derive explicit spectral criteria for perfect state transfer on Cayley graphs over abelian groups, dicyclic groups, and dihedral groups. These results yield several infinite families of Cayley graphs exhibiting perfect state transfer. We further obtain simple combinatorial characterizations of the existence of perfect state transfer on Cayley graphs over dihedral and dicyclic groups. Our general characterization also recovers a number of previously known results as special cases. As a further consequence, we obtain a complete characterization of perfect state transfer on unitary Cayley graphs. In particular, we prove that exactly four graphs in the class of unitary Cayley graphs exhibit perfect state transfer.

</details>

(b) d = 3 lattice corresponding to the cubic lattice with periodic boundary conditions, where each side has length 8 and N = 512. The solid line represents the exact value obtained by classical simulation, while the open circles indicate results from a quantum circuit simulation, with 1000 measurements per data point.</figcaption>
</figure>
</div>

**Summary.** This paper develops an explicit quantum circuit implementation for performing spatial search using discrete-time quantum walks on $d$-dimensional lattices. By decomposing the complex shift operator into basic gates, they achieve resource estimates showing logarithmic width growth and polynomial depth scaling ($O(\sqrt{N})$ to $O(\sqrt{N}(\log N)^{3/2})$). The results offer a practical roadmap for realizing such search algorithms on quantum hardware.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a concrete, implementable quantum algorithm framework for search problems on structured lattices, directly connecting abstract quantum walk theory to near-term hardware constraints like gate depth and error rates.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper develops an explicit quantum circuit for performing spatial search on $d$-dimensional lattices using discrete-time quantum walks.

**Main result.** They provide a practical framework, including circuits that reproduce theoretical dynamics for periodic and non-periodic boundaries, with depth scaling analyzed as $O(\sqrt{N}(\log N)^{3/2})$ for 2D and $O(\sqrt{N})$ for 3D.

**Method.** The core method involves decomposing the quantum walk operator ($\hat{W} = \hat{S}\hat{C}$) into implementable quantum gates, specifically using coin-controlled modular arithmetic operations to simulate the shift operator $\hat{S}$.

**Model / system.** The model is a discrete-time quantum walk on $d$-dimensional lattices (2D square and 3D cubic), where the state space includes both position $|xangle$ and internal direction/coin state $|langle$. The search process incorporates an oracle ($\hat{R}$) marking the target site.

**Key observables.** Success probability $P(x_0)$, circuit width ($O(\log N)$), and circuit depth (scaling as $O(\sqrt{N})$ or $O(\sqrt{N}(\log N)^{3/2})$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The dimensionality $d$ (2D, 3D) and the lattice size $N$. Noise robustness is analyzed under a CX-gate depolarizing noise model.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes a CX-gate depolarizing noise model for error estimation. The circuit construction relies on specific encoding schemes to implement direction reversal using minimal gates (e.g., a single X gate).

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the quantum circuits for 2D and 3D lattices, show time evolution of success probability $P(x_0)$ under different boundary conditions, and plot resource scaling (width/depth) versus lattice size $N$.

**Paper structure.** The paper first defines the theoretical framework using quantum walks on $d$-dimensional lattices. It then details the circuit construction for the shift operator $\hat{S}$ and extends it to non-periodic boundaries. Finally, it performs resource analysis (width/depth) and noise robustness testing.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We propose an explicit quantum circuit for quantum spatial search based on discrete-time quantum walks on $d$-dimensional lattices. In this algorithm, the flip-flop shift operator moves the walker to a neighboring site along the selected spatial direction and reverses the corresponding direction label after the move. By encoding each pair of opposite directions so that they differ only in the least significant qubit of the coin register, we implement the shift using coin-controlled modular increment and decrement operations on the position registers, together with a single $X$ gate that reverses the direction label. We verify that the proposed circuits reproduce the theoretical dynamics on two- and three-dimensional periodic lattices. We further extend the circuit construction to systems with position-dependent shift rules, such as non-periodic boundaries, and validate this extension on a two-dimensional lattice. The resource analysis shows logarithmic growth of the circuit width. For the two-dimensional lattice, the synthesized depth is consistent with $O(\sqrt{N}(\log N)^{3/2})$, while the three-dimensional depth empirically follows an $O(\sqrt{N})$ dependence over the investigated range. Under a CX-gate depolarizing noise model, CX-optimized circuits exhibit improved noise robustness. These results provide a practical framework for implementing quantum spatial search on regular lattices and extending it to defective and other irregular lattice structures.

</details>

### [Single Link Removal Perturbation in Szegedy Quantum Walk: from Graph Completeness Testing to Integrity Monitoring](http://arxiv.org/abs/2607.19129v1)

**Authors:** Sara Giordano, Miguel A. Martin-Delgado  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** quantum information and computing · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2607.19129v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks

**Summary.** This paper provides a deep theoretical analysis of detecting single link failures in dense quantum networks modeled by the Szegedy quantum walk. By applying perturbation theory, the authors derive precise scaling laws showing how the loss of one edge causes measurable shifts in spectral quantities like eigenvalues and phases. These results establish fundamental limits for quantum-assisted topology integrity monitoring.

**Why it may be interesting.** The rigorous quantification of detection limits based on spectral perturbations is highly relevant for understanding noise resilience in quantum communication protocols and network security.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper develops a rigorous perturbative analysis to detect structural anomalies, specifically the removal of a single link, in dense quantum communication networks.

**Main result.** It quantifies that this minimal perturbation causes predictable, small shifts ($\Theta(1/n^2)$) in spectral quantities like the gap eigenvalue and eigenphase shift, allowing for detection with high theoretical precision.

**Method.** The analysis employs time-dependent perturbation theory applied to the Szegedy quantum walk operator, analyzing how low-rank modifications affect the spectrum of the system's discriminant matrix.

**Model / system.** The model is a quantum walk executed on a complete graph ($K_n$) representing a dense communication network. The process uses the Szegedy quantum walk algorithm framework to test for link integrity.

**Key observables.** Spectral norm of the perturbation ($\Theta(1/n)$), shift in the gap eigenvalue ($\Theta(1/n^2)$), eigenphase shift ($\Delta	heta^\star = \Theta(1/n^2)$), and change in success probability ($O(1/\sqrt{n})$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The analysis is most relevant in the regime where the number of marked nodes $m$ scales linearly with the total number of nodes, i.e., $m = \Theta(n)$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary assumption is that the anomaly is the removal of a single unmarked-unmarked edge, and the analysis relies on first-order perturbation theory for eigenvalue shifts.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds from graph completeness testing to integrity monitoring. It analyzes the spectral consequences of removing an edge $\{u, v\} \subseteq V \setminus M$ by treating it as a small perturbation ($\Delta$) on the transition matrix $P$, leading to detailed calculations of eigenvalue shifts and success probability bounds.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We present a rigorous perturbative analysis of the Szegedy quantum walk search algorithm on the complete graph with marked nodes, when a specific anomaly is present in the graph. This is motivated by the problem of monitoring the integrity of dense trusted communication networks with a quantum-assisted procedure. The topology of these networks is modeled as a complete graph, and the anomaly of interest is the disappearance of a single communication link which represents the minimal and spectrally hardest structural defect to detect. Building on the graph-completeness testing algorithm framework, we quantify how the removal of a single unmarked-unmarked edge propagates through the relevant spectral quantities of the Szegedy quantum walk. Denoting by $n$ the total number of nodes of the graph and by $m$ the number of marked nodes, we prove that the perturbation to the transition matrix has spectral norm $Θ(1/n)$, and that the gap eigenvalue undergoes a strictly negative first-order shift for every $n$ and every number of marked nodes $m$, providing a formal proof of a conjecture from our completeness testing algorithm work; in the regime $m = Θ(n)$ relevant for the search algorithm, this shift has magnitude $Θ(1/n^2)$. The corresponding eigenphase shift satisfies $Δθ_\star = Θ(1/n^2)$ in the same regime. We establish that the rotation angle of the effective subspace under this perturbation is $O(1/n)$ for $m = Θ(n)$. Finally, we bound the change in success probability to $O(1/\sqrt{n})$ in this same regime of marked nodes, and show that this bound is dominated by the geometric misalignment of the effective subspace rather than by the spectral shift of the eigenphase. These results provide both the theoretical foundations and the fundamental scaling limits of quantum walk-based topology integrity monitoring under minimal structural perturbations.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper introduces Carrier Dynamics by Pockels Imaging (CDPI), a novel, non-invasive method that uses the electro-optic effect to directly image how photogenerated charge carriers move inside semiconductor devices. By correlating these internal field measurements with external current readings, researchers can map fundamental transport processes like drift and diffusion in real time. This technique offers a powerful new tool for understanding device physics across diverse optoelectronic materials.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on solid-state electronics, the core concept of using an external optical probe (Pockels effect) to map internal field dynamics—a non-invasive measurement technique—is highly relevant for studying open quantum systems or charge transfer in nanoscale devices where direct probing is difficult.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Directly imaging charge carrier dynamics within active semiconductor devices is challenging because existing measurement techniques are often indirect or perturbative.

**Main result.** The proposed technique, CDPI, successfully visualizes internal electron cloud evolution in real space while simultaneously correlating it with measurable conduction and displacement currents. This establishes a unified picture of carrier transport mechanisms like drift and diffusion.

**Method.** The method uses Carrier Dynamics by Pockels Imaging (CDPI), which non-invasively probes the local electric field via the electro-optic effect to map photogenerated electron clouds.

**Model / system.** The study is demonstrated in CdTe radiation detectors and is applicable to various optoelectronic materials like perovskites. The analysis models charge transport by relating the measured electric field variations to fundamental current components (conduction and displacement).

**Key observables.** Electric field distribution $E(y, t)$, electron cloud density $\delta n$, conduction current ($I_{cond}$), and displacement current.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Micrometre-scale spatial resolution and nanosecond temporal resolution; voltage biases ranging from 50 V to 200 V; varying optical irradiation power densities.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The derivation of the total current often assumes negligible diffusion when relating it solely to electric field derivatives. The analysis relies on detailed device simulation parameters for material properties like trap levels and mobilities.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the experimental setup using pump-probe beams, time-space charts showing electric field differences, and profiles comparing internal charge cloud evolution with external measured currents (conduction/displacement).

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces CDPI as a solution to direct imaging limitations. It details the physical principles linking EO measurements to current components, presents experimental demonstrations in CdTe, and discusses applicability across various semiconductor types.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Understanding charge carrier dynamics within electronic devices is crucial, as it governs both the internal evolution of signals and the externally measurable current. Yet, direct imaging of these dynamics remains elusive: existing approaches are often indirect or inherently perturbative. Here, we introduce Carrier Dynamics by Pockels Imaging (CDPI), a sensitive, quantitative, and non-invasive method that enables direct access to internal charge dynamics. By probing the local electric field, CDPI visualizes photogenerated electron clouds in real space while simultaneously correlating their evolution with both conduction and displacement currents, thereby establishing a unified picture of carrier transport. The technique provides direct insight into fundamental mechanisms \textemdash including drift, diffusion, carrier-carrier interactions, trapping and detrapping \textemdash as well as the spatiotemporal evolution of carrier cloud morphology. Demonstrated in CdTe radiation detectors with micrometre-scale spatial resolution and nanosecond temporal resolution, CDPI is broadly applicable to systems exhibiting appreciable electro-optic responses. Beyond conventional semiconductors, the approach extends to organic, perovskite, and two-dimensional optoelectronic materials, offering a new paradigm for directly linking internal carrier dynamics to device-level functionality.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper proposes using the vacuum fluctuations within a multi-gap superconducting resonator to couple distant mesoscopic quantum materials. This coupling mediates an attractive interaction, inducing remote Cooper pairing across millimeter gaps. The resulting correlated state allows for 'Andreev teleportation,' demonstrating emergent nonlocal functionalities in engineered quantum networks.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly bridges cavity QED concepts (vacuum mediation) with strongly correlated electron physics (superconductivity in moiré lattices), providing a mechanism for engineered long-range quantum interactions.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To create an unprecedented many-body quantum system by correlating mesoscopic quantum matter distributed over spatially separated nodes using vacuum fluctuations.

**Main result.** The cavity vacuum mediates remote Cooper pairing and enables Andreev teleportation across macroscopic separations, suggesting a paradigm for nonlocal quantum networks.

**Method.** Theoretical modeling involving effective Hamiltonians derived via Schrieffer-Wolff transformation, analyzed using DMRG, exact diagonalization, and Bogoliubov–de Gennes mode-matching.

**Model / system.** The system uses a multi-gap split-ring resonator (SRR) to couple two spatially separated moiré superlattices. The coupling mechanism is the virtual exchange of photons in the cavity vacuum field.

**Key observables.** Remote Cooper pairing, Andreev teleportation probability, and entanglement entropy scaling linearly with lattice area.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Millimeter-scale separation between gaps; deep-subwavelength hotspot confinement; interaction strength $V_{\lambda} \propto 1/\omega_{\lambda}^2$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis primarily focuses on cavity-mediated interactions, neglecting intra-gap Coulomb repulsion effects on the inter-gap process.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the setup showing mediated pairing and teleportation; other figures detail resonance spectra, binding energy oscillations, and the probability of Andreev teleportation versus local scattering channels.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the physical platform (SRR with multiple hotspots), derives an effective Hamiltonian from vacuum coupling, calculates key superconducting diagnostics (pairing amplitude, binding energy) using DMRG/ED, and finally demonstrates nonlocal functionality via Andreev teleportation probability calculations.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We introduce an architecture where mesoscopic quantum matter distributed over spatially separated nodes can be correlated in equilibrium, creating an unprecedented form of many-body quantum system. Central to the design is a multi-gap split-ring resonator (SRR) where the cavity photon has multiple hot spots -- each with deep-subwavelength volume at a split gap and separated by millimeter-scale distances. The cavity's vacuum fluctuations can then mediate a many-body interaction that bridges the distributed quantum materials embedded in the multiple gaps, coupling them into a single correlated mesoscopic system in equilibrium. As an example, we consider a THz SRR with two split gaps, each proximitized to a metallic moiré superlattice, where virtual exchange of a photon in the cavity vacuum mediates a current-current interaction across the gaps. The inherent attractive interaction channels lead to remote Cooper pairing reminiscent of mesoscopic superconductivity, demonstrated with density matrix renormalization group and exact diagonalization calculations. With the two constituents of a Cooper pair now paired across a millimeter-scale separation, a hole incident at one split gap can be converted into an outgoing electron at the remote gap, a process we term Andreev teleportation. The entanglement entropy between the two mesoscopic superlattices is shown to scale linearly with the area (total number of sites) of the mesoscopic lattice. Our results suggest an intriguing paradigm for equilibrium quantum networks of mesoscopic matter that enable emergent nonlocal functionalities and distributed quantum resources.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper experimentally and computationally investigates vortex motion in superconducting arrays under controlled disorder. The key finding is that the presence of disorder causes a two-step depinning transition, suggesting that the system's dynamics are governed by domain wall movement rather than simple glassy freezing. This provides direct evidence linking interface physics to transport phenomena in frustrated periodic systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** The work provides a concrete physical realization where concepts from disordered systems—like pinning and interface physics—manifest in measurable transport signatures, offering insights applicable to understanding complex energy landscapes in many-body quantum systems.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates how disorder affects crystalline superconducting vortex lattices, specifically determining if this leads to glassy states or domain fragmentation via mobile interfaces.

**Main result.** The authors observe a two-step depinning transition in incommensurate fillings, which is consistent with interface-dominated motion characteristic of a polycrystalline vortex lattice rather than homogeneous glassy dynamics.

**Method.** The research combines experimental measurements (I-V curves and differential resistance) on superconducting island arrays with molecular dynamics simulations using a Langevin equation approach.

**Model / system.** The system is superconducting island arrays hosting an Abrikosov vortex lattice. Disorder is controlled by tuning the magnetic field away from commensurate fillings, creating frustration in the periodic pinning potential.

**Key observables.** Two-step depinning transition in differential resistance (dI/dV), flux flow resistance ($R_{ff}$), and the splitting of single transitions into two distinct steps.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Filling fraction ($f$), which dictates commensurability; applied current ($I$); and lattice stiffness ratios ($B/A$) in simulations.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes that the observed intermediate transport regime is governed by domain wall motion separating ordered crystalline domains.

**Figures summary.** Figures show I-V curves demonstrating single vs. two-step transitions based on filling factor; simulation results compare defect structures for different lattice stiffnesses, and field sweeping history reveals pinning effects near commensurate fillings.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first establishing the experimental observation of a two-step transition in incommensurate regimes (Experiment). It then uses molecular vortex models to simulate this behavior, showing how single transitions split into two as disorder increases (Simulation/Theory). Finally, it explores the role of lattice stiffness and field history dependence to solidify the domain wall interpretation.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Disorder fundamentally reshapes how crystalline systems respond to external forces, yet it remains unclear whether disorder drives interacting lattices toward glassy states or instead fragments them into domains separated by mobile interfaces. Here, we investigate vortex motion in superconducting island arrays, where disorder is introduced in a controlled manner by tuning the magnetic field away from commensurate vortex fillings. By driving vortices with an applied current, we observe a two-step depinning transition at incommensurate fillings. Comparison with molecular vortex model simulations shows that this intermediate regime is consistent with domain wall motion in a polycrystalline vortex lattice. While two-step depinning has been explored theoretically in driven periodic systems, direct experimental evidence linking this behavior to interface-dominated vortex motion has been lacking. Our results demonstrate that disordered, interacting vortex systems with strong periodic pinning can favor interface physics over homogeneous glassy dynamics.

</details>
